Hyperoctahedral group characters and a type-BC analog of graph coloring
Mark Skandera

Abstract
. We state combinatorial formulas for hyperoctahedral group ( 𝔅 n \mathfrak{B}_{n} ) character evaluations of the form χ ⁡ ( C ~ w 𝖡𝖢 ​ ( 1 ) ) \chi(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)) , where C ~ w 𝖡𝖢 ​ ( 1 ) ∈ ℤ ⁡ [ 𝔅 n ] \widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{B}_{n}] is a type- 𝖡𝖢 \mathsf{BC} Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element, with w ∈ 𝔅 n w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n} corresponding to simultaneously smooth type- 𝖡 \mathsf{B} and 𝖢 \mathsf{C} Schubert varieties. We also extend the definition of symmetric group codominance to elements of 𝔅 n \mathfrak{B}_{n} and show that for each element w ∈ 𝔅 n w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n} as above, there exists a 𝖡𝖢 \mathsf{BC} -codominant element v ∈ 𝔅 n v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n} satisfying χ ⁡ ( C ~ w 𝖡𝖢 ​ ( 1 ) ) = χ ⁡ ( C ~ v 𝖡𝖢 ​ ( 1 ) ) \chi(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))=\chi(\widetilde{C}_{v}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)) for all 𝔅 n \mathfrak{B}_{n} -characters χ \chi . Combinatorial structures and maps appearing in these formulas are type- 𝖡𝖢 \mathsf{BC} extensions of planar networks, unit interval orders, indifference graphs, poset tableaux, and colorings. Using the ring of type- 𝖡𝖢 \mathsf{BC} symmetric functions, we introduce natural generating functions Y ⁡ ( C ~ w 𝖡𝖢 ​ ( 1 ) ) Y(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)) for the above evaluations. These provide a new type- 𝖡𝖢 \mathsf{BC} analog of Stanley’s chromatic symmetric functions [ Adv. Math. 111 (1995) pp. 166–194].



1.
Introduction

Let
W
be a Coxeter group,
H=H(W)
its Hecke algebra, and
\mathcal{T}(H)
the
space of Hecke algebra
traces
,
linear functionals
\theta_{q}:H\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
satisfying

\theta_{q}(DD^{\prime})=\theta_{q}(D^{\prime}D)
for all
D,D^{\prime}\in H
.
Included in
\mathcal{T}(H)
are the
H
-characters,
which encode much of the structure of
H
in a condensed form.
Since traces are linear,
one might hope to solve the
following problem for particular bases

\mathcal{D}=\{D_{w}\,|\,w\in W\}
of
H

and
\Theta=\{\theta_{q}^{(i)}\,|\,i=1,\dotsc,p\}

of
\mathcal{T}(H)
.

Problem 1.1
.

Find combinatorial formulas for all evaluations

\{\theta_{q}^{(i)}(D_{w})\,|\,\theta_{q}^{(i)}\in\Theta,D_{w}\in\mathcal{D}\}
.

Unfortunately, trace evaluation is not always easy,
even in type
\mathsf{A}
,
when
W
is the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
with Hecke algebra
H=H_{n}(q)
.
(See, e.g.,
[
17
, §1]
.)
Type-
\mathsf{A}
solutions were given in

[
18
]
,
[
37
]
,
using the induced sign character basis of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
,
and bases consisting of products of simple elements of
the (modified, signless) Kazhdan–Lusztig basis

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\}
of
H_{n}(q)
.
It would be interesting to solve Problem
1.1

for other pairs of type-
\mathsf{A}
bases as well, as these evaluations are related to
facts and conjectures
concerning nonnegativity,
graph coloring, and Hessenberg varieties, e.g.,

[
32
, Lem. 1.1]
,

[
32
, Conj. 2.1]
,

[
55
, Conj. 4.9]
,

[
63
, Conj. 5.5]
.

Partial type-
\mathsf{A}
solutions to Problem
1.1

were given in
[
17
]
,
[
58
]

for various bases of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
, and
the subset

(1.1)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\text{ avoids the patterns $3412$ and $4231${}}\}

of the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of
H_{n}(q)
.
By
[
57
, Thm. 4.3]
,
we have that for
w
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, there exists a planar network
F=F(w)

which serves as a combinatorial interpretation for
\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)
.
By
[
17
, Thm. 7.4]
,
there also exist
a poset
P=P(w)

and graph
G=G(w)

such that
evaluations
\theta_{q}(\widetilde{C}_{w}(q))
may be computed combinatorially
by

(1)

filling Young diagrams with paths in
F
,

(2)

filling Young diagrams with elements of
P
,

(3)

coloring vertices of
G
,

(4)

orienting edges of
G
,

while obeying certain rules in each case.
(See also
[
8
]
,
[
56
]
,
[
60
]
.)
While (
1.1
) is only a subset of the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis
of
H_{n}(q)
, it is conjectured
[
3
, Conj. 1.9]
,

[
32
, Conj.3̇.1]
that an even smaller subset

(1.2)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\text{ is codominant, i.e., avoids the pattern }312\}

explains trace evaluations at the entire Kazhdan–Lusztig basis.
It is known
[
17
, Thm. 4.6]

that for each element
\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)
of (
1.1
)
there exists an element
\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)
of (
1.2
)
with the property that
P(w)\cong P(v)

and therefore that
\theta_{q}(\widetilde{C}_{w}(q))=\theta_{q}(\widetilde{C}_{v}(q))
for all traces

\theta_{q}\in\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
.

One could also answer Problem
1.1

from the point of view of symmetric functions.
Let
\Lambda_{n}
be the
\mathbb{Z}
-module of homogeneous, degree-
n

symmetric functions.
Since the ranks of
\Lambda_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
are
equal,
it is possible to define
a generating function in
\Lambda_{n}

for evaluations of traces at
any fixed element
D\in H_{n}(q)
.
Following
[
58
, §2]
,
we use
the induced sign character basis
\{\epsilon_{q}^{\lambda}\}
of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))

and monomial symmetric function basis
\{m_{\lambda}\}
of
\Lambda_{n}

to define

Y_{q}(D):=\sum_{\lambda}\epsilon_{q}^{\lambda}(D)m_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]\otimes\Lambda_{n}.

A certain pairing of six natural bases of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))

and six natural bases of
\Lambda_{n}
then guarantees that for each pair

(\{\theta_{q}^{\lambda}\},\{g_{\lambda}\})
,
we have

Y_{q}(D)=\sum_{\lambda}\theta_{q}^{\lambda}(D)g_{\lambda}.

Thus
Y_{q}(D)
is in fact a generating function for the evaluation
of all elements of these six trace bases
at
D
. (See, e.g.,
[
58
, Prop. 2.1]
.)
Conveniently, the combinatorial computations mentioned above also
guarantee
that a certain

chromatic (quasi-)symmetric function

X_{G,q}
, defined in terms of the proper colorings of
G

[
55
]
,
satisfies
X_{G,q}=Y_{q}(\widetilde{C}_{w}(q))

[
17
, Thm. 7.4]
.
Thus for
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
the graph
G
essentially encodes all trace evaluations of the
form
\theta_{q}^{\lambda}(\widetilde{C}_{w}(q))
for
\{\theta_{q}^{\lambda}\}
one of the six natural
bases of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
.

Some of the above results
from
[
17
]
,
[
57
]
,
[
58
]

have type-
\mathsf{BC}
analogs,
i.e., extensions to the hyperoctahedral group
\mathfrak{B}_{n}

and its Hecke algebra
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
.
In Sections
2
–
4
,
we present these algebras, their Kazhdan–Lusztig bases,
and their trace spaces.
In Section
5
, we define type-
\mathsf{BC}
analogs
of type-
\mathsf{A}
planar networks, and use these to
graphically represent the subset

(1.3)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\text{ avoids the patterns $3412$ and $4231${}}\}

of the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]
.
In Section
6
we use immanants and total nonnegativity
to interpret trace evaluations at (
1.3
)
in terms of paths in the type-
\mathsf{BC}
planar networks.
In Sections
7
–
8
, we define type-
\mathsf{BC}
analogs

Q(w)
and
\Gamma(w)
of the type-
\mathsf{A}
posets and graphs
associated to planar networks.
We define a type-
\mathsf{BC}
analog of codominant permutations and
show that the posets correspond bijectively to the proper subset

(1.4)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\text{ $\mathsf{BC}$-codominant}\}

of (
1.3
).
We use the above networks, posets, and graphs
in Section
9

to state and prove our main results on
the combinatorial computation of type-
\mathsf{BC}
trace evaluations,
and in Section
10
to show that
for each element
\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)
of (
1.3
)
there exists an element
\widetilde{C}_{v}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)
of (
1.4
)
with the property that
Q(w)\cong Q(v)

and therefore that
\theta(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))=\theta(\widetilde{C}_{v}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))
for all traces

\theta\in\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{B}_{n})
.
Formulas in Section
9
lead to natural
type-
\mathsf{BC}
analogs of
type-
\mathsf{A}
chromatic symmetric functions
in Section
11
.
We finish in Section
12

with open problems concerning Hessenberg varieties.

2.
The symmetric and hyperoctahedral groups

The hyperoctahedral group
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is closely related to the symmetric
groups on
n
and
2n
letters. To describe these relationships,
we will use
subintervals of the set

[\overline{n},n]:=\{-n,\dotsc,n\}\smallsetminus\{0\},

where we define

\overline{a}=-a
for all
a\in[\overline{n},n]
.
We call any
subset

[h,l]:=\{h,\dotsc,l\}\smallsetminus\{0\}
of
[\overline{n},n]

an
interval
, even if
h<0<l
.
Let
\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
denote
the group of permutations of letters in the interval
[h,l]
.
The group
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is naturally related both to
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}

and
\mathfrak{S}_{n}=\mathfrak{S}_{[1,n]}
.
To illustrate these relationships and prepare for our main results,
we will consider the groups’ presentations, conjugacy classes, Bruhat
orders, and pattern-avoidance definitions.

2.1.
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
as a subgroup of
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}

The
2n
th symmetric group

\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}

is the Coxeter group
(see, e.g.,
[
14
]
) of type
\mathsf{A}_{2n-1}
,
with generators

s_{\overline{n-1}},\dotsc,s_{\overline{1}},s_{0},s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n-1}

and relations

\displaystyle s_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=e

\displaystyle\text{for $i=\overline{n-1},\dotsc,n-1$,}

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{j}

\displaystyle=s_{j}s_{i}

\displaystyle\text{for $|i-j|\geq 2$,}

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}

\displaystyle=s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}

\displaystyle\text{for $|i-j|=1$.}

If an expression
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}
for
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}

is as short as possible,
then call it
reduced
and call
\ell=\ell(w)
the
length
of
w
.
Define a (left) action of
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
on rearrangements

w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
of the word

\overline{n}\cdots\overline{1}1\cdots n
by

(2.1)

\begin{cases}s_{i}\text{ swaps letters in positions $i,i+1$}&\text{for $i=1,\dotsc,n-1$},\\
s_{\overline{i}}\text{ swaps letters in positions $\overline{i},\overline{i+1}$}&\text{for $i=1,\dotsc,n-1$},\\
s_{0}\text{ swaps letters in positions $\overline{1},1$,}\end{cases}

and
define the
one-line notation
of
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{r}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
to be

(2.2)

w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}=s_{i_{1}}(s_{i_{2}}(\cdots(s_{i_{r}}(\overline{n}\cdots\overline{1}1\cdots n))\cdots)).

For example, when
n=4
, the element
s_{\overline{1}}s_{0}s_{1}

has one-line notation

s_{\overline{1}}(s_{0}(s_{1}(\overline{4}\overline{3}\overline{2}\overline{1}1234)))=s_{\overline{1}}(s_{0}(\overline{4}\overline{3}\overline{2}\overline{1}2134))=s_{\overline{1}}(\overline{4}\overline{3}\overline{2}2\overline{1}134)=\overline{4}\overline{3}2\overline{2}\overline{1}134.

(By our definition, the right action of
s_{i}
swaps the letters
i,i+1
,
wherever they are.)
It follows that
w_{i}^{-1}
is the index
j
satisfying
w_{j}=i
.

For
[a,b]\subseteq[h,l]

let
\smash{s_{[a,b]}^{[h,l]}}
be the permutation
in
\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
whose one-line notation has the form

h\cdots(a-1)\cdot b(b-1)\cdots(a+1)a\cdot(b+1)\cdots l.

When the interval
[h,l]
is clear from context,
we will simply write
s_{[a,b]}
.
Call such an element a (type-
\mathsf{A}
)
reversal
.
Observe that the standard generators of
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}

are all reversals:

s_{0}=s_{[-1,1]}
,
and for
i\geq 1
we have

s_{i}=s_{[i,i+1]}
,

s_{\smash{\overline{i}}}=s_{\smash{[\overline{i+1},\overline{i}]}}
.
Also observe that each trivial reversal
s_{[a,a]}

is equal to the identity element
e
, and that two reversals commute
if their intervals do not intersect.

Let
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
be the Coxeter group of type
\mathsf{C}_{n}=\mathsf{B}_{n}
,
i.e., the hyperoctahedral group.
We may view
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
as the subgroup of
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}

generated by elements

t\ (=s^{\prime}_{0}):=s_{0},\qquad s^{\prime}_{i}:=s_{i}s_{\smash{\overline{i}}},\text{ for }i=1,\dotsc,n-1,

which satisfy the relations

\displaystyle\smash{{s^{\prime}_{i}}^{2}}

\displaystyle=e

\displaystyle\text{for $i=0,\dotsc,n-1$,}

\displaystyle ts^{\prime}_{1}ts^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{1}ts^{\prime}_{1}t,

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{i}s^{\prime}_{j}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{j}s^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle\text{for $i,j\geq 0$ and $|i-j|\geq 2$,}

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{i}s^{\prime}_{j}s^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{j}s^{\prime}_{i}s^{\prime}_{j}

\displaystyle\text{for $i,j\geq 1$ and $|i-j|=1$.}

The one-line notation for elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is inherited from that
of
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
.
For example, when
n=4
, the element
ts^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}_{4}

has one-line notation

t(s^{\prime}_{1}(s^{\prime}_{2}(\overline{4}\overline{3}\overline{2}\overline{1}1234)))=ts^{\prime}_{1}(\overline{4}\overline{2}\overline{3}\overline{1}1324))=t(\overline{4}\overline{2}\overline{1}\overline{3}3124)=\overline{4}\overline{2}\overline{1}3\overline{3}124.

If an expression
s^{\prime}_{i_{1}}\cdots s^{\prime}_{i_{\ell}}
for
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}

is as short as possible,
then call it
reduced
and call
\ell=\ell(w)
the
length
of
w
.
Let
\ell_{t}(w)
be the numer of ocurrences of
t
in any (equivalently, every)
reduced expression for
w
.
It is easy to see that one-line notations of
elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
are precisely the set of
permutations
w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}

which satisfy
w_{\overline{i}}=\overline{w_{i}}
,
i.e., each is completely determined by the
n
-letter subword

w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
Call these words the
long
and

short

one-line notations of
w
, respectively.

Define
type-
\mathsf{BC}
reversals
to be those elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}

having the forms

(2.3)

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{\smash{[\overline{a},a]}}

\displaystyle:=s_{\smash{[\overline{a},a]}},\mbox{ for }1\leq a\leq n,

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{\smash{[a,b]}}

\displaystyle:=s_{\smash{[\overline{b},\overline{a}]}}s_{[a,b]},\mbox{ for }1\leq a\leq b\leq n,

where
s_{[a,b]}
, etc., are reversals in
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
.

2.2.
Conjugacy classes, partitions, tableaux, bipartitions, and bitableaux

Conjugacy classes of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
correspond to
(integer) partitions
of
n
,
weakly decreasing positive integer sequences

\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{\ell})

satisfying
\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{\ell}=n
.
The
\ell=\ell(\lambda)
components of a partition
\lambda

are called its
parts

and we let the expressions

|\lambda|=n
and
\lambda\vdash n
denote that
\lambda
is
a partition of
n
.
Sometimes we use the notation
k^{a_{k}}
to denote a
sequence of
a_{k}
copies of the letter
k
.
Given
\lambda\vdash n
, we define the
transpose

partition
\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}=(\lambda_{1}^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace},\dotsc,\lambda_{\lambda_{1}}^{{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}})
by

\lambda_{i}^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}=\#\{j\,|\,\lambda_{j}\geq i\}
. Thus
n^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}=1^{n}
.
We call
\lambda

self-transpose
if
\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}=\lambda

and we define the empty sequence
\emptyset
to be the unique partition of the
integer
0
.

The conjugacy class of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
corresponding to
\lambda\vdash n

is the set of all permutations having cycle type
\lambda
. We write

\mathrm{ctype}(w)=\lambda
. Letting

a_{k}
be the multiplicity of
k
in
\lambda
, for
k=1,\dotsc,n
,
we may express the cardinality of the
\lambda
-conjugacy class of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
as

n!/z_{\lambda}
, where

(2.4)

z_{\lambda}={1^{a_{1}}\cdots n^{a_{n}}a_{1}!\cdots a_{n}!}.

Conjugacy classes of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
correspond to

integer bipartitions of
n
, pairs
(\lambda,\mu)

of integer partitions with

|\lambda|+|\mu|=n
.
We let

(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n
denote that
(\lambda,\mu)
is
a bipartition of
n
.
To explicitly describe the conjugacy classes of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
,
we define the homomorphism

(2.5)

\displaystyle\varphi:\mathfrak{B}_{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{n}

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{i},\quad i=1,\dotsc,n-1,

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto e,

which replaces letters in the short one-line notation of

v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
by their absolute values.
For each element
v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}

and each cycle
C=(c_{1},\dotsc,c_{k}=c_{0})

of
\varphi(v)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
define the
signed cycle

\hat{C}=(\hat{c}_{1},\dotsc,\hat{c}_{k})

of
v
by

\hat{c}_{i}=\begin{cases}c_{i}&\text{if $v(c_{i-1})=c_{i}$},\\
\overline{c}_{i}&\text{if $v(c_{i-1})=\overline{c_{i}}$}.\end{cases}

Call
\hat{C}

positive
if it has an even number of negative letters,
and
negative
otherwise.
The conjugacy class of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
corresponding to
(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n

is precisely the set of elements
whose
signed cycle type
, the bipartition of
positive cycle cardinalities and negative cycle cardinalities,
is equal to
(\lambda,\mu)
.
We write
\mathrm{sct}(w)=(\lambda,\mu)
.
We may express the cardinality of the
(\lambda,\mu)

conjugacy class of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
as
2^{n}n!/(z_{\lambda}z_{\mu}2^{\ell(\lambda)+\ell(\mu)})
.

To each integer partition

\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{\ell})\vdash n

we associate a
Young diagram of shape
\lambda
,
an arrangement of
n
boxes into
\ell
left-justified rows
with
\lambda_{i}
boxes in row
i
. By the French convention, row
1
appears
on the bottom.
A Young diagram filled with
elements of a set
S
is called a
tableau
or more specifically an

S
-tableau
. If
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
we also call it a

Young tableau
.
Repeated elements are permitted.
Given a bipartition
(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n
, we define
a Young
bidiagram
of shape
(\lambda,\mu)

to be an ordered pair of Young diagrams of shapes
\lambda
and
\mu
.
We define
bitableaux
similarly.

2.3.
The Bruhat order

For any Coxeter group
W
,
the
Bruhat order on
W
is the poset
defined by declaring
v\leq_{W}w

if
some (equivalently, every)
reduced expression for
w
contains a reduced expression for
v
.
Ehresmann
[
25
]

showed that the Bruhat order on
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
is isomorphic
to the (dual of the)
componentwise order on tableaux
\{A(w)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}\}

of shape
(2n,2n-1,\dotsc,1)
defined by placing the
increasing rearrangement of
w_{i}\cdots w_{n}
in row
i
,
for
i=\overline{n},\dotsc,n
.
For example, the type-
\mathsf{A}
Bruhat order comparison

\overline{3}21\overline{1}\overline{2}3\leq_{\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}}\overline{3}213\overline{2}\overline{1}

may be verified by the componentwise inequality

A(\overline{3}21\overline{1}\overline{2}3)\geq A(\overline{3}213\overline{2}\overline{1})
,

Proctor
[
51
, Thm. 5BC]
showed that the Bruhat order on
\mathfrak{B}_{n}

is isomorphic to a similar order on tableaux
\{B(w)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\}

of shape
(n,n-1,\dotsc,1)
defined by placing the
increasing rearrangement of
w_{i}\cdots w_{n}
in row
i
,
for
i=1,\dotsc,n
.
For example, the type-
\mathsf{BC}
Bruhat order comparison

\overline{3}21\overline{1}\overline{2}3\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}12\overline{3}3\overline{2}\overline{1}

may be verified by the componentwise inequality

B(\overline{3}21\overline{1}\overline{2}3)\geq B(12\overline{3}3\overline{2}\overline{1})
,

to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt,\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt|}}$}}\ .}}}}}}}}}}}}

It is not difficult to show that the Bruhat order on
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is
an induced subposet of the Bruhat order on
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
.

Proposition 2.1
.

For
v,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
, we have
v\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w
if and only if

v\leq_{\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}}w
.

Proof.

Consider
v,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
.
If we have
v\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w
,
then there is a reduced
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
-expression
s^{\prime}_{i_{1}}\cdots s^{\prime}_{i_{k}}
for
v

which is a subword of a reduced
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
-expression
s^{\prime}_{j_{1}}\cdots s^{\prime}_{j_{k}}

for
w
.
Then the recipe

s^{\prime}_{i}\mapsto\begin{cases}s_{\overline{i}}s_{i}&\text{if $i>0$}\\
t&\text{if $i=0$}\end{cases}

produces a reduced
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
-expression for
v

which is a subword of a reduced
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
-expression for
w
.
Now suppose that
v\leq_{\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}}w
.
By Ehresmann’s criterion, we have the componentwise
tableau inequality
A(v)\geq A(w)
.
But the upper
n
rows of these tableaux give the inequality
B(v)\geq B(w)
.
This is precisely Proctor’s criterion for
v\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w
.
∎

2.4.
Pattern avoidance

Given a word
u=u_{1}\cdots u_{k}
in
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,
and a word
y=y_{1}\cdots y_{k}
having
k
distinct letters,
we say that
y
matches the pattern
u
if the letters of
y
appear
in the same relative order as those of
u
; that is, if we have

u_{i}<u_{j}
if and only if
y_{i}<y_{j}
for all
i,j\in[k]
.
On the other hand, given a word
w=w_{1}\dotsc w_{m}
having distinct letters,
e.g.,
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
or
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
,
we say that
w
avoids the pattern
u
if no subword
of
w
matches the pattern
u
.

In
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, a second notion of pattern avoidance
involves signed letters and short one-line notation.
Let
v=v_{1}\cdots v_{k}
be the short one-line notation of
an element of
\mathfrak{B}_{k}
,
i.e., a word in
letters
[\overline{k},k]
with

|v_{1}|\cdots|v_{k}|\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
.
Let
y=y_{1}\cdots y_{k}
be a word in
[\overline{n},n]
such that

|y_{1}|\cdots|y_{k}|
has no repeated letters.
Say that
y
matches the signed pattern
v
if

(1)

for
i=1,\dotsc,k
, the letters
v_{i}
and
y_{i}
have the same sign,

(2)

for all
i,j
,
|v_{i}|<|v_{j}|
if and only if
|y_{i}|<|y_{j}|
.

Say that
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}

avoids the signed pattern
v
if no subword of
the short one-line notation of
w
matches the signed pattern
v
.
(See
[
10
, p. 108]
.)

Many properties of elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
can be expressed in terms of
signed pattern avoidance.

Lemma 2.2
.

The element
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoids the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
and
\overline{2}1

if and only if
the set of negative letters in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
is empty or
forms an interval
[\overline{b},\overline{1}]
for some
b\geq 1
.

Proof.

If all letters in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
are positive, then the claim is true.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Suppose therefore that the negative letters in this word do not form
an interval of the desired form. Then for some
i
,
j
, we have

w_{j}\leq\overline{1}
and
1\leq w_{i}<|w_{j}|
.
It follows that
w_{i}w_{j}
matches the signed
pattern
1\overline{2}
or
w_{j}w_{i}
matches the signed pattern
\overline{2}1
.

(\Leftarrow
)
If some subword
w_{i}w_{j}
with
i<j
matches the signed pattern

1\overline{2}
or
\overline{2}1
, then clearly the negative letters in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}

do not form the desired interval.
∎

Avoidance of signed patterns can also imply the avoidance of ordinary patterns.

Lemma 2.3
.

If
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoids the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2
, then
w
avoids the unsigned patterns

3412
and
4231
.

Proof.

First we claim that if
w
contains a subword
cdab

matching the unsigned pattern
3412
, then it must contain a subword
matching one of the five signed patterns.
Suppose that
cdab
or just
dab
is a subword of
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
If
b>0
then
dab
matches the signed pattern
312
or
3\overline{1}2
.
If
b<0
then
ab
matches the signed pattern
\overline{2}\overline{1}
.
Now suppose that
cd
appears in
w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}

and
ab
in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
If
b<0
then
ab
matches the signed pattern
\overline{2}\overline{1}
.
If
b>0
then
\overline{c}
and
\overline{d}
appear in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
without
\overline{b}
,
contradicting Lemma
2.2
.
Now suppose that
cdab
or just
cda

appears in
w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}
.
Then
\overline{badc}
or
\overline{adc}

is a subword of
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
, matching the unsigned pattern

3412
or
412
. By the first case,
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}

has a subword matching one of the signed patterns

312
,
3\overline{1}2
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
.

Now we claim that if
w
contains a subword
dbca

matching the unsigned pattern
4231
, then it must contain a subword
matching one of the five signed patterns.
Suppose that
dbca
is a subword of
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
If
c>0
then
dbc
matches the signed pattern
312
or
3\overline{1}2
.
If
c<0
then
bc
matches the signed pattern
\overline{2}\overline{1}
.
Now suppose that
d
appears in
w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}

and
bca
in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
. If
c>0
then
\overline{d}

appears in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
, contradicting Lemma
2.2
.
If
c<0
then
bc
matches the signed pattern
\overline{2}\overline{1}
.
Now suppose that
db
appears in
w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}

and
ca
in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
If
c>0
then
\overline{d}
(
<0
) appears in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
,
contradicting Lemma
2.2
.
If
c<0
then
\overline{b}
(
>0
) appears in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
,
also contradicting Lemma
2.2
.
Finally, suppose that
dbca
or just
dbc

appears in
w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}
.
Then
\overline{acbd}
or
\overline{cbd}

is a subword of
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
, matching the unsigned pattern

4231
or
423
. By the first and second cases above,
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}

has a subword matching one of the five signed patterns.
∎

3.
Schubert varieties and Hecke algebras

Our main results (Theorem
9.6
–
Theorem
9.8
)
partially answer
Problem
1.1
using a linearly independent set in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]
.
This set is best described in terms of a special basis of the
Hecke algebra
H(\mathfrak{B}_{n})
and smoothness of certain Schubert varieties.

3.1.
Schubert varieties

Let
\mathrm{G}
be a complex connected semisimple algebraic group,
choose a Borel subgroup
\mathrm{B}
of
\mathrm{G}
, and consider
the quotient

\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{B}
,
called a
flag variety
.
The action of
\mathrm{B}
on
\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{B}
by left multiplication
partitions
it
into orbits often written

\mathrm{B}\dot{w}\mathrm{B}
,
which are
parametrized by elements
w
of the corresponding Weyl group
W
.
The Zariski closure
\Omega_{w}
of

\mathrm{B}\dot{w}\mathrm{B}

in
\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{B}

is called the
Schubert variety indexed by
w
.
We have

\Omega_{v}\supseteq\Omega_{w}
if and only if
v\leq w
in the Bruhat order.
(See, e.g.,
[
10
, § 4.7]
.)
Standard choices of
\mathrm{G}

are

\mathrm{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})

(type
\mathsf{A}
),

\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{C})

(type
\mathsf{B}
),
and
\mathrm{SP}_{n}(\mathbb{C})

(type
\mathsf{C}
).
The corresponding Weyl groups are
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
(type
\mathsf{A}
), and

\mathfrak{B}_{n}

(types
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{C}
).

Call the Schubert variety
\Omega_{w}

rationally smooth
if
its ordinary cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\Omega_{w})
and
intersection cohomology
\mathrm{IH}^{*}(\Omega_{w})
coincide.
(See
[
10
, Ch. 6]
.)
Call
\Omega_{w}

smooth
if the tangent space at every
point has dimension equal to the dimension of the variety.
It is known that every smooth
Schubert variety is rationally smooth.
Let
\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{A}}
(
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
),
\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{B}}
,
\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{C}}
(
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
)
denote the type-
\mathsf{A}
,

\mathsf{B}
, and

C
Schubert varieties,
respectively. Elements
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
for which
\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{A}}
is rationally
smooth or smooth are characterized by pattern avoidance
[
43
]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
the
Schubert variety

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{A}}

is smooth, equivalently rationally smooth,
if and only if
w
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
.

Smoothness and rational smoothness of type-
\mathsf{B}
and

C

Schubert varieties are charcterized by more intricate pattern avoidance.
The conditions on
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
which imply
rational smoothness of

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{B}}

are the same as those which imply
rational smoothness of

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{C}}

[
12
, Thm. 4.2]
:

w
must avoid the twenty-five
patterns listed in
[
10
, Eq. (13.3.5)]
.
On the other hand,
the conditions
which imply smoothness of the two Schubert varieties
are different
[
10
, Thm. 8.3.17]
:

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{B}}

is smooth if and only if it is rationally smooth and

w
avoids
the additional pattern
3412
;

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{C}}

is smooth if and only if it is rationally smooth and

w
avoids
the additional pattern
4231
.

We will be interested in those elements
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
for which

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{B}}

and

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{C}}

are simultaneously smooth.
These are precisely the elements
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
for which

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{A}}
is smooth when
\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{SL}_{2n}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proposition 3.2
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
,
the
Schubert varieties

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{B}}

and

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{C}}

are simultaneously smooth
if and only if

w
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
.

Proof.

If
\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{B}}

and

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{C}}

are both smooth, then
by the above discussion,

w
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
.
Suppose that

w
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
.
It is straightforward to check that
each of the twenty-five patterns listed in

[
10
, Eq. (13.3.5)]

contains
3412
and/or
4231
.
Thus

w
avoids these twenty-five patterns as well,
and

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{B}}
,

\Omega_{w}^{\mathsf{C}}

are both smooth.
∎

3.2.
Hecke algebras

Given Coxeter group
W
with generator set
S
, define
the
Hecke algebra

H(W)
of
W
to be
the
\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
-span of
\{T_{w}\,|\,w\in W\}

with multiplicative unit
T_{e}
and multiplication defined by

T_{s}T_{w}=\begin{cases}qT_{sw}+(q-1)T_{w}&\text{if $sw<_{W}w$},\\
T_{sw}&\text{if $sw>_{W}w$},\end{cases}

where
s\in S
,
w\in W
, and
<_{W}
is the Bruhat order on
W
.
This formula guarantees that for
w\in W
and
any reduced expression
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}
for
w
,
we have
T_{w}=T_{s_{i_{1}}}\cdots T_{s_{i_{\ell}}}
.
Call
\{T_{w}\,|\,w\in W\}
the
natural basis
of
H(W)
.
It is easy to see that the specialization of
H(W)
at
q=1

is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}[W]
.

A second basis
[
39
]
of
H
is the (modified, signless)

Kazhdan–Lusztig basis

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in W\}
,
related to the natural basis by

\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)=\sum_{v\leq_{W}w}P_{v,w}(q)T_{v},

where

\{P_{v,w}(q)\,|\,v,w\in W\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}[q]
are the

Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials

whose recursive definition appears in
[
39
]
.
Coefficients of these polynnomials may be interpreted in terms of
intersection cohomology
\mathrm{IH}^{*}(\Omega_{w})

[
40
]
. Specifically,
when
\Omega_{w}
is rationally smooth,
all
polynomials

\{P_{v,w}(q)\,|\,v\leq_{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}w\}

are identically

1

[
39
, Thm. A.2]
.
Thus we have the following.

Proposition 3.3
.

For
W
equal to
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
or
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
and
w\in W
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element
\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)

of
H(W)

satisfies

\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)=\sum_{v\leq_{W}w}T_{v}.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
3.1
,
3.2
.
∎

In Sections
4
–
12
we will find it convenient to define

H_{n}(q):=H(\mathfrak{S}_{n}),\qquad H_{[h,l]}(q):=H(\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}),\qquad H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q):=H(\mathfrak{B}_{n}),

and to let
\{\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\}
and
\{\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\}

denote the Kazhdan–Lusztig bases of
H_{n}(q)
and
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
, respectively.

4.
Trace spaces

Given a Coxeter group
W
and its Hecke algebra
H=H(W)
,
let
\mathcal{T}(H)
be the
\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
-module of
H
-traces,
linear functionals
\theta_{q}:H\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
satisfying

\theta_{q}(DD^{\prime})=\theta_{q}(D^{\prime}D)
for all
D,D^{\prime}\in H
.
Thus
\mathcal{T}(H)
includes all
H
-characters.
Let
\mathcal{T}(W)=\mathcal{T}(\mathbb{Z}[W])

be the specialization of
\mathcal{T}(H)
at
q=1

That is,
for each
H
-trace

\theta_{q}\in\mathcal{T}(H)
satisfying

\theta_{q}(T_{w})=f_{w}(q)

for all
w\in W
,
define the
W
-trace

\theta=\theta_{1}\in\mathcal{T}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{S}_{n}])
by

\theta(w)=f_{w}(1)
for all
w\in W
.

The ranks of
\mathcal{T}(H)
and
\mathcal{T}(W)
are both equal to
the number of conjugacy classes of
W
.
We consider six bases of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
and
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{S}_{n})
,
and eleven bases of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
and
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{B}_{n})
.

4.1.
The trace spaces
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
and
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{S}_{n})

The rank of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
equals the number of partitions of
n
.
Three commonly used bases
consist of
H_{n}(q)
-characters.
These are the bases of
irreducible characters
\{\chi_{q}^{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
,
induced trivial characters
\{\eta_{q}^{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
,
and induced sign characters
\{\epsilon_{q}^{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
,
where

\eta_{q}^{\lambda}=\mathrm{triv}_{q}\big\uparrow\negthickspace\negthickspace\phantom{\uparrow}_{H_{\lambda}(q)}^{H_{n}(q)},\qquad\mathrm{triv}_{q}(T_{s_{i}})=q,\qquad\epsilon_{q}^{\lambda}=\mathrm{sgn}_{q}\big\uparrow\negthickspace\negthickspace\phantom{\uparrow}_{H_{\lambda}(q)}^{H_{n}(q)},\qquad\mathrm{sgn}_{q}(T_{s_{i}})=-1,

and
H_{\lambda}(q)
is the Young subgroup of
H_{n}(q)
generated by

\{T_{s_{1}},\dotsc,T_{s_{n-1}}\}\smallsetminus\{T_{s_{\lambda_{1}}},T_{s_{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}},\dotsc,T_{s_{n-\lambda_{\ell}}}\}.

All
H_{n}(q)
-characters in
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
belong to
\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{N}[q]}\{\chi_{q}^{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
.
Three more non-character bases of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
consist of traces which we call

power sum traces

\{\psi_{q}^{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
,

monomial traces

\{\phi_{q}^{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
, and

forgotten traces

\{\gamma_{q}^{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
,
and which are defined by

\psi_{q}^{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu}\chi^{\mu}(\lambda)\chi_{q}^{\mu},\qquad\phi_{q}^{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu}K^{-1}_{\lambda,\mu}\chi_{q}^{\mu},\qquad\gamma_{q}^{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu}K^{-1}_{\lambda,\mu^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}}\chi_{q}^{\mu},

where
\chi^{\mu}(\lambda):=\chi^{\mu}(w)

for any
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
with
\mathrm{ctype}(w)=\lambda
,
and
\{K^{-1}_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,\lambda,\mu\vdash n\}
are the
inverse
Kostka numbers
. (See
[
61
, §7]
.)
The specialization of the power sum trace basis at
q=1

is
essentially an indicator basis for conjugacy classes of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,

(4.1)

\psi^{\lambda}(w)=\begin{cases}z_{\lambda}&\text{if $\mathrm{ctype}(w)=\lambda$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

4.2.
The trace spaces
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{B}_{n})
and
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))

The rank of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
equals the number of bipartitions of
n
.
Ten commonly used bases
can be constructed from pairs of type-
\mathsf{A}

Hecke algebra trace bases, i.e., bases of

\bigoplus_{k=0}^{n}\mathcal{T}(H_{k}(q))\otimes\mathcal{T}(H_{n-k}(q)),

and from the Young subalgebra

H_{k,n-k}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
generated by

\{T_{t},T_{\smash{s^{\prime}_{\smash{1}}}},\dotsc,T_{\smash{s^{\prime}_{\smash{k-1}}}}\}\cup\{T_{t_{k}},T_{\smash{s^{\prime}_{\smash{k+1}}}},\dotsc,T_{\smash{s^{\prime}_{\smash{n-1}}}}\},

where
t_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k}\cdots s^{\prime}_{1}ts^{\prime}_{1}\cdots s^{\prime}_{k}
.
Specifically, given bases

(4.2)

\{\zeta_{q}^{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}\subseteq\mathcal{T}(H_{k}(q)),\qquad\{\xi_{q}^{\mu}\,|\,\mu\vdash n-k\}\subseteq\mathcal{T}(H_{n-k}(q)),

we define traces

q\zeta_{q}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{T}(H_{k}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
,

\delta\xi_{q}^{\mu}\in\mathcal{T}(H_{n-k}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
by

q\zeta_{q}^{\lambda}(T_{w}):=q^{\ell_{t}(w)}\zeta_{q}^{\lambda}(T_{\varphi(w)}),\qquad\delta\xi_{q}^{\mu}(T_{w}):=(-1)^{\ell_{t}(w)}\xi_{q}^{\mu}(T_{\varphi(w)}).

Then we create a basis

\{(\zeta\xi)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\}

of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
by
inducing

(4.3)

(\zeta\xi)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}:=(q\zeta_{q}^{\lambda}\otimes\delta\xi_{q}^{\mu})\big\uparrow\negthickspace\negthickspace\phantom{\uparrow}_{H_{k,n-k}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)}^{H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)}.

This construction of the irreducible characters

\{(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\}

of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)

can be deduced from Hoefsmit
[
35
, §2.2]
.
(See also
[
24
]
,
[
28
, §5.5]
.)
One then verifies that other trace bases are related
to the irreducible character basis by
matrices described in
[
9
, §3]
.
When the bases in (
4.2
) are type-
\mathsf{A}
character bases,
the definition (
4.3
) gives a character basis of

\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
.
More examples are the
induced one-dimensional characters,

\{(\eta\eta)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(\eta\epsilon)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(\epsilon\eta)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(\epsilon\epsilon)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\}.

Five
bases of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
which do not consist of characters are
formed from
the definition (
4.3
) and
type-
\mathsf{A}
power sum, monomial, and forgotten traces,

\begin{gathered}\{(\psi\psi)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\\
\{(\phi\phi)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(\phi\gamma)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(\gamma\phi)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(\gamma\gamma)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\}.\end{gathered}

An eleventh basis of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
,

\{\iota_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},

may be defined in terms of irreducible characters by

\iota_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}(\chi\chi)^{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda,\mu)(\chi\chi)_{q}^{\alpha,\beta},

where
we define

(\chi\chi)^{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda,\mu):=(\chi\chi)^{\alpha,\beta}(w)

for any
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
having signed cycle type

(\lambda,\mu)
. (See, e.g.,
[
5
]
.)
The specialization of this basis at
q=1

is
essentially an indicator basis for conjugacy classes of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
,

(4.4)

\iota^{\lambda,\mu}(w)=\begin{cases}z_{\lambda}z_{\mu}2^{\ell(\lambda)+\ell(\mu)}&\text{if $\mathrm{sct}(w)=(\lambda,\mu)$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

5.
Planar networks

Several partial solutions to the type-
\mathsf{A}
case of Problem
1.1

involve
the subset

(5.1)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\text{ avoids the patterns $3412$ and $4231${}}\}

of the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of
H_{n}(q)
.
The graphical representation of these elements
by
planar networks
called type-
\mathsf{A}

zig-zag networks

[
57
, §3]

allows for simple combinatorial interpretation of certain trace
evaluations
[
57
, §5–10]
.
Moreover, the subset

(5.2)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\text{ avoids the pattern }312\}

of (
5.1
)
and its graphical representation by the subset of zig-zag networks
called
descending star networks

[
57
]

captures much of the same information.

We will extend the above type-
\mathsf{A}
results to types
\mathsf{B}
and
C

by defining
type-
\mathsf{BC}
zig-zag networks

to graphically represent the subset

(5.3)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\text{ avoids the patterns $3412$ and $4231${}}\}

of the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
,
and
type-
\mathsf{BC}
descending star networks

to graphically represent the subset

(5.4)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\text{ avoids the signed patterns $1\overline{2}$, $\overline{2}1$, $\overline{2}\overline{1}$, $312$, $3\overline{1}2${}}\}

of (
5.3
).
These graphical representations facilitate
simple combinatorial interpretation of certain trace evaluations
(Section
9
), when we specialize at
q=1
.

5.1.
Type-
\mathsf{A}
planar networks and factoriztion

Define a
type-
\mathsf{A}
planar network with boundary vertices indexed by
the interval
[h,l]

to be a directed, planar,
acyclic multigraph which can be embedded in a disc so that

2|[h,l]|
boundary vertices can be labeled clockwise as

source

h,\dotsc,

source

l
,

sink

l,\dotsc,

sink

h
.
We will allow edges
(x,y)

to be marked by a positive integer multiplicity
k

and will say that such an edge contributes
k
to the outdegree of
x

and to the indegreee of
y
.
We will assume all sources to have indegree
0
and outdegree
1
,
and all sinks to have indegree
1
and outdegree
0
.
Let
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
denote the set of such networks.

For each subinterval
[a,b]
of
[h,l]
we define a
simple star network

\smash{F_{[a,b]}^{[h,l]}}\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
by

(1)

Sources
h,\dotsc,l
lie on a vertical line to the left;
sinks
h,\dotsc,l
lie on a vertical line to the right.
Both are labeled from bottom to top.

(2)

An interior vertex lies between the sources and sinks.

(3)

For
i=h,\dotsc,a-1
and
i=b+1,\dotsc,l
, a directed edge
begins at source
i
and terminates at sink
i
.

(4)

For
i=a,\dotsc,b
, a directed edge begins at source
i
and
terminates at the interior vertex, and another directed edge begins
at the interior vertex and terminates at sink
i
.

(5)

All edges have multiplicity
1
.

When the set of source and sink labels is clear,
we omit the superscript
[h,l]
and write
F_{[a,b]}
.
For zero- and one-element subintervals
we define the trivial network
F_{\emptyset}=F_{[h,h]}=\cdots=F_{[l,l]}

to have no interior vertex, and
|[h,l]|
horizontal edges, each from source
i

to sink
i
, for
i=h,\dotsc,l
.
For example,
the (infinite) set
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([\overline{2},2])
contains
seven simple star networks:

(5.5)

where

.
In figures,
all edges in planar networks
should be understood to be oriented from left to right,
with vertices at both ends of all line
segments, and additional vertices at the centers of the
stars formed from crossing line segments.

F_{\smash{[\overline{1},2]}}
above can be more completely drawn as

For economy, we will omit edge orientations and vertices from
drawings of planar networks.
When there is no danger of confusion, we will omit source and sink labels
as well.

Given networks
E,F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
,
in which all sources have
outdegree
1
and all sinks have indegree
1
, define
the concatenation
E\circ F
of
E
and
F

as follows. For
i=h,\dotsc,l
, do

(1)

remove sink
i
of
E
and source
i
of
F
,

(2)

merge each edge
(x,\text{sink }i)
in
E
with each edge

(\text{source }i,y)
in
F

to form a single edge
(x,y)
in
E\circ F
.

Observe that for
nonintersecting intervals
[c_{1},d_{1}]
,
[c_{2},d_{2}]
,
the concatenations

F_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\circ F_{[c_{2},d_{2}]}
and

F_{[c_{2},d_{2}]}\circ F_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}
are
isomorphic as directed graphs.
Observe also that sometimes in a concatenation
E\circ F
,
there may exist vertices
x
in
E
,
y
in
F

with

m(x,y)>1
multiplicity-
1
edges
incident upon both.
Define the
condensed concatenation

E\bullet F
to be the
subdigraph of
E\circ F
obtained
by removing, for all such pairs
(x,y)
,
all but one of the
m(x,y)
edges incident upon both,
and by marking this edge with the multiplicity
m(x,y)
.
For example, in
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([\overline{2},2])
we have the isomorphic graphs

(5.6)

F_{[\overline{2},\overline{1}]}\circ F_{[1,2]}=F_{[\overline{2},\overline{1}]}\bullet F_{[1,2]}=\hbox to51.5pt{\vbox to54.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.52347pt\lower-27.51146pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-7.68352pt}{19.08401pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-7.68352pt}{4.85764pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -9.84 -29.53 L 0 -9.84 L 9.84 -29.53 L 49.21 -29.53 L 59.06 -9.84 L 68.9 -29.53}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

in which two pairs of edges are replaced by two single edges
marked with multiplicity
2
.
We refer to all iterations of concatenations and condensed concatenations
of simple star networks as
star networks
.
In fact, each element of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
is isomorphic to a star network,
so we may think of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
as a set of
star networks.

Given planar network
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
, define
its
path matrix

A=A(F)=(a_{i,j})_{i,j\in[h,l]}
by

(5.8)

a_{i,j}=\#\text{ paths in $F$ from source $i$ to sink $j$},

ignoring multiplicities.
For instance, the star networks in
(
5.6
) – (
5.7
)
have path matrices

\begin{bmatrix}a_{\overline{2},\overline{2}}&a_{\overline{2},\overline{1}}&a_{\overline{2},1}&a_{\overline{2},2}\\
a_{\overline{1},\overline{2}}&a_{\overline{1},\overline{1}}&a_{\overline{1},1}&a_{\overline{1},2}\\
a_{1,\overline{2}}&a_{1,\overline{1}}&a_{1,1}&a_{1,2}\\
a_{2,\overline{2}}&a_{2,\overline{1}}&a_{2,1}&a_{2,2}\end{bmatrix}\quad=\quad\begin{bmatrix}1&1&0&0\\
1&1&0&0\\
0&0&1&1\\
0&0&1&1\end{bmatrix}\negthickspace,\ \begin{bmatrix}5&5&5&2\\
5&5&5&2\\
5&5&5&2\\
2&2&2&1\end{bmatrix}\negthickspace,\ \begin{bmatrix}2&2&2&1\\
2&2&2&1\\
2&2&2&1\\
1&1&1&1\end{bmatrix}\negthickspace,

repectively.

Definition 5.1
.

Define
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
to be set of all type-
\mathsf{A}
planar networks of the
form

(5.9)

F=F_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[c_{t},d_{t}]},\\

and call these

type-
\mathsf{A}
condensed star networks
(with boundary vertices indexed by
[h,l]
)
.

We will be interested in two subclasses of these,
which we define as follows.

Definition 5.2
.

Call a type-
\mathsf{A}
condensed star network
F

(
5.9
)
a
type-
\mathsf{A}
zig-zag network

if we have
F=F_{\emptyset}
or

(1)

the intervals
[c_{1},d_{1}],\dotsc,[c_{t},d_{t}]

are distinct and pairwise nonnesting,

(2)

for all triples

i<j<k
satisfying

[c_{i},d_{i}]\cap[c_{j},d_{j}]\neq\emptyset
and

[c_{j},d_{j}]\cap[c_{k},d_{k}]\neq\emptyset
,
we have

c_{i}<c_{j}<c_{k}

(and
d_{i}<d_{j}<d_{k}
)
or
c_{i}>c_{j}>c_{k}

(and
d_{i}>d_{j}>d_{k}
).

Let
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([h,l])
denote the set of type-
\mathsf{A}
zig-zag networks
with boundary vertices indexed by
[h,l]
.

Definition 5.3
.

Call a type-
\mathsf{A}
condensed star network
F
(
5.9
)
a
type-
\mathsf{A}
descending star network

if we have
F=F_{\emptyset}
or

(1)

the intervals
[c_{1},d_{1}],\dotsc,[c_{t},d_{t}]

are distinct and pairwise nonnesting,

(2)

for all pairs

i<j
satisfying

[c_{i},d_{i}]\cap[c_{j},d_{j}]\neq\emptyset

we have
c_{i}>c_{j}
(and
d_{i}>d_{j}
).

Let
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([h,l])
denote the set of type-
\mathsf{A}
descending star networks
with boundary vertices indexed by
[h,l]
.
Thus we have
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([h,l])\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([h,l])
.
To illustrate, let us fix boundary vertices
indexed by any interval of cardinality
4
.
Then we have
14
descending star networks,

(5.10)

\raisebox{-17.07164pt}{
\includegraphics[height]{xfigures2-eps-converted-to.pdf}}\ ,\hskip-361.34999pt{\tiny{{}^{{}_{(2)}}}}\hskip 361.34999pt

and
8
more zig-zag networks
which are not descending star networks,

(5.11)

\raisebox{-17.07164pt}{
\includegraphics[height]{xfigureszz-eps-converted-to.pdf}}\ .\hskip-267.39877pt{\tiny{{}^{{}_{(2)}}}}\hskip 267.39877pt

The result
[
17
, Lem. 3.5]

describes intersections of paths in a descending star network.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\pi_{i_{1}}
,
\pi_{i_{2}}
be paths in a descending star network
F

from sources
i_{1}<i_{2}
to sinks
m_{1}
,
m_{2}
, respectively.
Then the two paths intersect if and only if there exists a path in
F

from
i_{1}
to sink
m_{2}
.

By
[
57
, Thm. 3.5, Lem. 5.3]

and
[
17
, Thm. 3.6]
,
the sets

\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([h,l])
,
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([h,l])

are related to pattern avoidance in
\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

There is a natural bijection
F\mapsto w(F)
from

\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([h,l])

to
3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
,
which restricts to a bijection from
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([h,l])
to

312
-avoiding permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
.

To describe
the bijection
explicitly
we
define
a relation
\prec\negthickspace\negthinspace\cdot\

on the set of intervals appearing in (
5.9
)
by
declaring

(5.12)

[c_{i},d_{i}]\prec\negthickspace\negthinspace\cdot\ [c_{j},d_{j}]

if

i<j
and

[c_{i},d_{i}]\cap[c_{j},d_{j}]\smallsetminus([c_{i+1},d_{i+1}]\cup\cdots\cup[c_{j-1},d_{j-1}])\neq\emptyset
.
The relation
\prec\negthickspace\negthinspace\cdot\
may be viewed as an acyclic directed graph on
the intervals.
The transitive, reflexive closure of
\prec\negthickspace\negthinspace\cdot\
is a partial order
\preceq
.
For
F\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([h,l])
, the directed graph is the Hasse diagram
of the partial order; for other
F\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
this is not the case.
For example, the networks

F_{[2,5]}\bullet F_{[1,3]}\bullet F_{[4,6]}\bullet F_{[6,7]}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([1,7])

and

F_{[2,5]}\bullet F_{[1,2]}\bullet F_{[4,6]}\bullet F_{[2,5]}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}([1,7])

and their corresponding interval digraphs and posets are

(5.13)

The bijection
F\mapsto w(F)
,
stated in

[
57
, §3]
,
is given by the following algorithm.

Algorithm 5.6
.

Given
F
as in (
5.9
), do

(1)

Initialize the sequence of reversals

S:=(s_{[c_{1},d_{1}]},\dotsc,s_{[c_{t},d_{t}]})
.

(2)

For all pairs
(i,j)
with
[c_{i},d_{i}]\prec\negthickspace\negthinspace\cdot\ [c_{j},d_{j}]
and

|[c_{i},d_{i}]\cap[c_{j},d_{j}]|>1
,

(a)

Update
S
by inserting
s_{[c_{i},d_{i}]\cap[c_{j},d_{j}]}

immediately after
s_{[c_{i},d_{i}]}
.

(3)

Define
w(F)
to be the product of reversals in
S
, from left to right.

We call the final sequence of reversals a

zig-zag factorization
of
w(F)
.
For example, let
F
be the first star network in (
5.13
).
This zig-zag network
F
gives the
reversal sequence
(s_{[2,5]},s_{[1,3]},s_{[4,6]},s_{[6,7]})
which we update
by inserting
s_{[2,5]\cap[1,3]}=s_{[2,3]}
after
s_{[2,5]}
, and then

s_{[2,5]\cap[4,6]}=s_{[4,5]}
after
s_{[2,5]}
to obtain
the permutation

w=w(F)=s_{[2,5]}s_{[4,5]}s_{[2,3]}s_{[1,3]}s_{[4,6]}s_{[6,7]}=3752146.

The inverse of the map
F\mapsto w(F)
,
which we write

(5.14)

w\mapsto F_{w},

is a bit intricate and is given in
[
57
, §3]
.
It turns out that the network
F
above is
F_{3752146}
.
In (
5.10
), if we label sources and sinks
1,2,3,4
from bottom
to top, the descending star networks are

(5.15)

F_{4321},F_{3421},F_{2431},F_{3241},F_{1432},F_{3214},F_{2341},F_{1243},F_{1324},F_{2134},F_{2143},F_{1342},F_{2314},F_{1234},

respectively.
In (
5.11
), the remaining zig-zag networks are

(5.16)

F_{4312},F_{4213},F_{4132},F_{4123},F_{3124},F_{1423},F_{1423},F_{3142},F_{2413}.

The restriction of the map (
5.14
) to
312
-avoiding elements of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}

is in fact rather simple. Given word
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}

with distinct letters,
say that
w
has a
record
at position
j

if
w_{j}=\max\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{j}\}
.

Algorithm 5.7
.

Given
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the pattern
312
, do

(1)

Let
w
have records at positions
1=j_{1},\dotsc,j_{k}
.

(2)

Define

F_{w}=F_{[j_{k},w_{j_{k}}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[j_{1},w_{j_{1}}]}
.

The bijection
F\mapsto w(F)
is closely related to
families of source-to-sink paths in
F
, and also to Kazhdan–Lusztig
basis elements of the Hecke algebra of
\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
.
Given
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
,
call a sequence
\pi=(\pi_{h},\dotsc,\pi_{l})
of paths in
F

a
path family of type
w=w_{h}\cdots w_{l}
if for
i=h,\dotsc,l
,
path
\pi_{i}
begins at source
i
and ends at sink
w_{i}
.
Say that a path family
\pi

covers

F
if every edge of
F

appears in at least one path of
\pi
, and define the sets

(5.17)

\begin{gathered}\Pi(F)=\{\pi\,|\,\pi\text{ covers }F\},\\
\Pi_{w}(F)=\{\pi\in\Pi(F)\,|\,\mathrm{type}(\pi)=w\}.\end{gathered}

the star network
and path family

(5.18)

belong to
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([1,3])
and
\Pi_{s_{1}}(F)\subset\Pi(F)
, respectively.
When
F
is a zig-zag network,
we may characterize
w(F)
in terms of
\Pi(F)
as
follows
[
57
, Lem. 5.3]
.

Proposition 5.8
.

For
F\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([h,l])
,

w(F)
is the unique permutation of maximum length in

\{\mathrm{type}(\pi)\,|\,\pi\in\Pi(F)\}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
.

For all
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
, the set
\Pi(F)
associates an element of

\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}]
to
F
:
we say that
F

graphically represents

(5.19)

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(F)}\mathrm{type}(\pi)

as an element of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}]
.
For example, the network
F
in (
5.18
) can be covered by

72
different path families:
12
of each type
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}
.
Thus it graphically represents

12\,\widetilde{C}_{s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}}(1)

as an element of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{S}_{3}]
.

Again for all
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{A}}([h,l])
,
the set
\Pi(F)
also associates an element of

H_{[h,l]}(q)
to
F
.
To describe this element explicitly, we first assume
that
F
is formed by some iteration of ordinary or condensed concatenatation
of simple star networks
F_{[c_{1},d_{1}]},\dotsc,F_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}
with
internal vertices
z_{1},\dotsc,z_{t}
. Observe that
the intersection of two source-to-sink paths
\pi_{i}
,
\pi_{j}
in
F

must be a disjoint union of the above internal vertices of
F

and paths between these.
We say that
\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{j}

meet
at a central vertex
z_{k}

if both paths contain
z_{k}
, and enter it via different edges.
Given a path family
\pi
covering
F
,
define a
defect
of
\pi
to be a triple
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
with

(1)

i<j
,

(2)

\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{j}
meet at vertex
z_{k}
of

F
after having crossed an odd number of times.

Let
\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)
denote the number of defects of
\pi
.
(This definition from
[
18
]

generalizes those of
[
13
]
,
[
23
]
.)
We say that
F

graphically represents

(5.20)

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(F)}q^{\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}

as an element of
H_{[h,l]}(q)
.
For example,
the path family
\pi
in (
5.18
)
satisfies
\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)=3
: the defects are

(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},2)
,
(\pi_{1},\pi_{3},3)
,
(\pi_{2},\pi_{3},3)
.
It is possible to show that the network
F
in (
5.18
)
graphically represents

(1+q)^{2}(1+q+q^{2})\widetilde{C}_{s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}}(q)

as an element of
H_{3}(q)
.

It is clear that if
F
graphically represents

D(q)\in H_{[h,l]}(q)
as an element of
H_{[h,l]}(q)
, then
it graphically represents

D(1)
as an element of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}]
.
It is possible to show that
all star networks
graphically represent products of Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements
(possibly divided by integers or polynomials in
q
).
In particular, the result
[
13
, Thm. 1]

shows that certain wiring diagrams
graphically represent certain Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let

w\in\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}

avoid the patterns

321
,
56781234
,
56718234
,
46781235
,
46718235
,
and let
F
be the wiring diagram corresponding to any reduced expression
for
w
. Then
F
graphically represents
\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)

as an element of

H_{[h,l]}(q)
.

The result
[
57
, Lem. 5.3]
shows that zig-zag networks
give graphical representations of other Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements.

Proposition 5.10
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
the zig-zag network
F_{w}
graphically represents
\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)

as an element of
H_{[h,l]}(q)
.

This fact has the following consequence.

Corollary 5.11
.

For
v,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
with
w
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
the number of path families of type
v
covering
F_{w}
is

1
if
v\leq_{\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}}w
, and is
0
otherwise.

5.2.
Type-
\mathsf{BC}
planar networks and factoriztion

For fixed
n
, define
type-
\mathsf{BC}
simple star networks
with boundary vertices indexed by
[\overline{n},n]
to be the
type-
\mathsf{A}
star networks

(5.21)

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{[a,b]}

\displaystyle:=F_{[a,b]}\bullet F_{[\overline{a},\overline{b}]}=F_{[a,b]}\circ F_{[\overline{a},\overline{b}]},

\displaystyle 1\leq a\leq b\leq n,

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{[\overline{a},a]}

\displaystyle:=F_{[\overline{a},a]},

\displaystyle 1\leq a\leq n,

which correspond naturally to the type-
\mathsf{BC}
reversals (
2.3
).
For example the seven type-
\mathsf{BC}

simple star networks

F^{\prime}_{\emptyset}=F^{\prime}_{[1,1]}=F^{\prime}_{[2,2]}=F^{\prime}_{[3,3]}
and
F^{\prime}_{[1,2]},F^{\prime}_{[2,3]},F^{\prime}_{[1,3]},F^{\prime}_{\smash{[\overline{1},1]}},F^{\prime}_{\smash{[\overline{2},2]}},F^{\prime}_{\smash{[\overline{3},3]}}
,

(5.22)

correspond to the reversals

s^{\prime}_{\emptyset}
,
s^{\prime}_{[1,2]}
,
s^{\prime}_{[2,3]}
,
s^{\prime}_{[1,3]}
,
s^{\prime}_{[\overline{1},1]}
,
s^{\prime}_{[\overline{2},2]}
,

s^{\prime}_{[\overline{3},3]}
.
We refer to all iterations of concatenations and condensed concatenations
of type-
\mathsf{BC}
simple star networks as
type-
\mathsf{BC}
star networks
,
and let
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{BC}}([\overline{n},n])
denote the set of these having boundary
vertices indexed by
[\overline{n},n]
.
We will be interested in three subsets of these formed
by condensed concatenation of type-
\mathsf{BC}
simple star networks.

Definition 5.12
.

Define
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}([\overline{n},n])
to be
the set of all type-
\mathsf{A}
condensed star networks of the form

(5.23)

F=F^{\prime}_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}

and call these

type-
\mathsf{BC}
condensed star networks
(with boundary vertices indexed by
[\overline{n},n]
)
.

Definition 5.13
.

Call a type-
\mathsf{BC}
condensed star network
F
(
5.23
) a

type-
\mathsf{BC}
zig-zag network
if
the intervals
[c_{1},d_{1}],\dotsc,[c_{t},d_{t}]

satisfy the conditions of Definition
5.2
,
i.e., if the type-
\mathsf{A}
star network

F_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}

is a type-
\mathsf{A}
zig-zag network with boundary vertices indexed by

[\min\{1,c_{1},\dotsc,c_{t}\},n]
.
Let
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])

denote the set of type-
\mathsf{BC}
zig-zag networks with boundary vertices
labeled by
[\overline{n},n]
.

Definition 5.14
.

Call a type-
\mathsf{BC}
star network
F
(
5.23
) a

type-
\mathsf{BC}
descending star network
if
the intervals
[c_{1},d_{1}],\dotsc,[c_{t},d_{t}]

satisfy the conditions of Definition
5.3
,
i.e., if the type-
\mathsf{A}
star network

F_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}

is a type-
\mathsf{A}
descending star network with boundary vertices indexed by

[\min\{1,c_{1},\dotsc,c_{t}\},n]
.
Let
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])

denote the set of type-
\mathsf{BC}
descending star
networks with boundary vertices
labeled by
[\overline{n},n]
.

Thus we have
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n]),

and each zig-zag network of type
\mathsf{BC}

is
F_{w}
for some
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{[\overline{n},n]}
.
By the symmetry of these networks, we necessarily have
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

To illustrate, consider the set
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{3},3])

of twenty-two type-
\mathsf{BC}
zig-zag networks.
Fourteen of these
are type-
\mathsf{BC}
descending star networks

(5.24)

By Corollary
5.11
and the containment

\mathfrak{B}_{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
, we have that
for all
F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
and
v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
,
at most one
path family
\pi
of type
v

covers
F_{w}
.
For
i>0
, paths
\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{\overline{i}}
in this family
are necessarily mirror
images of one another, and we call
\pi_{i}

grounded
if it intersects path
\pi_{\overline{i}}
.
For example consider
F_{3\overline{1}2}
in (
5.25
)
and the path families

\pi
of type
123
and
\sigma
of type
3\overline{1}2
covering it,

(5.26)

\pi=\hbox to43.53pt{\vbox to68.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 16.07576pt\lower-34.6242pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

The path
\pi_{1}
is grounded while
\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{3}
are not;
the path
\sigma_{2}
is grounded while
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{3}
are not.

By (
5.21
) the intervals appearing in the construction
of the type-
\mathsf{BC}
star network
G
(
5.23
)
are roughly half of those that appear in the type-
\mathsf{A}
construction
of the same network. The subposet of
\preceq
induced by these intervals
satisfies the following.

Proposition 5.15
.

For
F^{\prime}_{\smash{[a_{1},b_{1}]}}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{\smash{[a_{t},b_{t}]}}

a type-
\mathsf{BC}
zig-zag network,
there is at most one interval
[a_{i},b_{i}]

satisfying
a_{i}<0
,
b_{i}=\overline{a_{i}}
.
Furthermore, this interval is maximal or minimal
(or both) in the poset
\preceq
on
\{[a_{1},b_{1}],\dotsc,[a_{t},b_{t}]\}
.

Proof.

Condition (1) of Definition
5.2
requires that the intervals

[a_{1},b_{1}],\dotsc,[a_{t},b_{t}]
be distinct and form a nonnesting set.
Thus at most one of these intervals satisfies
b_{i}=\overline{a_{i}}
.
Let
[a_{j},\overline{a_{j}}]
be such an interval (
a_{j}<0
) and suppose that
it is neither maximal nor minimal in the partial order
\preceq
.
Then there are indices
i,k
with
i<j<k
and

[a_{i},b_{i}]\cap[a_{j},\overline{a_{j}}]\neq\emptyset
,

[a_{k},b_{k}]\cap[a_{j},\overline{a_{j}}]\neq\emptyset
. By Condition (2)
of Definition
5.2
, we must have
a_{i}<a_{j}<a_{k}

or
a_{i}>a_{j}>a_{k}
. But this implies that
a_{i}<0
or
a_{k}<0
, and
therefore that
[a_{j},\overline{a_{j}}]
is properly contained in
[a_{i},\overline{a_{i}}]

or
[a_{k},\overline{a_{k}}]
, contradicting Condition (1).
∎

As a consequence, the cardinalities of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
and

\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])
are related to their type-
\mathsf{A}
analogs,
with the second cardinality equal to a Catalan number.

Theorem 5.16
.

For all
n
we have

(1)

|\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])|=|\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([1,n+1])|
,

(2)

|\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])|=|\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([1,n+1])|=\frac{1}{n+2}\tbinom{2n+2}{n+1}
.

Proof.

(1) Define a map

\Upsilon:\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([1,n+1])
by

\Upsilon(F^{\prime}_{[a_{i},b_{i}]})=F_{[\max\{a_{i}+1,1\},b_{i}+1]}
and

\Upsilon(F^{\prime}_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{[a_{t},b_{t}]})=\Upsilon(F^{\prime}_{[a_{1},b_{1}]})\bullet\cdots\bullet\Upsilon(F^{\prime}_{[a_{t},b_{t}]}),

so that all positive endpoints of intervals increase by one
and all negative endpoints are replaced by
1
.
To see that
\Upsilon
is well defined, recall
that by Proposition
5.15

at most one of the intervals
[a_{i},b_{i}]
satisfies
a_{i}<0
.
Thus the conditions of Definition
5.2
are satisfied
and
\Upsilon(F)
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([1,n+1])
.
Furthermore, for
F\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])
,
the inequalities
a_{1}>\cdots>a_{t}
imply that we have

a_{1}+1>\cdots>\max\{a_{t}+1,1\}
and

\Upsilon(F)\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([1,n+1])
.
To see that
\Upsilon
is bijective, observe that we have

\begin{gathered}\;[a_{i},b_{i}]\subseteq[1,n]\;\Longleftrightarrow\;[a_{i}+1,b_{i}+1]\subseteq[2,n+1],\\
a_{i}=b_{i}\in[1,n]\;\Longleftrightarrow\;a_{i}+1=b_{i}+1\in[2,n+1],\\
[\overline{b_{i}},b_{i}]\subseteq[\overline{n},n]\;\Longleftrightarrow\;[1,b_{i}+1]\subseteq[1,n+1].\end{gathered}

Finally, by
[
17
, Thm. 3.6]
we have

|\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([1,n])|=\tfrac{1}{n+1}\tbinom{2n}{n}
.
∎

In Theorems
5.18
–
5.19

we will characterize

\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])

as subsets of
\{F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\}

defined by
w
avoiding certain patterns.
In order to do so, we
decompose certain
elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
into pairs
(u,v)\in\mathfrak{B}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}
,
and certain
zig-zag networks in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])

into pairs of components in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{k},k])\times\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([1,n-k])
.
Define the map

(5.27)

\displaystyle\oplus:\mathfrak{B}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{[1,n-k]}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathfrak{B}_{n}

\displaystyle(u,v)

\displaystyle\mapsto u\oplus v=w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n}

by

w_{i}=\begin{cases}u_{i}&\text{if $i\in[\overline{k},k]$},\\
v_{i}+k&\text{if $i>k$},\\
\overline{v_{i}+k}&\text{if $i<\overline{k}$}.\end{cases}

For example,
the elements

u=\overline{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{1}
,
v=231\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}
give

u\oplus v=\overline{2}\overline{4}\overline{3}1\overline{1}342\in\mathfrak{B}_{4}
.
Observe that to make sense of the more general expression

u\oplus v^{(1)}\oplus\cdots\oplus v^{(p)}
,
we must interpret it as

(\cdots((u\oplus v^{(1)})\oplus v^{(2)})\oplus\cdots\oplus v^{(p-1)})\oplus v^{(p)}
, with
u\in\mathfrak{B}_{k}
,
v^{(i)}\in\mathfrak{S}_{[1,j_{i}]}

for some
k,j_{1},\dotsc,j_{p}
.
We will say that any element
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
which can be written as

w=u\oplus v
is
\oplus
-decomposable
.
Equivalently,
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is
\oplus
-decomposable if there is
some index
k
such that

\{|w_{1}|,\dotsc,|w_{k}|\}=[1,k],\qquad\{w_{k+1},\dotsc,w_{n}\}=[k+1,n].

We define a similar map

(5.28)

\displaystyle\oplus:\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}([\overline{k},k])\times\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}([1,n-k])

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}([\overline{n},n])

\displaystyle(E,F)

\displaystyle\mapsto E\oplus F

as follows.

(1)

Create
F^{+}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}([k+1,n])
by adding
k
to the
indices of all sources and sinks of
F
.

(2)

Create
F^{-}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}([\overline{n},\overline{k+1}])
by drawing
F^{+}
upside-down
and by multiplying each source and sink index by
-1
.

(3)

Vertically arrange the sources and sinks of these networks and
E

in order
(\overline{n},\dotsc,n)
, so that we have
F^{+}
above
E
above
F^{-}
.

For example, to construct
the network
F^{\prime}_{[\overline{1},1]}\oplus(F_{[2,3]}\bullet F_{[1,2]})
,
we place
F^{\prime}_{[\overline{1},1]}
between
two copies of
F_{[2,3]}\bullet F_{[1,2]}
,
one upside-down,
to obtain
