Lemma 5.17
.

For elements
u\in\mathfrak{B}_{k}
,
v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}

and zig-zag networks

F_{u}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{k},k])
,

F_{v}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([1,n-k])
,
we have the following.

(1)

F_{u}\oplus F_{v}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
is a zig-zag network
satisfying
w(F_{u}\oplus F_{v})=u\oplus v
.

(2)

If
F_{u}
and
F_{v}
are descending star networks, then so is

F_{u}\oplus F_{v}
.

Proof.

(1) To see that
F_{u}\oplus F_{v}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
, write

F_{u}=F^{\prime}_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{[c_{t},d_{t}]},\qquad F_{v}=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[a_{r},b_{r}]}.

By the definition (
5.28
) of the map
\oplus
we have

F_{u}\oplus F_{v}=F^{\prime}_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}\bullet F^{\prime}_{[a_{1}+k,b_{1}+k]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{[a_{r}+k,b_{r}+k]}.

It is easy to see that the set
\{[a_{1}+k,b_{1}+k],\dotsc,[a_{r}+k,b_{r}+k]\}

satisfies the conditions of Definition
5.2
,
and since each interval
[c_{i},d_{i}]
is disjoint
from each interval
[a_{j}+k,b_{j}+k]
, the union

\{[c_{1},d_{1}],\dotsc,[c_{t},d_{t}],[a_{1}+k,b_{1}+k],\dotsc,[a_{r}+k,b_{r}+k]\}

satisfies the conditions of Definition
5.2
as well.

Now let
w=w(F_{u}\oplus F_{v})\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
and let
y=u\oplus v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.
To see that
w=y
,
recall by Proposition
5.8

that
w
is the permutation in
\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}

which maximizes
\textsc{inv}(z)
over all
z\in\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}

for which there is a path family of type
z
covering
F_{u}\oplus F_{v}
.
By the disconectedness of
F_{u}\oplus F_{v}
, we have

\{w_{\overline{k}},\dotsc,w_{k}\}=[\overline{k},k],\qquad\{w_{k+1},\dotsc,w_{n}\}=[k+1,n],\qquad\{w_{\overline{n}},\dotsc,w_{\overline{k+1}}\}=[\overline{n},\overline{k+1}].

Since
w(F_{u})=u
, it is clear that
w
has as many inversions as possible
among entries
\overline{k},\dotsc,k
when

w_{\overline{k}}\cdots w_{k}=u_{\overline{k}}\cdots u_{k}
.
Similarly,

w
has as many inversions as possible
among entries
k+1,\dotsc,n
when

w_{k+1}\cdots w_{n}
matches the pattern
v_{1}\cdots v_{n-k}
, i.e.,
when
w_{k+i}=v_{i}+k
for
i=1,\dotsc,n-k
.
In this case,
we also have

w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{k+1}}=\overline{w_{n}}\cdots\overline{w_{k+1}}
.
Thus we have
w=u\oplus v
.

(2) The fact that
F_{u}\oplus F_{v}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])

follows immediately from Definition
5.14
.
∎

Now we may characterize
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])

in terms of pattern avoidance.

Theorem 5.18
.

Elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
correspond bijectively to

3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
. Specifically we have

(5.29)

\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])=\{F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\}.

Proof.

(
\subseteq
)
Consider
F\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])\subset\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
.
By
[
57
, §3]
,
F
has the form
F_{w}

for some
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, and factors as in
(
5.23
)
and Definition
5.2
.
By Proposition
5.15
,
at most one of the intervals
[c_{i},d_{i}]
appearing in
(
5.23
) satisfies
c_{i}=\overline{d_{i}}
.
If such an interval exists, then we may assume that it appears
first or last.
Thus we may factor
F
as

F^{\prime}_{[c_{0},d_{0}]}\bullet(F^{\prime}_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet F^{\prime}_{[\overline{d_{1}},\overline{c_{1}}]})\bullet\cdots\bullet(F^{\prime}_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}\bullet F^{\prime}_{[\overline{d_{t}},\overline{c_{t}}]})\bullet F^{\prime}_{[c_{t+1},d_{t+1}]},

with one or both of the intervals
[c_{0},d_{0}]
and
[c_{t+1},d_{t+1}]

satisfying
c_{i}=d_{i}
, and at most one of these satisfying
c_{i}=\overline{d_{i}}
.
Algorithm
5.6

then gives a reversal factorization of
w
.
This factorization consists of the subsequence of reversals

(5.30)

(s_{[c_{0},d_{0}]},s_{[c_{1},d_{1}]},s_{[\overline{d_{1}},\overline{c_{1}}]},\dotsc,s_{[c_{t},d_{t}]},s_{[\overline{d_{t}},\overline{c_{t}}]},s_{[\overline{d_{t+1}},\overline{c_{t+1}}]}),

and more pairs of reversals

(5.31)

s_{[c_{i},d_{i}]\cap[c_{j},d_{j}]},\quad s_{[\overline{d_{i}},\overline{c_{i}}]\cap[\overline{d_{j}},\overline{c_{j}}]}

inserted between these.
Since the only intervals appearing in
(
5.30
) – (
5.31
)
which can contain both positive and negative integers
are
[c_{0},d_{0}]
,
[c_{t+1},d_{t+1}]
,
we may reorder the sequence of reversals
to place each pair
s_{[a,b]}
and
s_{[\overline{b},\overline{a}]}
consecutively.
Thus
w
equals a product of type-
\mathsf{BC}
reversals of the
forms
s_{[c_{0},d_{0}]}
,
s^{\prime}_{[a,b]}=s_{[a,b]}s_{[\overline{b},\overline{a}]}
,

s_{[c_{t+1},d_{t+1}]}
, and belongs to
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

(
\supseteq
)
We claim that for each element
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
we have
F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
.
This is true when
n=1
because

\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{1},1])=\{F_{\emptyset},F_{[\overline{1},1]}\}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{1},1])
.
Now suppose that the
statement is true
for
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{1},\dotsc,\mathfrak{B}_{n-1}
and consider
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

If
w
is
\oplus
-decomposable then we can write

w=u\oplus v
for
u\in\mathfrak{B}_{k}
,
v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}
, and

1\leq k<n
.
Then by induction we have
F_{u}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{k},k])
and

F_{v}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([1,n-k])
.
By Lemma
5.17
the network
F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])

satisfies

F_{w}=F_{u}\oplus F_{v}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
.

If

w
is not
\oplus
-decomposable,
then we may apply
[
57
, Obs. 3.3]
to find a zig-zag factorization
of
w
(as in the paragraph following (
5.13
))
and to obtain an expression (
5.9
)
for
F_{w}
which satisfies the conditions of Definition
5.2
.
In particular, we compare the lengths
\ell
,
m

of the longest decreasing prefixes of
w
and
w^{-1}
respectively,

w_{\smash{\overline{n}}}>\cdots>w_{\smash{\overline{n-\ell+1}}},\qquad(w^{-1})_{\smash{\overline{n}}}>\cdots>(w^{-1})_{\smash{\overline{n-m+1}}}.

If
\ell=m
, then
w=s_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
and
F_{w}
is the type-
\mathsf{BC}

zig-zag network
F_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
.
If
\ell<m
, then
w
has a type-
\mathsf{A}
zig-zag factorization beginning
with

s_{\smash{[\overline{n},\overline{n-\ell+1}]}}s_{\smash{[\overline{n-m},\overline{n-\ell+1}]}}s_{\smash{[\overline{n-m},k]}}

for some
k>\overline{n-m}
,
and the interval
[\overline{n},\overline{n-\ell+1}]
is
\prec
-minimal.
By the
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
-skew-symmetry of
w
,
it also has a type-
\mathsf{A}
zig-zag factorization beginning with

s_{[n-\ell+1,n]}s_{[n-\ell+1,n-m]}s_{\smash{[\overline{k},n-m]}},

and the interval
[n-\ell+1,n]
is also
\prec
-minimal.
In other words, we can write
w=s^{\prime}_{\smash{[n-\ell+1,n]}}w^{\prime}

for some
w^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
satisfying

w^{\prime}_{i}=i
for
i=\overline{n},\dotsc,\overline{n-m+1},n-m+1,\dotsc,n
, i.e.,

w^{\prime}=w^{\prime}_{\smash{\overline{n-m}}}\cdots w^{\prime}_{n-m}\oplus 1\cdots m.

It follows that we have
F_{w}\cong F^{\prime}_{\smash{[n-\ell+1,n]}}\bullet F_{w^{\prime}}
.
By induction
F_{w^{\prime}}
is a type-
\mathsf{BC}
zig-zag network, and so is
F_{w}
.
∎

Theorem 5.19
.

Elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])
correspond bijectively to
elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2
.
Specifically we have

(5.32)

\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])=\{F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\text{ avoids the signed patterns $1\overline{2}$, $\overline{2}1$, $\overline{2}\overline{1}$, $312$, $3\overline{1}2${}}\}.

Proof.

First we observe that by Lemma
2.3
,
avoidance of the signed patterns

1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2
implies
avoidance of the unsigned patterns
3412
and
4231
.
Thus the right-hand side of (
5.32
)
includes one zig-zag network
F_{w}
for every element
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}

avoiding the five signed patterns.
Next, consider the subset of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])

consisting of networks
F_{w}
factoring as

(5.33)

F^{\prime}_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}

with
c_{1}>\cdots>c_{t}>0
.
By Proposition
5.5
,
these networks are precisely

\{F_{u}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])\,|\,u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n},u_{1}\cdots u_{n}\text{ nonnegative and avoiding the pattern }312\}.

Therefore we may prove the proposition
by proving (
5.32
),
restricting our attention
on the right-hand-side
to networks
F_{u}

with
u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
having
at least one negative letter in the subword
u_{1}\cdots u_{n}
, and
on the left-hand-side to networks
F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])

factoring as
(
5.33
)
with
c_{1}>\cdots>c_{t}=\overline{d_{t}}
.
Such networks
F_{w}
correspond bijectively to networks

F_{v}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([c_{t},n])

factoring as

(5.34)

F_{v}:=F_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[c_{t},d_{t}]},

with
v
and
w
satisfying

(5.35)

\begin{gathered}v_{\overline{d_{t}}}\cdots v_{\overline{2}}v_{\overline{1}}=d_{t}\cdots 21,\qquad v_{1}\cdots v_{n}=w_{1}\cdots w_{n},\\
\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{n}\}=\{\overline{1},\dotsc,\overline{d_{t}},d_{t}+1,\dotsc,n\}.\end{gathered}

(
\subseteq
)
Consider

F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])\subset\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])

factoring as
(
5.33
)
with
c_{1}>\cdots>c_{t}=\overline{d_{t}}
.
Since the related network
F_{v}
(
5.34
)
belongs to

\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([c_{t},n])
,
the element
v=v_{c_{t}}\cdots v_{\overline{1}}v_{1}\cdots v_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{[c_{t},n]}

avoids the ordinary pattern
312
.
Thus
v_{1}\cdots v_{n}=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}

avoids the signed patterns
312
and
3\overline{1}2
.
By
[
57
, Obs. 3.2]

we have that

\overline{1}\cdots\overline{d_{t}}
is a subword of
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
Thus
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
contains a negative letter and also
avoids the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
.

(
\supseteq
) Consider
F_{w}
on the right-hand side of
(
5.32
) with
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
containing a negative
letter.
By (
5.29
)
we have

F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
.
By Proposition
5.15
,
there exists a factorization
(
5.33
) of
F_{w}

in which exactly one interval
[c_{i},d_{i}]
satisfies
c_{i}=\overline{d_{i}}
,
and this interval must be maximal or minimal (or both)
in the partial order
\preceq
.

Assume that this interval is minimal and that
i=1
.
By
[
57
, Obs. 3.2]

we have

(5.36)

w_{\overline{d_{i}}}>\cdots w_{\overline{1}}>w_{1}>\cdots>w_{d_{i}},

with
w_{\overline{1}}>0>w_{1}
since
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.
If some positive letter
j\leq d_{i}
does not appear in these positions,
then
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
contains either
the subword
w_{d_{i}}j
which matches the pattern
\overline{2}1
,
or the subword
w_{d_{i}}\overline{j}
which matches the pattern
\overline{2}\overline{1}
.
This contradicts our choice of
F_{w}
.
Thus
the
2d_{i}
letters in these positions
must be

d_{i}>\cdots>1>\overline{1}>\cdots>\overline{d_{i}}
,
and the interval
[\overline{d_{i}},d_{i}]
is both minimal and maximal.
It follows that we have
w=s_{[\overline{d_{i}},d_{i}]}\oplus u
for some

u\in\mathfrak{S}_{[d_{i}+1,n]}
.
Since
w
avoids the signed pattern
312
, we have that

u
avoids the ordinary pattern
312
,

F_{u}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([1,n-d_{i}])
,
and
F_{w}=F^{\prime}_{[\overline{d_{i}},d_{i}]}\oplus F_{u}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])
.

Now assume that the interval
[c_{i},d_{i}]=[\overline{d_{i}},d_{i}]

in the factorization (
5.33
) of
F_{w}

is maximal with
i=t
.
Define
F_{v}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([c_{t},n])
as in (
5.34
).
We claim that

v_{c_{t}}\cdots v_{\overline{1}}v_{1}\cdots v_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{[c_{t},n]}

avoids the ordinary pattern
312
.
To obtain a contradiction,
assume that some subword

v_{j_{1}}v_{j_{2}}v_{j_{3}}

matches the ordinary pattern
312
.
Suppose first that
j_{1}\geq 1
. Then

w_{j_{1}}w_{j_{2}}w_{j_{3}}

matches one of the signed patterns
312
,
3\overline{1}2
,
3\overline{2}\overline{1}
,

\overline{1}\overline{3}\overline{2}
and this contradicts the containment of
F_{w}
on the
right-hand side of (
5.32
).
Now suppose that
j_{1}\leq\overline{1}
and
j_{2}\geq 1
.
Then the letter
v_{j_{1}}
is positive by (
5.35
),
and letters
\overline{1},\dotsc,\overline{v_{j_{1}}}
appear in

v_{1}\cdots v_{n}=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
Since
w
avoids the signed pattern
\overline{2}\overline{1}
, it is
impossible for
letters
v_{j_{2}}v_{j_{3}}
in
v_{1}\cdots v_{n}

to complete the ordinary pattern
312
.
Now suppose that
j_{1}<j_{2}\leq\overline{1}
.
Since
v_{j_{1}}
and
v_{j_{2}}
are both positive,
all of the letters
v_{j_{2}}+1,\dotsc,v_{j_{1}}-1
appear between
these two letters, none can complete the pattern
312
.
Thus no subword of
v_{c_{t}}\cdots v_{\overline{1}}v_{1}\cdots v_{n}
matches
the pattern
312
, and

F_{w}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])
.
∎

It is easy to see that the list of signed patterns in
Theorem
5.19
can not be shortened.
For
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
,
failure to avoid the signed pattern
1\overline{2}
or
\overline{2}\overline{1}

implies failure to avoid the ordinary pattern
3412
or
4231
,
which implies that
F_{w}
is not a type-
\mathsf{BC}
zig-zag network.
Furthermore, inspection of
F_{\overline{2}1}

(
F_{\overline{2}13}
with highest and lowest edges removed),

F_{312}
, and
F_{\smash{3\overline{1}2}}
in (
5.25
)
shows that these are not type-
\mathsf{BC}
descending star networks.

Since any element
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231

can be viewed as a permutation in
\mathfrak{S}_{[\overline{n},n]}

and any zig-zag network in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])

can be viewed as a zig-zag network in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
,
the bijection
F\mapsto w(F)
guaranteed by
Theorems
5.18
–
5.19

can be realized by Algorithm
5.6
.
The inverse
w\mapsto F_{w}

of the map can be realized as in
[
57
, §3]
,
or as follows in the special case that
w
avoids the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2
.

Algorithm 5.20
.

Given
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2
, do

(1)

Set

v=\begin{cases}|h|\cdots 1w_{1}\cdots w_{n}&\text{if $w_{1}\dotsc w_{n}$ contains negative letters $h\cdots\overline{1}$,}\\
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}&\text{if $w_{1}\cdots w_{n}$ contains no negative letters}.\end{cases}

(2)

Apply Algorithm
5.7
to
v
to obtain

F_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}
.

(3)

Set
F_{w}=F^{\prime}_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}
.

Theorems
5.18

and
5.19
suggest defining
type-
\mathsf{BC}
analogs of path families and graphical representation
(
5.19
), (
5.20
).
Given
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{BC}}([\overline{n},n])
,
and

\pi=(\pi_{\overline{n}},\dotsc,\pi_{\overline{1}},\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{\overline{n}})

covering
F
, call
\pi
a
\mathsf{BC}
-path family
if
for each factor
F^{\prime}_{[a,b]}
of
F

and each index
i\in[1,n]
, there exist indices
j
,
k
, such that
paths
\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{\overline{i}}
enter
F^{\prime}_{[a,b]}
via sources
j
,
\overline{j}

and exit
F^{\prime}_{[a,b]}
via sinks
k
,
\overline{k}
, respectively.
In other words,
\pi_{\overline{i}}
must be a reflection of
\pi_{i}
.
For
F\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{BC}}([\overline{n},n])
and
u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, define the sets

(5.37)

\begin{gathered}\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)=\{\pi\,|\,\pi\text{ a $\mathsf{BC}$-path family covering }F\},\\
\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F)=\{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)\,|\,\mathrm{type}(\pi)=u\}.\end{gathered}

For example, the two path families in (
5.26
)
belong to

\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{3\overline{1}2})
with

\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{e}(F_{3\overline{1}2})
,
\sigma\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{3\overline{1}2}(F_{3\overline{1}2})
.
On the other hand,
the path family

(5.38)

\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle{\overline{2}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{1}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{2}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{1}}

is not a
\mathsf{BC}
-path family,
even though it has type
e\in\mathfrak{B}_{2}
.

The set
\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)
associates elements of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]
and
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)

to
F
. Specifically, we say that

F

graphically represents

(5.39)

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)}\negthickspace\negthickspace\mathrm{type}(\pi)

as an element of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]
.
To describe the corresponding element of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
we first extend
the definition of
type-
\mathsf{A}
defects
from
Subsection
5.1
(and

[
13
]
,

[
18
]
,

[
23
]
).
Assume that
F

is formed by some iteration
of ordinary or condensed concatenatation
of simple star networks
F^{\prime}_{[c_{1},d_{1}]},\dotsc,F^{\prime}_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}
.
Each factor
F^{\prime}_{[c_{k},d_{k}]}
contributes a single internal vertex
if
c_{k}=\overline{d_{k}}
, and two such vertices otherwise.
Given a
\mathsf{BC}
-path family
\pi
covering
F
,
define a
type-
\mathsf{BC}
defect
of
\pi
to be
a triple
(\pi_{i},\pi_{j},k)
with

(1)

|i|\leq j
,

(2)

\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{j}
meet at one of the internal vertices of

F^{\prime}_{[c_{k},d_{k}]}
after having crossed an odd number of times.

Let
\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)
denote the number of type-
\mathsf{BC}
defects of
\pi
.
For example, consider the star network
and path family

(5.40)

The defects of
\pi
are

(\pi_{\overline{1}},\pi_{1},2)
,

(\pi_{\overline{1}},\pi_{2},3)
,

(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},4)
,

(\pi_{\overline{2}},\pi_{2},4)
,
and we have
\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)=4
.
We say that
F

graphically represents

(5.41)

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F)}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\mathrm{dfct}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\pi)}T_{\mathrm{type}(\pi)}

as an element of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
.
Specializing at
q=1
, we see that if
F
graphically represents

D(q)
as an element of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
, then
it graphically represents

D(1)
as an element of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]
.

In the special case that
F=F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
,
it graphically represents a Kazhdan–Lusztig basis element.

Theorem 5.21
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, the zig-zag network
F_{w}
represents

\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
as an element of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
.

Proof.

By
[
57
, Lem. 5.3]
, we have that for all
u\in\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
,
there exists exactly one path family of type
u
covering
F_{w}

if
u\leq_{\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}}w
and no such path family otherwise.
In particular, this is true for
u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}
.
But by Proposition
2.1

we have
u\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w
if and only if
u\leq_{\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}}w
.
Since
w
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
, the network
F_{w}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])
,
and every path family

\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{w})
satisfies
\mathrm{dfct}(\pi)=0
. Thus the sum
(
5.41
) becomes

\sum_{u\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w}\negthickspace T_{u},

which by (
3.3
) is
\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
.
∎

Corollary 5.22
.

For
v,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
with
w
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
the number of
\mathsf{BC}
-path families of type
v
covering
F_{w}
is

1
if
v\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w
, and is
0
otherwise.

6.
Immanants and total nonnegativity

In order to use Section
5
to produce partial solutions
to Problem
1.1
for the subsets
(
5.1
) – (
5.4
)
of the Kazhdan–Lusztig bases,
we rely heavily upon methods borrowed from the study of total nonnegativity
and upon trace generating functions
in a ring
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{x}]

where
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{i,j})_{i,j\in[\overline{n},n]}
is viewed
as the
2n\times 2n
matrix

(6.1)

\mathbf{x}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{n},\overline{n}}&\cdots&\mathbf{x}_{\overline{n},\overline{1}}&\mathbf{x}_{\overline{n},1}&\cdots&\mathbf{x}_{\overline{n},n}\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\mathbf{x}_{\overline{1},\overline{n}}&\cdots&\mathbf{x}_{\overline{1},\overline{1}}&\mathbf{x}_{\overline{1},1}&\cdots&\mathbf{x}_{\overline{1},n}\\
\mathbf{x}_{1,\overline{n}}&\cdots&\mathbf{x}_{1,\overline{1}}&\mathbf{x}_{1,1}&\cdots&\mathbf{x}_{1,n}\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\mathbf{x}_{n,\overline{n}}&\cdots&\mathbf{x}_{n,\overline{1}}&\mathbf{x}_{n,1}&\cdots&\mathbf{x}_{n,n}\end{bmatrix}.

For subsets
I,J\subseteq[\overline{n},n]
we define the submatrix

\mathbf{x}_{I,J}:=(\mathbf{x}_{i,j})_{i\in I,j\in J}
.
To economize notation, we abbreviate

(6.2)

[n]:=[1,n].

Thus
\mathbf{x}_{[n],[n]}
denotes the submatrix of positively indexed entries of

\mathbf{x}
.
Given polynomial

p(\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{x}]
,
and
2n\times 2n
matrix
A=(a_{i,j})_{i,j\in[\overline{n},n]}
, we define

p(A)
to be the expression obtained by evaluating
p(\mathbf{x})
at

\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=a_{i,j}
, for all
i,j\in[\overline{n},n]
.

6.1.
Type-
\mathsf{A}
immanants

For certain
\theta\in\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{S}_{n})

and for all
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
combinatorial formulas for
\theta(\widetilde{C}_{w}(1))

depend upon generating functions
which are polynomials in entries of the submatrix
\mathbf{x}_{[n],[n]}
of
\mathbf{x}
.
Following Littlewood
[
47
]
and Stanley
[
62
]
,
we define the
(type-
\mathsf{A}
)
\theta
-immanant
to be

(6.3)

\mathrm{Imm}_{\theta}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(\mathbf{x}_{[n],[n]}):=\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\theta(w)\mathbf{x}_{1,w_{1}}\negthinspace\cdots\mathbf{x}_{n,w_{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{x}_{1,1},\mathbf{x}_{1,2},\dotsc,\mathbf{x}_{n,n}].

When
\theta
is an induced one-dimensional character

\eta^{\lambda}
or

\epsilon^{\lambda}
with
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{r})\vdash n
,
we may neatly express its corresponding immanant in terms of
permanents or determinants, and

ordered set partitions of type
\lambda
,
i.e., sequences
(J_{1},\dotsc,J_{r})
of subsets of
[n]
with

(1)

J_{1}\uplus\cdots\uplus J_{r}=[n]
,

(2)

|J_{i}|=\lambda_{i}
for
i=1,\dotsc,r
.

In particular,
we have the Littlewood–Merris–Watkins
identities
[
47
]
,
[
49
]
,

(6.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Imm}_{\epsilon^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(\mathbf{x}_{[n],[n]})=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{(J_{1},\dotsc,J_{r})}\negthickspace\negthickspace\det(\mathbf{x}_{J_{1},J_{1}})\cdots\det(\mathbf{x}_{J_{r},J_{r}}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Imm}_{\eta^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(\mathbf{x}_{[n],[n]})=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{(J_{1},\dotsc,J_{r})}\negthickspace\negthickspace\mathrm{per}(\mathbf{x}_{J_{1},J_{1}})\cdots\mathrm{per}(\mathbf{x}_{J_{r},J_{r}}),

where the sums are over ordered set partitions
(J_{1},\dotsc,J_{r})
of
[n]

of type
\lambda
.
(See
[
41
, Thm. 2.1]
for a
q
-analog.)
We also have

(6.5)

\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(\mathbf{x}_{[n],[n]})=z_{\lambda}\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\\
\mathrm{ctype}(w)=\lambda\end{subarray}}\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\mathbf{x}_{1,w_{1}}\negthinspace\cdots\mathbf{x}_{n,w_{n}},

where
z_{\lambda}
is defined as in (
2.4
).
(See
[
30
]
for work on
\mathrm{Imm}_{\chi^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(\mathbf{x}_{[n],[n]})
.)

Immanants and trace evaluations of the form
\theta(\widetilde{C}_{w}(1))
are connected
by the following identity
[
17
, Eqn. (3.5)]
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Fix
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
with corresponding zig-zag network
F_{w}

having path matrix
A=A(w)
. Then for any linear functional

\theta:\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{S}_{n}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
we have

(6.6)

\theta(\widetilde{C}_{w}(1))=\mathrm{Imm}_{\theta}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A),

where
\mathrm{Imm}_{\theta}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A)
should be interpreted as

\smash{\mathrm{Imm}_{\theta}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(\mathbf{x}_{[n],[n]})}

evaluated at
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=a_{i,j}
.

Thus each combinatorial interpretation of
\smash{\mathrm{Imm}_{\theta}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A)}

yields a combinatorial interpretation of
\theta(\widetilde{C}_{w}(1))
.
To produce such combinatorial interpretations, we appeal
to methods of total nonnegativity, namely,
Lindstrom’s Lemma
[
38
]
,
[
46
]

and some simple extensions.

Proposition 6.2
.

Fix
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
with
corresponding zig-zag network
F_{w}
having path matrix
A=A(w)
.
We have

(6.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Imm}_{\epsilon^{n}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A)=\det(A)=\#\{\pi\in\Pi_{e}(F_{w})\,|\,\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{n}\text{ pairwise nonintersecting\,}\},

(6.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Imm}_{\eta^{n}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A)=\mathrm{per}(A)=\#\Pi(F_{w}),

(6.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{n}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A)=n\cdot\#\{\pi\in\Pi_{u}(F_{w})\,|\,u\in\mathfrak{S}_{n},\mathrm{ctype}(u)=n\}.

Proposition
6.2

implies simple interpretations of

\mathrm{Imm}_{\epsilon^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A)
,

\mathrm{Imm}_{\eta^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A)
,

\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A)

as well, for
\lambda\vdash n
arbitrary.
We will return to these in Subection
9.1
.
For
q
-analogs, see
[
17
]
.

6.2.
Type-
\mathsf{BC}
immanants

To create a generating function for
\theta\in\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{B}_{n})
,
we define
the
(type-
\mathsf{BC}
)
\theta
-immanant
to be

(6.10)

\mathrm{Imm}_{\theta}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}\theta(w)\mathbf{x}_{\smash{\overline{n},w_{\overline{n}}}}\negthinspace\cdots\mathbf{x}_{\smash{\overline{1},w_{\overline{1}}}}\mathbf{x}_{1,w_{1}}\negthinspace\cdots\mathbf{x}_{n,w_{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{x}].

This is a special case of the
wreath product immanant

defined in
[
59
, Eqns. (26)–(27)]
, and generalizes the
Littlewood – Stanley immanant (
6.3
).
When
\theta
is an induced character of the form

(\zeta\otimes\delta\xi)\big\uparrow\negthickspace\negthickspace\phantom{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{B}_{m}\times\mathfrak{B}_{n-m}}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}

for symmetric group characters
\zeta
,
\xi
,
as in (
4.3
) with
q=1
,
then
we may neatly express its corresponding immanant
in terms of type-
\mathsf{A}
immanants
and
n\times n
matrices

\mathbf{x}^{+}=(\mathbf{x}^{+}_{i,j})_{i,j\in[n]}
,

\mathbf{x}^{-}=(\mathbf{x}^{-}_{i,j})_{i,j\in[n]}
defined in terms of
the
2n\times 2n
matrix
\mathbf{x}
(
6.1
) by

(6.11)

\mathbf{x}^{+}_{i,j}=\mathbf{x}_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{i},\overline{j}}+\mathbf{x}_{i,\overline{j}}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{i},j},\qquad\mathbf{x}^{-}_{i,j}=\mathbf{x}_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{i},\overline{j}}-\mathbf{x}_{i,\overline{j}}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{i},j}.

For
I,J\subseteq[n]
, we let

\mathbf{x}^{+}_{I,J}:=(\mathbf{x}^{+})_{I,J}

and
\mathbf{x}^{-}_{I,J}:=(\mathbf{x}^{-})_{I,J}
denote the
I,J
submatrices of these.
For example,

\mathbf{x}^{+}_{12,14}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}_{1,1}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{1},\overline{1}}+\mathbf{x}_{1,\overline{1}}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{1},1}&\mathbf{x}_{1,4}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{1},\overline{4}}+\mathbf{x}_{1,\overline{4}}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{1},4}\\
\mathbf{x}_{2,1}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{2},\overline{1}}+\mathbf{x}_{2,\overline{1}}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{2},1}&\mathbf{x}_{2,4}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{2},\overline{4}}+\mathbf{x}_{2,\overline{4}}\mathbf{x}_{\overline{2},4}\end{bmatrix}.

By
[
59
, Thm. 3.1]
we have for bipartitions

(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n

with
|\lambda|=k
that

(6.12)

\mathrm{Imm}_{(\epsilon\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{I}\mathrm{Imm}_{\epsilon^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}}(\mathbf{x}^{+}_{I,I})\mathrm{Imm}_{\epsilon^{\mu}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}}}(\mathbf{x}^{-}_{\smash{[n]\smallsetminus I,[n]\smallsetminus I}}),

where the
sum is over all
m
-element subsets
I
of
[n]
.
More generally we have the following formula,
which is a type-
\mathsf{BC}
analog of

[
65
, Prop. 2.4]
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Given symmetric group traces
\zeta\in\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{S}_{k})
,

\xi\in\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{S}_{n-k})
,
and hyperoctahedral group trace
\theta\in\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{B}_{n})
satisfying

\theta=(\zeta\otimes\delta\xi)\big\uparrow\negthickspace\negthickspace\phantom{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{B}_{k,n-k}}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}
,
we have

(6.13)

\mathrm{Imm}_{\theta}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[n]\\
|I|=k\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Imm}_{\zeta}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}}(\mathbf{x}_{I,I}^{+})\mathrm{Imm}_{\xi}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}}}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]\smallsetminus I,[n]\smallsetminus I}^{-}).

Proof.

Expand
\zeta
,
\xi
in the induced sign character bases
of
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{S}_{k})
,
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{S}_{n-k})
as

\zeta=\sum_{\lambda\vdash k}a_{\lambda}\epsilon^{\lambda},\qquad\xi=\sum_{\mu\vdash n-k}b_{\mu}\epsilon^{\mu}.

Then
we have

\theta=\Big(\sum_{\lambda\vdash k}a_{\lambda}\epsilon^{\lambda}\otimes\delta\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\mu\vdash n-k}\negthickspace\negthickspace b_{\mu}\epsilon^{\mu}\Big)\big\uparrow\negthickspace\negthickspace\phantom{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{B}_{k,n-k}}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash k\\
\mu\vdash n-k\end{subarray}}\negthickspace\negthickspace a_{\lambda}b_{\mu}(\epsilon^{\lambda}\otimes\delta\epsilon^{\mu})\big\uparrow\negthickspace\negthickspace\phantom{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{B}_{k,n-k}}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash k\\
\mu\vdash n-k\end{subarray}}\negthickspace\negthickspace a_{\lambda}b_{\mu}(\epsilon\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu},

and the left-hand side of (
6.13
) is

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash k\\
\mu\vdash n-k\end{subarray}}\negthickspace\negthickspace a_{\lambda}b_{\mu}\mathrm{Imm}_{(\epsilon\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(\mathbf{x}).

But by (
6.12
), this is

\smash{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash k\\
\mu\vdash n-k\end{subarray}}\negthickspace\negthickspace a_{\lambda}b_{\mu}\negthickspace\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[n]\\
|I|=k\end{subarray}}\negthickspace\mathrm{Imm}_{\epsilon^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}}(\mathbf{x}^{+}_{I,I})\,\mathrm{Imm}_{\epsilon^{\mu}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}}}(\mathbf{x}^{-}_{[n]\smallsetminus I,[n]\smallsetminus I})}\\
=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[n]\\
|I|=k\end{subarray}}^{\phantom{a}}\sum_{\lambda\vdash k}a_{\lambda}\mathrm{Imm}_{\epsilon^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}}(\mathbf{x}^{+}_{I,I})\negthickspace\sum_{\mu\vdash n-k}\negthickspace\negthickspace b_{\mu}\mathrm{Imm}_{\epsilon^{\mu}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}}}(\mathbf{x}^{-}_{[n]\smallsetminus I,[n]\smallsetminus I}),

which is the right-hand side of (
6.13
).
∎

Evaluating the immanants (
6.10
) at path matrices of
type-
\mathsf{BC}
zig-zag networks

\{F_{w}\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\text{ avoids the patterns $3412$ and $4231${}}\}

gives the following type-
\mathsf{BC}
analog of (
6.6
) which allows
us to use type-
\mathsf{BC}
immanants to compute trace evaluations of the
form
\theta(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoid the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
and let zig-zag network
F_{w}
have path matrix
A
.
Then for any linear functional
\theta:\mathfrak{B}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
we have

(6.14)

\theta(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))=\mathrm{Imm}_{\theta}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(A).

Proof.

The right-hand side of (
6.14
) is

(6.15)

\sum_{v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}\theta(v)a_{\overline{n},v_{\overline{n}}}\cdots a_{\overline{1},v_{\overline{1}}}a_{1,v_{1}}\cdots a_{n,v_{n}}.

Since
F_{w}
is a type-
\mathsf{BC}
zig-zag network of order
2n
,
it is also a type-
\mathsf{A}
zig-zag network of order
2n
.
By
[
57
, Lem. 5.3]
, the product

a_{\overline{n},v_{\overline{n}}}\cdots a_{\overline{1},v_{\overline{1}}}a_{1,v_{1}}\cdots a_{n,v_{n}}

is
1
when
v\leq_{\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}}w
and is
0
otherwise.
Thus by Proposition
2.1
it
is
1
when
v\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w
and is
0
otherwise,
and the sum (
6.15
) is

\sum_{v\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w}\negthickspace\negthickspace\theta(v)=\theta\Big(\negthickspace\sum_{v\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w\negthickspace\negthickspace}\negthickspace v\Big)=\theta(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)).

∎

Thus each combinatorial interpretation of
\mathrm{Imm}_{\theta}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(A)

yields a combinatorial interpretation of
\theta(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))
.
Taking the special cases of Lemma
6.3
corresponding to

\zeta
,
\xi
equal to
\mathrm{triv}
,
\mathrm{sgn}
, or
\psi^{n}

and evaluating

\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-immanants at
A^{+}
and
A^{-}
,
we have the following type-
\mathsf{BC}
analogs of the sets of path families
appearing in Proposition
6.2
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Fix
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
let
F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])

have path matrix
A
,
and define
A^{+}
,
A^{-}
as in (
6.11
).
We have

(6.16)

\mathrm{per}(A^{+})=\#\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{w}),

(6.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{per}(A^{-})

\displaystyle=\#\{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{w})\,|\,\pi_{i},\pi_{j}\text{ may share a vertex only if $i,j<0$ or $i,j>0$}\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\#\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{w})&\text{if $\ell_{t}(w)=0$},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(6.18)

\displaystyle\det(A^{+})

\displaystyle=\#\{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{w})\,|\,\pi_{i},\pi_{j}\text{ may share a vertex only if }-1\leq i,j\leq 1\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2^{\ell(w)}&\text{if $w\leq t$},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(6.19)

\displaystyle\det(A^{-})

\displaystyle=\#\{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{w})\,|\,\pi_{i},\pi_{j}\text{ are vertex-disjoint for all }i\neq j\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $w=e$},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(6.20)

\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{n}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A^{+})=n\cdot\#\{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})\,|\,u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathrm{ctype}(\varphi(u))=n\},

(6.21)

\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{n}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A^{-})=\begin{cases}n\cdot\#\{\pi\in\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})\,|\,u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathrm{ctype}(\varphi(u))=n\},&\text{if $\ell_{t}(w)=0$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Observe that for
v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
we have

\begin{gathered}a^{+}_{1,v_{1}}\negthinspace\cdots a^{+}_{n,v_{n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\\
\varphi(u)=v\end{subarray}}a_{\smash{\overline{n},u_{\overline{n}}}}\negthinspace\cdots a_{\smash{\overline{1},u_{\overline{1}}}}a_{1,u_{1}}\negthinspace\cdots a_{n,u_{n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\\
\varphi(u)=v\end{subarray}}|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})|,\\
a^{-}_{1,v_{1}}\negthinspace\cdots a^{-}_{n,v_{n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\\
\varphi(u)=v\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ell_{t}(u)}a_{\smash{\overline{n},u_{\overline{n}}}}\negthinspace\cdots a_{\smash{\overline{1},u_{\overline{1}}}}a_{1,u_{1}}\negthinspace\cdots a_{n,u_{n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\\
\varphi(u)=v\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ell_{t}(u)}|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})|,\end{gathered}

where
\varphi
is defined as in (
2.5
).
Thus we have

(6.22)

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{per}(A^{+})=\sum_{u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})|,\qquad\mathrm{per}(A^{-})=\sum_{u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(-1)^{\ell_{t}(u)}|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})|,\\
\det(A^{+})=\sum_{u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(-1)^{\ell_{s}(u)}|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})|,\qquad\det(A^{-})=\sum_{u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(-1)^{\ell(u)}|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})|,\\
\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{n}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A^{+})=\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\\
\mathrm{ctype}(\varphi(u))=n\end{subarray}}\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace n|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})|,\qquad\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{n}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A^{-})=\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\\
\mathrm{ctype}(\varphi(u))=n\end{subarray}}\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace\negthickspace(-1)^{\ell_{t}(u)}n|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})|.\end{gathered}

By Corollary
5.22
the cardinality

|\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}_{u}(F_{w})|
is
1
if
u\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w

and is
0
otherwise.

The interpretations (
6.16
), (
6.20
) follow
from the subtraction-free expressions for
\mathrm{per}(A^{+})

and
\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{n}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A^{+})
in (
6.22
).

Now consider the interpretations (
6.17
), (
6.21
).
If
\ell_{t}(w)=0
,
then all elements

u\leq_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}w
also satisfy
\ell_{t}(u)=0
.
Thus the expressions
for
\mathrm{per}(A^{-})
and
\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{n}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A^{-})
in
(
6.22
)
are subtraction-free and have the claimed interpretations.
Furthermore, since there is no path in
F_{w}

from source
1
to sink
\overline{1}
(or source
\overline{1}
to sink
1
),
in any path family
\pi
covering
F_{w}

paths
\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{j}
cannot intersect unless
i,j<0
or
i,j>0
.
On the other hand if

\ell_{t}(w)\neq 0
,
then
F_{w}
has a factorization of the form
(
5.21
)
which begins or ends with

F^{\prime}_{\smash{[\overline{k},k]}}

for some
k
.
If the factorization begins with

F^{\prime}_{\smash{[\overline{k},k]}}
,
define an involution on
\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{w})
by

\pi\mapsto\pi^{\prime}
where
\pi^{\prime}
is obtained from
\pi
by swapping
paths
\pi_{1}

and
\pi_{\overline{1}}

after they touch at the central vertex of
F^{\prime}_{\smash{[\overline{k},k]}}
.
This map satisfies

\mathrm{ctype}(\varphi(\mathrm{type}(\pi^{\prime})))=\mathrm{ctype}(\varphi(\mathrm{type}(\pi))),\qquad\mathrm{type}(\pi^{\prime})=t\cdot\mathrm{type}(\pi).

Thus the two families contribute to the expressions for
\det(A^{-})

and
\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{n}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}}(A^{-})
in (
6.22
), specifically contributing

(-1)^{\smash{\ell_{t}(\mathrm{type}(\pi))}}+(-1)^{\smash{\ell_{t}(\mathrm{type}(\pi))+1}}=0

to each.
If the factorization of
F_{w}
ends with
F^{\prime}_{\smash{[\overline{k},k]}}
,
form
\pi^{\prime}
from
\pi

by swapping the final portions
(from the central vertex of
F^{\prime}_{\smash{[\overline{k},k]}}
to the end)
of paths terminating at sinks
1
,
\overline{1}
.
Then we have
\mathrm{type}(\pi^{\prime})=\mathrm{type}(\pi)\cdot t
and again
the two families together contribute
0
to
\mathrm{per}(A^{-})

and to
\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{n}}(A^{-})
.

Now consider the interpretation (
6.18
).
If

\ell_{s}(w)=0
,
then we have
w\in\{e,t\}
and
the third sum in (
6.22
)
is subtraction-free.
It has two terms equal to
1
if
w=t
, and one such term if
w=e
.
On the other hand,
if

\ell_{s}(w)\neq 0
,
then
F_{w}
has a factorization of the form
(
5.21
)
which contains at least one factor of the form

F^{\prime}_{\smash{[k_{1},k_{2}]}}

with
1\leq k_{1}<k_{2}\leq n

and with
[k_{1},k_{2}]
maximal or minimal with respect to
\preceq
.
If
[k_{1},k_{2}]
is minimal,
define an involution on
\Pi^{\mathsf{BC}}(F_{w})
by
\pi\mapsto\pi^{\prime}

where
\pi^{\prime}
is obtained from
\pi
by swapping paths
\pi_{k_{1}}
and

\pi_{k_{1}+1}

(and
\pi_{\overline{k_{1}}}
and
\pi_{\overline{k_{1}+1}}
) after they intersect at the
central vertices of
F^{\prime}_{\smash{[k_{1},k_{2}]}}
.
Then we have
\mathrm{type}(\pi^{\prime})=s^{\prime}_{k_{1}}\cdot\mathrm{type}(\pi)
and the two families
together contribute

(-1)^{\ell_{s}(\mathrm{type}(\pi))}+(-1)^{\ell_{s}(\mathrm{type}(\pi))\pm 1}=0

to
\det(A^{+})
.
If
[k_{1},k_{2}]
is maximal, then form
\pi^{\prime}
from
\pi
by swapping
the final portions (from the central vertices of
F^{\prime}_{\smash{[k_{1},k_{2}]}}
to
the end) of the paths terminating at sinks
k_{1}
,
k_{1}+1

(and
\overline{k_{1}}
,
\overline{k_{1}+1}
).
Then we have
\mathrm{type}(\pi^{\prime})=\mathrm{type}(\pi)\cdot s^{\prime}_{k_{1}}
and the two families
together contribute
0
to
\det(A^{+})
.

Finally consider the interpretation (
6.19
).
Repeating either of the above arguments with
\ell(w)
in place of

\ell_{t}(w)
or
\ell_{s}(w)
, we see that any network
F_{w}
with
w\neq e

leads to a bijection in which all pairs of paths families contribute
0
.
The only path families which are counted by
\det(A^{-})

are those of type
e
covering the network
F_{e}
.
∎

It would be interesting to define an appropriate noncommutative
ring in the variables (
6.1
)
to extend the above results for
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
-characters
to analogous results for
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
-characters.

Problem 6.6
.

State and prove
q
-analogs of
Lemma
6.3
– Proposition
6.5
.

7.
Unit interval orders

More partial solutions to Problem
1.1
for the
subsets (
5.1
) – (
5.4
)
of the Kazhdan–Lusztig bases employ posets called

unit interval orders
, those posets for which no induced
four-element subposet is isomorphic to a disjoint union
of two two-element chains (
\mathbf{2}+\mathbf{2}
)
or of a three-element chain and a single element (
\mathbf{3}+\mathbf{1}
).

In type
\mathsf{A}
,
a map
w\mapsto P(w)

from
3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
to
unit interval orders
facilitates
combinatorial interpretations of
trace evaluations
[
17
, §4–10]
.
The restriction of this map to
312
-avoiding permutations
is bijective.
In types
\mathsf{B}
and
C
, we define an analogous map

w\mapsto Q(w)
from
3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
to posets we
call
type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval orders
.
The restriction of this map to elements avoiding the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2

is bijective.
These graphical representations facilitate
combinatorial interpretation of
trace evaluations
(Section
9
) when we specialize at
q=1
.

7.1.
Type-
\mathsf{A}
unit interval orders

Fix
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{[h,n]}
(
h\in\{\overline{n},1\}
)
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, and let
F_{w}
be the planar network
corresponding to
w
by the bijection following (
5.14
),
i.e., in
[
57
, §3]
.
Given path family
\pi=(\pi_{h},\dotsc,\pi_{n})
covering
F_{w}
,
we define a partial order
P(\pi)
on these paths by
declaring
\pi_{i}<_{P(\pi)}\pi_{j}
if

(1)

i<j
as integers,

(2)

\pi_{i}
does not intersect
\pi_{j}
.

For every zig-zag network
F_{w}
, there is a unique path family of type
e

which covers
F_{w}
. If
\pi
is this path family, we define

(7.1)

P(w):=P(\pi),

and we
label the elements
of
P(w)
by

h,\dotsc,n

rather than by
\pi_{h},\dotsc,\pi_{n}
.
For example, consider the descending star networks
(
5.10
)
in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([1,4])
,
labeled
F_{4321},\dotsc,F_{1234}
as in (
5.15
).
The unit interval orders
P(4321),\dotsc,P(1234)
are

(7.2)

respectively.
The map
w\mapsto P(w)
is a surjection from
3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding permutations
in
\mathfrak{S}_{[h,n]}
to unit interval orders on
|[h,n]|
elements.
Furthermore, we have the following
[
17
, Thm. 4.4]
.

Theorem 7.1
.

The restriction of the map
w\mapsto P(w)

to the subset of
312
-avoiding permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{[h,n]}

is a bijection.

One may construct
P(w)
directly from
w
as follows.

Algorithm 7.2
.

Given
w=w_{h}\cdots w_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{[h,n]}
avoiding the pattern

312
, do

(1)

Define the word
m_{h}\cdots m_{n}
by

m_{i}=\max\{w_{h},\dotsc,w_{i}\}
.

(2)

For
i=h,\dotsc,n
define
i<_{P(w)}j
if and only if
j>m_{i}
.

The labels which paths in
F_{w}
assign to poset elements are redundant in the
sense that they are determined up to automorphism by the structure of the poset.
Specifically, for each poset element
y
define

(7.3)

\beta(y)=\#\{x\in P\,|\,x\leq_{P}y\}-\#\{z\in P\,|\,z\geq_{P}y\}.

It is easy to see that
the labels of
P(w)

inherited from the zig-zag network
F_{w}
satisfy

i<j
(as integers) if
\beta(i)<\beta(j)
.
The inverse of Algorithm
7.2
is the following.

Algorithm 7.3
.

Given unlabeled unit interval order
P
on
|[h,n]|
elements, do

(1)

For each element
y\in P
, compute

\beta(y):=\#\{x\in P\,|\,x\leq_{P}y\}-\#\{z\in P\,|\,z\geq_{P}y\}
.

(2)

Label the poset elements by
[h,n]

so that we have

\beta(h)\leq\cdots\leq\beta(n)
.

(3)

Define

w=w_{h}\cdots w_{n}
by

w_{j}=\max(\{i\in[h,n]\,|\,i\not>_{P}j\}\smallsetminus\{w_{h},\dotsc,w_{j-1}\})
.

Observe that the path families of type
e
covering the zig-zag networks
(
5.11
), which are not descending star networks and which
have the form
F_{w}
for
w
containing the pattern
312
,
form posets isomorphic to posets 2, 4, 3, 7, 13, 12, 7, 7, respectively,
in (
7.2
). It is straightforward to show that
the poset labeling inherited from
\pi
(
7.1
)
guarantees that for some indices
i
,
j
, the
minimal and maximal elements of
P(w)
are
given by intervals
[h,i]
and
[j,n]
, respectively.
Furthermore we have the following. (See, e.g.,
[
27
, p. 33]
,
[
67
, §8.2]
.)

Proposition 7.4
.

Fix
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{[h,n]}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
and define
P=P(w)
.

(1)

If
i
,
j
are incomparable in
P
with
i<j
in
\mathbb{Z}
,
then
[i,j]
is an antichain in
P
.

(2)

If
i<_{P}j
then all elements
h,\dotsc,i
are less than all elements

j,\dotsc,n
in
P
.

7.2.
Type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval orders

For each element
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{\smash{[\overline{n},n]}}

avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, the zig-zag network
F_{w}
and poset
P(w)

are defined as in Subsections
5.1
,
7.1
.
For example, the fourteen
posets corresponding to the
descending star networks in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{3},3])
(
5.24
) are

(7.4)

\begin{gathered}\hbox to14.85pt{\vbox to82.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.9462pt\lower-41.73784pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 49.21 M 2.36 49.21 C 2.36 50.52 1.3 51.57 0 51.57 C -1.3 51.57 -2.36 50.52 -2.36 49.21 C -2.36 47.91 -1.3 46.85 0 46.85 C 1.3 46.85 2.36 47.91 2.36 49.21 Z M 0 49.21}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-9.10625pt}{33.3104pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

respectively.
Observe that the path families of type
e
covering the
zig-zag networks (
5.25
)
which are not descending star networks
form posets isomorphic to posets

5
,
7
,
8
,
8
,
8
,
10
,
12
,
13
, respectively in (
7.4
).

The conditions preceding (
5.37
),
which define
\mathsf{BC}
-path families, guarantee that each such poset

P(w)

is self-dual with antiautomorphism

i\mapsto\overline{i}
.
Thus
it belongs to the class of
type-
\mathsf{C}
posets

defined in
[
19
, Defn. 10]
.
Since
P(w)
is a unit interval order, we also have the following.

Proposition 7.5
.

Fix
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
and define
P=P(w)
.
Let
\pi

be the unique path familiy of type
e
covering
F_{w}
, and let

i+1
be the smallest element of
[1,n]
such that
\pi_{i+1}
is not grounded.
Then we have

(1)

if
i>0
then
[\overline{i},i]
is an antichain in
P
,

(2)

\overline{n},\dotsc,\overline{i+1}
are less than
1,\dotsc,n
in
P
,

(3)

\overline{n},\dotsc,\overline{1}
are less than
i+1,\dotsc,n
in
P
.

Proof.

(1) Since
\pi_{\overline{i}}
and
\pi_{i}
intersect in
F_{w}
,
elements
\overline{i}
and
i
are incomparable in
P
.
By Proposition
7.4
,
[\overline{i},i]
is an antichain in
P
.

(2),(3) Suppose that
\overline{i+1}
is incomparable to
1
in
P
.
By symmetry,
\overline{1}
is incomparable to
i+1
as well.
Then
\pi_{\overline{i+1}}
and
\pi_{1}
intersect, as do
\pi_{\overline{1}}
and
\pi_{i+1}
.
Factor
F_{w}
as in (
5.23
)
and suppose that paths
\pi_{\overline{i+1}}
,
\pi_{1}

meet in
F^{\prime}_{[c_{k},d_{k}]}
.
By the definition of
\mathsf{BC}
-path family,
paths
\pi_{{i+1}}
,
\pi_{\overline{1}}
meet there as well.
If
c_{k}\geq 1
then
\pi_{\overline{1}}
,
\pi_{1}
cross twice, contradicting
the uniquess of
\pi
of type
e
covering
F_{w}
. Thus we have that

c_{k}=\overline{d_{k}}
. But then
\pi_{\overline{i+1}}
,
\pi_{i+1}
meet as well,
contradicting the assumption that these paths are not grounded.
We conclude that
\overline{i+1}<_{P}1
and
\overline{1}<_{P}i+1
.
Now Proposition
7.4
gives the desired results.
∎

This fact and
[
26
, Lem. 1.1]
show that
P(w)

is a
signed poset
as
defined in
[
26
]
,
[
52
]
.
By Proposition
7.5

the information in
P(w)
can be recorded by the subposet induced by elements

[1,n]
,
if we circle elements corresponding to grounded paths of
\pi
.
(This is not true of signed posets in general.)
Call this decorated poset
Q(w)
, and in general,
define a
type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval order
to be
a unit interval order decorated by circling
a (possibly empty) subset of minimal elements, declared to be
grounded
,
with the property that if element
i
is grounded and
j
is not,
then
\beta(i)\leq\beta(j)
, where
\beta
is the function defined in
Algorithm
7.3
.
We define an isomorphism of type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval orders
to be a poset isomorphism which respects circled elements.
For example, the
3
-element
type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval orders
Q(w)

corresponding to the
6
-element unit interval orders
P(w)
in
(
7.4
) are

(7.5)

If we remove labels from the map
w\mapsto Q(w)
,
we obtain a surjection from
3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
to type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval orders.
The restriction of this map to the subset of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2

is a bijection.
Equivalently, we have the following.

Proposition 7.6
.

The map
F_{w}\mapsto Q(w)

from

\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])

to type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval orders is bijective.

Proof.

To see that the map is injective, consider

F_{v}\neq F_{w}
in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])
.
By
[
17
, Thm. 4.4]
we have
P(v)\neq P(w)
,
since for each fixed unit interval order
P
on
2n
elements,
the set

\{F_{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{Z}}([\overline{n},n])\,|\,P(w)=P\}

contains exactly one type-
\mathsf{BC}

descending star network: the rearrangement

F^{\prime}_{[a_{u_{1}},b_{u_{1}}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{[a_{u_{t}},b_{u_{t}}]}
of
(
5.23
) satisfying
a_{u_{1}}>\cdots>a_{u_{t}}

(and
b_{u_{1}}>\cdots>b_{u_{t}}
).
Now let
P^{\prime}(v)
,
P^{\prime}(w)
be the subposets of
P(v)
and
P(w)
induced
by elements
\{1,\dotsc,n\}
. If
P^{\prime}(v)\neq P^{\prime}(w)
then
we clearly have
Q(v)\neq Q(w)
. Suppose
therefore that
P^{\prime}(v)=P^{\prime}(w)
. Since
P(v)\neq P(w)
,
there must be two indices
i\neq j
such that
elements
1,\dotsc,i
of
P^{\prime}(v)
are grounded, and elements

1,\dotsc,j
of
P^{\prime}(w)
are grounded.
Again we have
Q(v)\neq Q(w)
.

To see that the map is surjective, consider a type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval
order
Q
on
n
elements with elements labeled
as in Algorithm
7.9

and with a subset
\{1,\dotsc,i\}
of minimal elements circled,
for some
i
.
Let
F_{u}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathrm{D}}([1,n])
,
u\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
be the descending star network corresponding
to
Q
viewed as an ordinary poset, ignoring circles,
and write
F_{u}=F_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[a_{t},b_{t}]}
as
in Definition
5.3
. Now construct

F^{\prime}_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{[a_{t},b_{t}]}\bullet F^{\prime}_{[\overline{i},i]}
in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{D}}([\overline{n},n])
and call this
F_{w}

for
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.
It is easy to see that we have
F_{w}\mapsto Q
,
i.e.,
Q=Q(w)
.
∎

The bijection
w\mapsto Q(w)
, which we have defined to be the composition

(7.6)

w\mapsto F_{w}\mapsto P(w)\mapsto Q(w)

of the three maps
described in
[
57
, §3]
, (
7.1
),
and before (
7.5
),
can also be described
by the following algorithm.

Algorithm 7.7
.

Given
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2
, do

(1)

Let
b
be the least positive letter in
\{w_{1},\dotsc w_{n},n+1\}
.

(2)

Define
the word
m_{1}\cdots m_{n}
by

m_{j}=\max\{b-1,w_{1},\dotsc,w_{j}\}
.

(3)

For
j=1,\dotsc,n-1
, define
j<_{Q(w)}m_{j}+1,\dotsc,n
.

(4)

For
j=1,\dotsc,n
, if
w_{j}<0
then circle element
|w_{j}|
.

Proposition 7.8
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2
, the composition
(
7.6
) agrees with
Algorithm
7.7
.

Proof.

Computing
Q(w)
via the composition (
7.6
),
we let
F_{w}
be the descending star network
given by (
5.14
), i.e.,
[
57
, §3]
.
To construct
P(w)
, let

\pi=(\pi_{\overline{n}},\dotsc,\pi_{\overline{1}},\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{n}),\qquad\sigma=(\sigma_{\overline{n}},\dotsc,\sigma_{\overline{1}},\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{n})

be the unique path families of types
e
and
w
covering
F_{w}
,
and for
j=1,\dotsc,n-1
find the elements

k\in P
satisfying
j<_{P}k
.
First we claim that for
i^{*}
maximizing
\{w_{i}\,|\,i\in[\overline{n},j]\}
,
we have that

(7.7)

j\not<_{P}\overline{n},\dotsc,w_{i^{*}}.

By the definition of
P(w)
we have
j\not<_{P}\overline{n},\dotsc,j
,
and by the pigeonhole principle, we have

w_{i^{*}}\geq j
(as integers).
Since the path
\sigma_{i^{*}}
from source
i^{*}
to sink
w_{i^{*}}
intersects
the path
\pi_{j}
from source
j
to sink
j
,
we have a path from source
j
to sink
w_{i^{*}}
.
This path in turn intersects all paths
\pi_{j+1},\dotsc,\pi_{w_{i^{*}}}
,
and we have paths from source
j
to all sinks
j+1,\dotsc,w_{i^{*}}
.
Since
the subnetwork of
F
covered by paths
\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{n}

is isomorphic to a type-
\mathsf{A}
descending star network,
we may apply Lemma
5.4
to conclude that
\pi_{j}
intersects

\pi_{j+1},\dotsc,\pi_{w_{i^{*}}}
.
Thus we
obtain the remaining
inequalities
j\not<_{P}j+1,\dotsc,w_{i^{*}}
in
(
7.7
).

Now we claim that

(7.8)

j<_{P}w_{i^{*}}+1,\dotsc,n.

Consider the paths
\pi_{k}
for
k>w_{i^{*}}
.
Again by Lemma
5.4
, paths
\pi_{k}
and
\pi_{j}
do not intersect,
since there is no path in
F_{w}
from source
j
to sink
k
. Thus we have

j<_{P}k
as in (
7.8
).

Now we define
b
to be
the least positive letter in
\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{n},n+1\}
, and
we claim that

(7.9)

w_{i^{*}}=\max\{b-1,w_{1},\dotsc,w_{j}\}.

By Lemma
2.2
, the set of positive letters in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}

is empty or forms the interval
[b,n]
.
If this set is empty, then avoidance of the signed pattern
\overline{2}\overline{1}
implies
that
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}=\overline{1}\cdots\overline{n}
. Thus we have

w_{i^{*}}=\max\{n,\dotsc,1,\overline{1},\dotsc,\overline{j}\}=n
,

b=n+1
, and the right-hand-side of (
7.9
) is
n
.
Suppose therefore that the positive letters are
[b,n]
.
Then the positive letters
[1,b-1]
appear in
w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}
.
This allows us to write

w_{i^{*}}=\max\{w_{i}\,|\,i\leq j\}=\max(\{w_{\overline{n}},\dotsc,w_{\overline{1}}\}\cup\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{j}\})=\max\{b-1,w_{1},\dotsc,w_{j}\}.

The subposet of
P(w)
induced by
[1,n]
, which will become
Q(w)
,
now agrees with steps (1) – (3) of Algorithm
7.7
.
To complete the construction of
Q(w)
by (
7.6
),
we circle grounded elements of
P(w)
, if there are any.
If no path of
\pi
is grounded, then we do nothing.
In this case,
no path of
\sigma
has a source and sink with different signs,
all letters in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
are positive, and nothing is done in
step (4) of Algorithm
7.7
.
On the other hand, if some
2k
paths of
\pi
are grounded,
then by Proposition
7.5
, these paths are

(\pi_{\overline{k}},\dotsc,\pi_{\overline{1}},\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{k})
,
and we circle elements
1,\dotsc,k
of
P(w)
to form
Q(w)

by (
7.6
).
In this case,

\smash{F^{\prime}_{[c_{t},d_{t}]}=F^{\prime}_{[\overline{k},k]}}
is the last factor
in the expression
(
5.23
)
for
F_{w}
,
and the letters
\overline{1},\dotsc,\overline{k}

appear in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
Thus in step (4) of Algorithm
7.7
,
elements
1,\dotsc,k
are circled.
∎

Like Algorithm
5.6
,
Algorithm
7.7
is invertible even if labels of the poset
Q
are not
given.

Algorithm 7.9
.

Given unlabeled type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval order
Q

with
p
circled elements, do

(1)

For each element
y\in Q
, compute

\beta(y):=\#\{x\in Q\,|\,x\leq_{Q}y\}-\#\{z\in Q\,|\,z\geq_{Q}y\}
.

(2)

Label the poset elements by
[1,n]

so that we have

\beta(1)\leq\cdots\leq\beta(n)
, and so that circled elements
form the interval
[1,p]
.

(3)

Define the word

a_{1}\cdots a_{n}=\overline{1}\cdots\overline{p}(p+1)\cdots n
.

(4)

Define

w=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
by

w_{j}=\max(\{a_{i}\,|\,i\not>_{Q}j\}\smallsetminus\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{j-1}\})
.

To see that
Algorithm
7.9
inverts Algorithm
7.7
,
we consider a close relationship between certain descending
star networks of types
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{BC}
.

Lemma 7.10
.

Fix
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2
with
p>0

negative
letters
(\overline{1},\dotsc,\overline{p})
appearing in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
,
and
type-
\mathsf{BC}
descending star network

F_{w}=F^{\prime}_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F^{\prime}_{[c_{t-1},d_{t-1}]}\bullet F^{\prime}_{[\overline{p},p]},

with factors defined as in (
5.21
).
Define
u\in\mathfrak{S}_{[\overline{p},n]}
to be the
312
-avoiding permutation
corresponding to the type-
\mathsf{A}
descending star network

F_{u}=F_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\bullet\cdots\bullet F_{[c_{t-1},d_{t-1}]}\bullet F_{[\overline{p},p]},

with factors defined as in (§
5.1
).
Then the one-line notation of
u
is
p\cdots 21w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
and
the subposet
P_{[1,n]}
of
P(u)
induced by
[1,n]
satisfies

P_{[1,n]}\cong Q(w)
(as undecorated posets).

Proof.

Let
\pi^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime}_{\overline{n}},\dotsc,\pi^{\prime}_{\overline{1}},\pi^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\pi^{\prime}_{n})
and

\pi=(\pi_{\overline{p}},\dotsc,\pi_{\overline{1}},\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{n})

be the unique path families of type
e
covering
F_{w}
and
F_{u}
,
respectively.
By Definition
5.14
and Proposition
5.15

we have
c_{1}>\cdots>c_{t-1}\geq 1
.
Thus for
1\leq i<j\leq n
we have that
\pi_{i}
intersects
\pi_{j}

if and only if
\pi^{\prime}_{i}
intersects
\pi^{\prime}_{j}
. It follows that

P_{[1,n]}\cong Q(w)
.

Now let

\sigma^{\prime}=(\sigma^{\prime}_{\overline{n}},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{\overline{1}},\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{n})
and

\sigma=(\sigma_{\overline{p}},\dotsc,\sigma_{\overline{1}},\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{n})

be the unique path families of types
w
and
u
covering
F_{w}
and
F_{u}
,
respectively. Both families have the property that any two paths which
intersect must cross. Thus paths
\sigma_{\overline{p}},\dotsc,\sigma_{\overline{1}}
,
which intersect only at the central vertex of
F_{[\overline{p},p]}
, have
sinks
p,\dotsc,1
, respectively. Thus

u_{\overline{p}}\cdots u_{\overline{1}}=p\cdots 1
.
Also, paths
\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{n}
pass through the same stars
as
\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{n}
, respectively, and have the same sinks.
Thus
u_{1}\cdots u_{n}=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
∎

Proposition 7.11
.

Algorithm
7.9
inverts Algorithm
7.7
.

Proof.

Fix
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
. If no negative letters appear in

w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
,
then we may interpret this word as an element of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.
The applications of Algorithms
7.2
and
7.7
to
w
agree
and produce the poset
P(w)=Q(w)
.
Since this poset has no circled elements,
the applications of Algorithms
7.3
and
7.9
to it agree,
producing
w
since Algorithm
7.3
inverts Algorithm
7.2
.
It follows that Algorithm
7.9
inverts Algorithm
7.7
as well.

Now suppose that
p>0
negative letters appear in
w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
,
and define

u=u_{\overline{p}}\cdots u_{\overline{1}}u_{1}\cdots u_{n}=p\cdots 21w_{1}\cdots w_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{[\overline{p},n]}.

By Lemma
7.10
,
u
avoids the ordinary pattern
312
.
Define
P_{[1,n]}
to be the subposet of
P(u)
induced by elements
[1,n]
.
Applying Algorithm
7.3
to
P(u)
, we obtain
u
.
It follows that for
j=1,\dotsc,n
, we have

(7.10)

\displaystyle u_{j}=w_{j}

\displaystyle=\max(\{i\in[\overline{p},n]\,|\,i\not>_{P(u)}j\}\smallsetminus\{u_{\overline{p}},\dotsc,u_{j-1}\})

\displaystyle=\max(\{i\in[\overline{p},\overline{1}]\cup[p+1,n]\,|\,i\not>_{P(u)}j\}\smallsetminus\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{j-1}\}).

Since
[\overline{p},p]\subseteq P(u)
is an antichain of minimal elements,
each pair
(i,j)\in[\overline{p},\overline{1}]\times[1,n]

satisfies

i\not>_{P(u)}j
if and only if
\overline{i}\not>_{P_{[1,n]}}j
.
Thus we may rewrite (
7.10
) as

w_{j}=\max(\{i\in[\overline{p},\overline{1}]\,|\,\overline{i}\not>_{P_{[1,n]}}j\}\cup\{i\in[p+1,n]\,|\,i\not>_{P_{[1,n]}}j\}\smallsetminus\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{j-1}\}).

On the other hand, applying Algorithm
7.9
to
Q(w)
,
we obtain a word
v_{1}\cdots v_{n}
satisfying

v_{j}=\max(\{i\in[\overline{p},\overline{1}]\,|\,\overline{i}\not>_{Q(w)}j\}\cup\{i\in[p+1,n]\,|\,i\not>_{Q(w)}j\}\smallsetminus\{v_{1},\dotsc,v_{j-1}\}).

By Lemma
7.10
, we have
P_{[1,n]}\cong Q(w)
, and therefore

v_{1}\cdots v_{n}=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
.
Again, Algorithm
7.9
inverts Algorithm
7.7
.
∎

8.
Indifference graphs

More partial solutions to Problem
1.1
for the
subsets (
5.1
) – (
5.4
)
of the Kazhdan–Lusztig bases employ graphs
called

indifference graphs
, those graphs
whose vertices correspond
to elements of a unit interval order
P
and whose edges correspond to
unordered pairs
\{i,j\}
of poset elements which are incomparable,
i.e.,
i\not\leq_{P}j
and
j\not\leq_{P}i
.

In type
\mathsf{A}
, we have a map
w\mapsto G(w)
,
from
3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
to indifference graphs
whose colorings and edge orientations
facilitate simple combinatorial interpretations of
trace evaluations
[
17
, §5–10]
.
In types
\mathsf{B}
and
C
, we define an analogous map

w\mapsto G(w)
from
3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
to
objects which we call

type-
\mathsf{BC}
indifference graphs
.
These graphical representations facilitate
simple combinatorial interpretation of certain trace evaluations
(Section
9
), when we specialize at
q=1
.

8.1.
Type-
\mathsf{A}
indifference graphs, coloring, and orientation

Given any poset
P
,
we define its
incomparability graph

\mathrm{inc}(P)

to be the graph whose vertices are the elements of
P
and whose
edges are the pairs of incomparable elements of
P
.
When
P=P(w)
is a unit interval order, write
G(w)=\mathrm{inc}(P)

and call this an
indifference graph
.
It is possible to have
P(w)\not\cong P(v)
and
G(w)\cong G(v)
.
For example, the incomparability graphs
of the fourteen unit interval orders (
7.2
) are the nine
nonisomorphic indifference graphs

(8.1)

In Section
9

we will combinatorially evaluate certain traces
at Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements
\widetilde{C}_{w}(1)\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{S}_{n}]
with
w
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
by
coloring the vertices of
G(w)
or by orienting the edges of
G(w)
.

Given any graph
G=(V,E)
call a map

\kappa:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\smallsetminus\{0\}

a
proper coloring of
G
if
\{a,b\}\in E

implies that
\kappa(a)\neq\kappa(b)
.
More specifically, say that a proper coloring has
type

\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{r})\vdash n

if
\lambda_{k}
vertices have color
k
for
k=1,\dotsc,r
.
If
G=\mathrm{inc}(P)

then a proper coloring of
\mathrm{inc}(P)
of type
\lambda
corresponds
to a sequence of pairwise dijoint chains of cardinalities

(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{r})
in
P
.

Call a directed graph
O=(V,E^{\prime})

an
orientation
of
G=(V,E)
if
O
is obtained from
G

by replacing each undirected edge
\{a,b\}\in E

with exactly one of the directed edges
(a,b)
or
(b,a)
.
Call
O

acyclic
if it has no directed cycles.
Acyclic orientations of
G(w)

correspond to
sequences
(v_{h},\dotsc,v_{n})
of elements of
P(w)

satisfying
v_{i}\not\geq_{P(w)}v_{i+1}
for
i=h,\dotsc,n-1
.
We call these
P(w)
-descent-free
sequences.
(See
[
8
, §4]
and references there.)

Proposition 8.1
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{[h,n]}
, acyclic orientations of
G(w)

correspond bijectively to
P(w)
-descent-free sequences of elements
of
P(w)
.

Specifically, this bijection from acyclic orientations to
P(w)
-descent-free
sequences is given by the following algorithm.

Algorithm 8.2
.

Given
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
and an acyclic orientation
O
of
G(w)
, do

(1)

Set
O(h)=O
.

(2)

For
i=h,\dotsc,l
,

(a)

Let
j
be the least integer appearing as a
vertex
in
O(i)
and having indegree
0
.

(b)

Set
v_{i}=j
.

(c)

Form
O(i+1)
by removing vertex
j
and its incident edges from
O(i)
.

(3)

Output the sequence
(v_{h},\dotsc,v_{l})
.

The inverse of Algorithm
8.2
is very simple.

Algorithm 8.3
.

Given
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{[h,l]}
,
undirected graph
G(w)
with vertices labeled
\{h,\dotsc,l\}
,
and
P(w)
-descent-free sequence
v=(v_{h},\dotsc,v_{l})
,
do

(1)

Orient each edge
\{a,b\}
of
G(w)

as
(a,b)
if
a
appears before
b
in
v
, and as
(b,a)
otherwise.

8.2.
Type-
\mathsf{BC}
indifference graphs, coloring, and orientation

Given a type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval order
Q
, define its
incomparability
graph

\mathrm{inc}(Q)
to be the decorated graph
whose vertices are the elements of
Q
, maintaining circles,
and whose edges are the pairs of incomparable elements of
Q
.
For
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, write
\Gamma(w)=\mathrm{inc}(Q(w))

and call
\Gamma(w)
a
type-
\mathsf{BC}
indifference graph
.
We define an isomorphism of type-
\mathsf{BC}
indifference graphs
to be a graph isomorphism which respects circled elements.
Again, it is possible to have
Q(w)\not\cong Q(v)

and
\Gamma(w)\cong\Gamma(v)
.
For instance, the
fourteen (labeled) type-
\mathsf{BC}
indifference graphs on three elements are

(8.2)

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{3}
\scriptstyle{2}

with the third and fourth graphs being isomorphic.

Analogous to type-
\mathsf{A}
indifference graphs,
type-
\mathsf{BC}
indifference graphs have colorings and edge
orientations which facilitate the evaluation of certain type-
\mathsf{BC}

traces at Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements
\smash{\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]

when
w
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
.
Given a type-
\mathsf{BC}
indifference graph
\Gamma=(V,E)
,
define a
marked
\mathsf{BC}
-coloring

\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}):V\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\{0,1\}

of
\Gamma
to be an assignment
of a nonzero color
\kappa_{1}(b)
and possibly a star
(if
\kappa_{2}(b)=1
)
to each vertex
b\in V
,
with the properties that

(1)

for vertex
b
grounded we have
\kappa_{1}(b)>0
,

(2)

for vertex
b
not grounded we have
\kappa_{2}(b)=0
.

Say that
\kappa
has
type

(\lambda,\mu)=((\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{m}),(\mu_{1},\dotsc,\mu_{k}))
if

(1)

\lambda_{i}
vertices have color
i
, for
i=1,\dotsc,m
,

(2)

\mu_{i}
vertices have color
\overline{i}
, for
i=1,\dotsc,k
,

and that
\kappa
is
proper

if
\{a,b\}\in E
implies that
\kappa_{1}(a)\neq\kappa_{1}(b)
.
As before, monochromatic sets of
\Gamma(w)

correspond to chains in
Q(w)
;
now each such chain
contains at most one grounded element.
Thus a proper
\mathsf{BC}
-coloring of
\Gamma
of type
(\lambda,\mu)

may be represented by a pair
(U,V)
of
Q
-tableaux
in which column
i
of
U

(i=1,\dotsc,m)

contains the color-
i
chain of
Q

with at most one grounded element marked with a star,
and
column
i
of
V

(i=1,\dotsc,k)

contains the color-
\overline{i}
chain of
Q

with no grounded elements.

Define a
marked acyclic orientation
of a type-
\mathsf{BC}
indifference graph
to be a directed graph
O
on the same verices, with some subset of
grounded vertices marked by stars,
in which each undirected edge
\{a,b\}
is replaced with one of
the directed edges
(a,b)
or
(b,a)
.
For example, the type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval order
Q=Q(\overline{1}4365\overline{2})
,
its incomparability graph
\mathrm{inc}(Q)
,
a marked acyclic orientation of
\mathrm{inc}(Q)
, and
a marked coloring of
\mathrm{inc}(Q)
of type
((2,1,1),(2))
are

(8.3)

To connect acyclic orientations of
\Gamma(w)

to
Q(w)
-descent-free sequences as in
Proposition
8.1
,
we define
marked
Q(w)
-descent-free

sequences to be
those
Q(w)
-descent-free sequences in which some subset of
grounded elements is marked.

Proposition 8.4
.

Marked acyclic orientations of
\Gamma(w)

correspond to marked
Q(w)
-descent-free
sequences
of elements of
Q(w)
.

Proof.

The correspondence is given by Algorithms
8.2
–
8.3
,
modified so that marked graph vertices correspond to marked poset elements.
∎

For example,
the
Q
-descent-free sequence corresponding to the acyclic orientation
in (
8.3
) is
(2^{\star}\negthickspace,1,3,4,6,5)
.

We remark that other authors have defined
\mathsf{BC}
-analogs of
graphs
[
34
]
,
[
52
]
,
have associated these to posets generalizing
type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval orders
[
20
]
,
[
52
]
,
and have studied their colorings
[
42
]
,
[
71
]
.
However, it is not clear that such graphs and colorings
are closely related to those in the present work.
In particular, the other authors’ graphs have edges describing
comparability of poset elements rather than
incomparability, and their colorings include restrictions on which
pairs of vertices can receive colors of the same absolute value,
whereas ours do not.

9.
Combinatorial trace evaluations: path tableaux, poset tableaux,
and acyclic orientations

Our main results,
Theorem
9.6
– Theorem
9.8
,
combinatorially interpret
trace evaluations

\theta(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))
for
certain
\theta\in\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{B}_{n})

and all
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
.
Analogous to known type-
\mathsf{A}
results,
our new type-
\mathsf{BC}
evaluations use the type-
\mathsf{BC}

unit interval orders and their incomparability graphs
defined in Subsections
7.2
,
8.2
.

9.1.
Type-
\mathsf{A}
trace evaluations

To state these interpretations, we fill
(French) Young diagrams with paths
and we call the resulting structures

path tableaux
.
If the paths are a family
\pi=(\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{n})
which covers
F_{w}
,
we will more specifically call the path tableau an
F_{w}
-tableau
,
or a
\pi
-tableau.

If
\pi
has type
v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, then we also say that each

\pi
-tableau has
type
v
.
Since
\pi
can be viewed as the poset
Q(\pi)
defined in
Subsection
7.1
,

\pi
-tableaux are special cases of
Gessel and Viennot’s
poset tableaux

[
29
]
,
Young diagrams filled with elements of a poset.
Thus if
\pi
is the unique family of type
e
covering
F_{w}
,
then a
P(\pi)
-tableau
is a
P(w)
-tableau.
For any tableau

U
, let
U_{i}
be the
i
th row of
U
,
and let
U_{i,j}
be the
j
th entry in row
i
.
Let
\mathcal{U}(\pi,\lambda)
denote the set of all
\pi
-tableaux of shape

\lambda
, and let
\mathcal{U}(F_{w},\lambda)

denote the set of
all

F_{w}
-tableaux of shape
\lambda
, i.e.,
containing all path families covering
F_{w}
,

(9.1)

\mathcal{U}(F_{w},\lambda)=\negthickspace\negthickspace\bigcup_{\pi\in\Pi(F_{w})}\negthickspace\negthickspace\mathcal{U}(\pi,\lambda).

If
\pi
is the unique path family of type
e
covering
F_{w}
, then define

\mathcal{U}(P(w),\lambda):=\mathcal{U}(\pi,\lambda)
.
For example, consider
F_{2341}
,
the seventh zig-zag network in (
5.11
),
and let
\pi=(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4})

be the unique path family of type
e
covering
F_{2341}
.
Then
P(2341)=Q(\pi)
is the seventh unit interval order in (
7.2
).
Labeling each element
\pi_{i}
of
P(2341)
by
i
and forming a few

P(2341)
-tableaux, we have

(9.2)

\begin{gathered}P(2341)=\hbox to47.52pt{\vbox to33.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.79143pt\lower-26.47206pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The tableaux
S
,
T
,
U
,
V
,
W
all belong to

\mathcal{U}(P(2341),31)
.

Several properties which path-tableaux may posess can be defined for
poset tableaux. Let
P
be any labeled poset and let

U
be a
P
-tableau.
Call a entry
U_{i,j}
a
record
in
U

if it is greater in
P
than
U_{i,1},\dotsc,U_{i,j-1}
.
Call a record
U_{i,j}

nontrivial
if
j>1
.
Call a row of
U

left anchored
(
right anchored
)
if its leftmost (rightmost) element is less in
\mathbb{Z}

than all other elements
in the row.
Call elements
(a,b)
a
P
-inversion
in
U

if the elements are incomparable in
P
with
a<b

in
\mathbb{Z}
and
b
appearing in an earlier column than
a
.
Let
\textsc{inv}_{P}(U)
denote the number of
P
-inversions in
U
.
Call elements
(U_{i,j},U_{i,j+1})
a
P
-descent
in
U

if
U_{i,j}>_{P}U_{i,j+1}
.
Let
\mathrm{des}_{P}(U)
denote the number of
P
-descents in
U
.
Define
\mathrm{sort}(U)
to be the tableau obtained from
U
by sorting entries
in each row so that labels increase to the right.
Define a
P
-excedance in
U
to be a position
(i,j)
such that

U_{i,j}>_{P}\mathrm{sort}(U)_{i,j}
.
Let
\mathrm{exc}_{P}(U)
be the number of
P
-excedances in
U
.

Call a
P
-tableau
U

(1)

column-strict
if the entries of each column satisfy

U_{i,j}<_{P}U_{i+1,j}
,

(2)

descent-free
or
row-semistrict

if
\mathrm{des}_{P}(U)=0
,

(3)

cyclically row-semistrict
if it is row-semistrict,
and if the last entry
U_{i,\lambda_{i}}
of each row satisfies

U_{i,\lambda_{i}}\not>_{P}U_{i,1}
,

(4)

standard
if it is column-strict and row-semistrict,

(5)

excedance-free
if
\mathrm{exc}_{P}(U)=0
,

(6)

record-free
if no row has a nontrivial
P
-record,

(7)

left anchored
(
right anchored
) if each row is
left anchored (right anchored).

For example, we may examine the tableaux in (
9.2
)
for these properties to obtain the table

\phantom{M}S

\phantom{M}T

\phantom{M}U

\phantom{M}V

\phantom{ni}W

column-strict

\checkmark

\checkmark

row-semistrict

\checkmark

\checkmark

\checkmark

cyclically row-semistrict

\checkmark

standard

\checkmark

excedance-free

\checkmark

\checkmark

record-free

\checkmark

\checkmark

\checkmark

left anchored

\checkmark

\checkmark

\checkmark

right anchored

\checkmark

\checkmark

,

where
the row-semistrict tableaux
S
and
T
fail to be
cyclically row-semistrict because their first rows begin with
1
and
end with
3>_{P}1
and
4>_{P}1
, respectively.

Other properties of path-tableaux depend upon the fact that
each path

\pi_{j}

in a path family
has a source vertex

\mathrm{src}(\pi_{j})

and a sink vertex

\mathrm{snk}(\pi_{j})
.
Given a path-tableau
U
,
let
\mathrm{src}(U)
and
\mathrm{snk}(U)
denote the Young tableaux of integers
obtained from
U
by replacing paths
\pi_{1},\dotsc,\pi_{n}

with their corresponding source and sink indices, respectively.
If
U
is a path-tableau, call
U

(1)

row-closed
if for each index
i
,

\mathrm{snk}(U_{i})
is a permutation of
\mathrm{src}(U_{i})
,

(2)

left row-strict
if entries of
\mathrm{src}(U)

strictly increase in each row,

(3)

cylindrical
if in each row
U_{i}=(U_{i,1},\dotsc,U_{i,k})

we have

\mathrm{snk}(U_{i})=\mathrm{src}(U_{i,2},\dotsc,U_{i,k},U_{i,1})
.

For example consider
F_{2341}
again,
the unique path families

\rho
,

\sigma
,
and

\tau

of type
2314
,
2134
, and
2341

which cover
F_{2341}
,

(9.3)

to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$12$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt,\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$33$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt|\vbox
