Theorem 9.8
.

Let
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
have type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval order
Q
,
and fix bipartition
(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n
.
We have

(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))=\#\{(U,V)\in\mathcal{B}(Q,\lambda,\mu)\,|\,U,V\text{ standard }\}.

Proof.

Let
A
be the path matrix of
F_{w}
and let
\chi^{\lambda}
,
\chi^{\mu}

be the characters of
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,
\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}
satisfying

(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}=(\chi^{\lambda}\otimes\delta\chi^{\mu})\big\uparrow\negthickspace\negthickspace\phantom{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{B}_{k,n-k}}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}
.
By
Lemma
6.3
and
Theorem
6.4

we have

(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))=\mathrm{Imm}_{(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(A)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset[n]\\
|I|=k\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Imm}_{\chi^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}}(A_{I,I}^{+})\mathrm{Imm}_{\chi^{\mu}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}}}(A_{[n]\smallsetminus I,[n]\smallsetminus I}^{-}).

Expanding irreducible character immanants

\mathrm{Imm}_{\chi^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}}
and
\mathrm{Imm}_{\chi^{\mu}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}}}

in terms of induced trivial character immanants, we obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset[n]\\
|I|=k\end{subarray}}\sum_{\alpha\vdash k}K_{\alpha,\lambda}^{-1}\mathrm{Imm}_{\eta^{\alpha}}(A_{I,I}^{+})\negthickspace\sum_{\beta\vdash n-k}\negthickspace\negthickspace K_{\beta,\mu}^{-1}\mathrm{Imm}_{\eta^{\beta}}(A_{[n]\smallsetminus I,[n]\smallsetminus I}^{-}).

Now let
Q

be the type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval order corresponding to
w
.
For any subset
J
of
[n]
and any partition
\nu\vdash|J|
,
let
r(Q_{J},\nu)
be the number of row-semistrict

Q_{J}
-tableaux of shape
\nu
, and let
p(J)

be the number of elements of
Q_{J}
which are grounded.
By (
6.4
) and Proposition
9.5
, we have

\mathrm{Imm}_{\eta^{\alpha}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}}(A_{I,I}^{+})=2^{p(I)}r(Q_{I},\alpha),\qquad

and

\mathrm{Imm}_{\eta^{\beta}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}}}(A_{[n]\smallsetminus I,[n]\smallsetminus I}^{-})=\begin{cases}r(Q_{[n]\smallsetminus I},\beta)&\text{if $p([n]\smallsetminus I)=0$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Thus by
[
17
, Thm. 4.7 (ii-b), (iii)]
, the sums

\begin{gathered}\sum_{\alpha\vdash k}K_{\alpha,\lambda}^{-1}\mathrm{Imm}_{\eta^{\alpha}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}}(A_{I,I}^{+})=2^{p(I)}\sum_{\alpha\vdash k}K_{\alpha,\lambda}^{-1}r(Q_{I},\alpha),\\
\sum_{\beta\vdash n-k}\negthickspace\negthickspace K_{\beta,\mu}^{-1}\mathrm{Imm}_{\eta^{\beta}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}}}(A_{[n]\smallsetminus I,[n]\smallsetminus I}^{-})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta\vdash n-k}\negthickspace\negthickspace K_{\beta,\mu}^{-1}r(Q_{[n]\smallsetminus I},\beta)}&\text{if $p([n]\smallsetminus I)=0$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{gathered}

are equal to the numbers of
standard
Q_{I}
-tableaux of shape
\lambda

containing any number of grounded elements which may be circled,
and standard
Q_{[n]\smallsetminus I}
-tableaux of shape
\mu

containing no grounded elements, respectively.
∎

For example, consider the evaluation
(\chi\chi)^{21,11}(\widetilde{C}_{2345\overline{1}}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))

and the poset
Q(2345\overline{1})
in (
9.17
).
Of the bitableaux shown there, only the second contributes to this evaluation.

Theorem 9.9
.

Let
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231

have type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval order
Q
,
and fix bipartition

(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n
.
We have

(\psi\psi)^{\lambda,\mu}(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))=\#\{(U,V)\in\mathcal{B}(Q,\lambda,\mu)\,|\,U,V\text{ cyclically
row-semistrict }\}.

Proof.

Let
A
be the path matrix of
F_{w}
and let
k=|\lambda|
.
By Lemma
6.3
and
Theorem
6.4

we have

(9.22)

(\psi\psi)^{\lambda,\mu}(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))=\mathrm{Imm}_{(\psi\psi)^{\lambda,\mu}}^{\mathfrak{B}_{n}}(A)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset[n]\\
|I|=m\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}}(A_{I,I}^{+})\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{\mu}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{n-k}}}(A_{[n]\smallsetminus I,[n]\smallsetminus I}^{-}).

By
[
65
, Prop. 2.4]
we have

\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{\lambda}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}}(\mathbf{x})=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{(J_{1},\dotsc,J_{r})}\negthickspace\negthickspace\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{\lambda_{1}}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda_{1}}}}(\mathbf{x}_{J_{1},J_{1}})\cdots\mathrm{Imm}_{\psi^{\lambda_{r}}}^{\smash{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda_{r}}}}(\mathbf{x}_{J_{r},J_{r}}).

Thus each term in the sum (
9.22
) is itself
a sum of products of type-
\mathsf{A}
single-cycle
power sum trace immanants, evaluated at
A^{+}
or
A^{-}
.
By Proposition
9.5
each factor counts
one-row cyclically row-semistrict tableaux,
and their product counts
bitableaux of the required shape.
∎

Corollary 9.10
.

Let
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
have zig-zag network
F_{w}
and
type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval order
Q=Q(w)
.
In Theorem
9.9

we may replace
cyclically row-semistrict
Q
-tableaux
with either of the following:

(i)

record-free
Q
-tableaux,

(ii)

cylindrical
F_{w}
-tableaux.

We also have that
(\psi\psi)^{\lambda,\mu}(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))
equals

(iii)

\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{r}\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{s}\cdot\#\{(U,V)\in\mathcal{B}(Q,\lambda,\mu)\,|\,U,V\text{ left-anchored, row-semistrict }\}
.

(iv)

the number of marked acyclic orientations of subgraph sequences
(
9.19
)
in which each oriented subgraph has one source,
and all grounded vertices appear in
I_{1},\dotsc,I_{r}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.5
,
one-rowed tableaux
in Theorem
9.9

which are cyclically
row-semistrict
correspond bijectively to one-rowed tableaux
with properties

(i)
or
(ii)

and to acyclic orientations
(iii)
of

\mathrm{inc}(Q)
which have one source.
Thus there is a correspondence of
several-rowed tableaux and of acyclic orientations of subgraph sequences.
In all cases, we have equality of
the indices of paths, poset elements, and vertices which are grounded,
so markings correspond in the obvious way.
∎

For example, consider evaluating
(\psi\psi)^{21,11}(\widetilde{C}_{2345\overline{1}}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))
by
applying Theorem
9.9

to the poset
Q(2345\overline{1})
in (
9.17
).
Of the tableaux listed there, only the first and third contribute to
this trace evaluation. If we modify the criteria of the theorem as in
Corollary
9.10
(i), the same is true,
since a tableau with two or
fewer columns is
Q
-record-free if and only
if it is cyclically
Q
-row-semistrict.
On the other hand, if we modify the criteria of the theorem as in
Corollary
9.10
(iii), then the first, second,
and fourth bitableaux contribute.

Now suppose that we modify the criteria of the theorem
as in Corollary
9.10
(ii). Then we may
apply these to path families
covering
F_{2345\overline{1}}
in (
9.15
),
and we find that the first, third and fourth
tableaux in (
9.16
)
contribute to
(\psi\psi)^{21,11}(\widetilde{C}_{2345\overline{1}}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))
.

Finally, we may modify the criteria of the theorem
by applying Corollary
9.10
(iii) to
(
9.20
). Both tableaux in (
9.21
) contribute,
since no component has more than one source.

While
Proposition
9.1
– Theorem
9.3

have known
q
-analogs,
no such
q
-analogs are known for the results in Subsection
9.2
.

Problem 9.11
.

State and prove
q
-analogs of the results in
Lemma
9.4
–
Corollary
9.10

10.
Some equivalence relations

Given a Coxeter group
W
and its Hecke algebra
H=H(W)
,
the trace space
\mathcal{T}(H)
naturally partitions
H
into equivalence classes
via the relation
\approx
defined by

(10.1)

D_{1}\approx D_{2}\text{ if for all }\theta_{q}\in\mathcal{T}(H)\text{ we have }\theta_{q}(D_{1})=\theta_{q}(D_{2}).

When
W=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
or
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, we may restrict this relation to the subset
of Kazhdan-Lusztig basis elements indexed by elements of
W
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
.
Two more equivalence relations related to this restricted relation
are defined in terms of
isomorphism of the posets and graphs
described in Sections
7
–
8
:

P(v)\cong P(w)
(
Q(v)\cong Q(w)
), or
G(v)\cong G(w)
.
In type
\mathsf{A}
these relations refine the first;
in types
\mathsf{BC}
, we conjecture the same to be true
and prove a weaker statement.

10.1.
Type-
\mathsf{A}
equivalence relations

The equivalence relation on

(10.2)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\text{ avoids the patterns $3412$ and $4231${}}\}

defined by poset isomorphism
P(v)\cong P(w)

refines that defined by graph isomorphism
G(v)\cong G(w)
,
which in turn refines
the restriction of the relation
\approx
(
10.1
)
to this set.

Theorem 10.1
.

For
v,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, we have the implications

P(v)\cong P(w)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad G(v)\cong G(w)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)\approx\widetilde{C}_{w}(q).

Proof.

The first implication is clear; the second follows from
a
q
-analog of Theorem
9.2
(i-b) in

[
17
, Prop. 7.3 – Thm. 7.4]
which states that
all of the evaluations

\{\epsilon_{q}^{\lambda}(\widetilde{C}_{w}(q))\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}

are determined by

G(w)
.
∎

The converse of the first implication above is not true (
8.1
);
the converse of the second is not known to be true.

Problem 10.2
.

For some
n
find
3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding permutations
v,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}

which satisfy

G(v)\not\cong G(w)
and

\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)\approx\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)

or show that this is impossible.

By Theorems
7.1
and
10.1
,
the problem of evaluating
H_{n}(q)
-traces at (
10.2
)
reduces to the problem of evaluating these traces at the subset of
(
10.2
) indexed by
312
-avoiding
permutations
[
17
, Thm. 5.6]
.

Corollary 10.3
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, there exists
v\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding
312

such that we have
\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\approx\widetilde{C}_{v}(q)
.

Furthermore, some experimentation suggests that
the problem of evaluating traces
at
all
Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements reduces to the
problem of evaluating traces at
the subset of (
10.2
) indexed by
312
-avoiding
permutations.
With precise details of such a reduction not yet conjectured,
we have the following problem
[
3
, Conj. 1.9]
,

[
32
, Conj. 3.1]
.

Problem 10.4
.

Show that for each
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
there exists a set
S=S(w)\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{n}

of
312
-avoiding permutations
and a set
\{p_{v,w}(q)\,|\,v\in S\}\subset\mathbb{N}[q]

of polynomials such that we have

\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)\approx\sum_{v\in S}p_{v,w}(q)\widetilde{C}_{v}(q).

We remark that Haiman
[
32
, §3]

introduced the name
codominant
for
312
-avoiding permutations
because by (
2.1
) these have the form

vw_{0}

for
w_{0}
the longest element of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and

v
belonging to the dominant (
132
-avoiding) subset of
the vexillary (
2143
-avoiding) permutations defined in
[
45
]
.

10.2.
Type-
\mathsf{BC}
equivalence relations

We distinguish between the
\mathsf{BC}
-analog
of the equivalence relation (
10.1
)
and that of its
q=1
specialization.
For
D_{1},D_{2}\in H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
, define

(10.3)

D_{1}\approx_{q}D_{2}\text{ if for all }\theta_{q}\in\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))\text{ we have }\theta_{q}(D_{1})=\theta_{q}(D_{2}).

For
D_{1},D_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]
,
let
D_{1}\approx_{1}D_{2}
be the
q=1
specialization of the above.
These relations naturally restrict to the Kazhdan–Lusztig bases of

H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
and
\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]
, and to the subsets of these indexed by elements
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}

avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
.
It is easy to show that the equivalence relation on

(10.4)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\ {\text{avoiding the patterns $3412$ and $4231${}}}\}

defined by type-
\mathsf{BC}
unit interval order
isomorphism
Q(v)\cong Q(w)

refines that defined by
type-
\mathsf{BC}
indifference graph
isomorphism

\Gamma(v)\cong\Gamma(w)
,
which in turn refines the relation
\approx_{1}
.

Theorem 10.5
.

For
v,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, we have the implications

Q(v)\cong Q(w)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\Gamma(v)\cong\Gamma(w)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\widetilde{C}_{v}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)\approx_{1}\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1).

Proof.

The first implication is clear; the second follows from
Corollary
9.7

∎

The converse of the first implication above is not true (
8.2
);
the converse of the second is not known to be true.
We conjecture that the second implication can be strengthened.

Conjecture 10.6
.

For
v,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, we have the implication

\Gamma(v)\cong\Gamma(w)\ \Longrightarrow\ \widetilde{C}_{v}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)\approx_{q}\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
.

The converse of this conjectured implication is not known to be true.

Problem 10.7
.

For some
n
find
3412
-avoiding,
4231
-avoiding permutations
v,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}

which satisfy

\Gamma(v)\not\cong\Gamma(w)

and
\widetilde{C}_{v}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)\approx_{q}\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
,
or show that this is impossible.

By Theorems
5.19
and
10.5
,
the problem of evaluating
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
-traces at

(10.5)

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\ {\text{avoids the patterns $3412$ and $4231${}}}\}

reduces to the problem of evaluating these traces at the subset of
(
10.5
) indexed by
elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2
.

Corollary 10.8
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
, there exists
v\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2

such that we have
\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)\approx_{1}\widetilde{C}_{v}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1)
.

If Conjecture
10.6
is true, then the conclusion
of Corollary
10.8
becomes
\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)\approx_{q}\widetilde{C}_{v}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
.
It would be interesting to discover the extent to which trace evaluations at

\{\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)\,|\,w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\ {\text{avoids the signed patterns $1\overline{2}$, $\overline{2}1$, $\overline{2}\overline{1}$, $312$, $3\overline{1}2${}}}\}

describe trace evaluations at the entire Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
,
as in Problem
10.4
.
One might call the above
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
-elements

codominant

in analogy to codominant
permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
although the author is not aware of a definition of
dominant elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
in the literature.
On the other hand,
it would be interesting to relate
codominant elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}

to the subsets of
vexillary,
theta-vexillary,
Grassmanian,
leading, and
amenable
elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, which
appear in

[
6
]
,

[
7
]
,

[
11
]
,

[
44
]
,

[
66
]
.

11.
Symmetric functions

For
W=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
or
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, and
H=H(W)
its Hecke algebra,
bases of
\mathcal{T}(W)
and
\mathcal{T}(H(W))

are often studied in conjunction
with bases of an appropriate
module
\Lambda

of symmetric functions.
When
W=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, the symmetric function bases consist of
traces of the corresponding Lie group; when
W=\mathfrak{B}_{n}
they do not.
In either case, we have the equalities

\mathrm{rank}(\mathcal{T}(W))=\mathrm{rank}(\mathcal{T}(H(W)))=\mathrm{rank}(\Lambda),

which makes
\Lambda
a convenient setting
in which to define generating functions for trace evaluations.

11.1.
Type-
\mathsf{A}
symmetric functions

Corresponding to the six commonly used bases
of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))
and
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{S}_{n})

(§
4.1
)
are six bases of the
\mathbb{Z}
-module

\Lambda_{n}(x)=\Lambda_{n}(x_{1},x_{2},\dotsc),

of homogeneous degree-
n
symmetric functions:
the Schur basis
\{s_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
,
elementary basis
\{e_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
,
(complete) homogeneous basis
\{h_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
,
power sum basis
\{p_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
,
monomial basis
\{m_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
,
and forgotten basis
\{f_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
.
(See
[
61
, Ch. 6]
.)
The correspondence of trace bases and symmetric function bases
is given explicitly by (the
q
-extension of) the Frobenius map

(11.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}_{q}:\mathcal{T}(H_{n}(q))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Lambda_{n}(x)

(11.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}_{q}(\theta_{q})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\theta(w)p_{\mathrm{ctype}(w)}

(11.3)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\vdash n}\frac{1}{z_{\mu}}\theta(\mu)p_{\mu},

where
\theta=\theta_{1}
as in Section
4
,
and
\theta(\mu):=\theta(w)
for any
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
of cycle type
\mu
.
Specifically, we have

(11.4)

\begin{gathered}\mathrm{ch}_{q}(\chi_{q}^{\lambda})=s_{\lambda},\qquad\mathrm{ch}_{q}(\epsilon_{q}^{\lambda})=e_{\lambda},\qquad\mathrm{ch}_{q}(\eta_{q}^{\lambda})=h_{\lambda},\\
\qquad\mathrm{ch}_{q}(\psi_{q}^{\lambda})=p_{\lambda},\qquad\mathrm{ch}_{q}(\phi_{q}^{\lambda})=m_{\lambda},\qquad\mathrm{ch}_{q}(\gamma_{q}^{\lambda})=f_{\lambda}.\end{gathered}

We construct generating functions for
H_{n}(q)
-trace evaluations as follows.
Given element
D\in\mathbb{Q}(q)\otimes H_{n}(q)
, define
the generating function

Y_{q}(D):=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\epsilon_{q}^{\lambda}(D)m_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Q}(q)\otimes\Lambda_{n}(x)

for the evaluation of induced sign characters at
D
.
By
[
58
, Prop. 2.1]
, this symmetric function is in fact a generating
function for the evaluation of
all
the standard traces at
D
, because it
is equal to

\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\eta_{q}^{\lambda}(D)f_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\frac{(-1)^{n-\ell(\lambda)}\psi_{q}^{\lambda}(D)}{z_{\lambda}}p_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\chi_{q}^{\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}}(D)s_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\phi_{q}^{\lambda}(D)e_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\gamma_{q}^{\lambda}(D)h_{\lambda}.

Equivalently, if we let

\omega:\Lambda_{n}(x)\rightarrow\Lambda_{n}(x)

be the standard involution mapping

(11.5)

s_{\lambda}\mapsto s_{\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}},\qquad e_{\lambda}\mapsto h_{\lambda},\qquad p_{\lambda}\mapsto(-1)^{n-\ell(\lambda)}p_{\lambda},\qquad m_{\lambda}\mapsto f_{\lambda},

then we have that

\omega Y_{q}(D)
is equal to

\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\epsilon_{q}^{\lambda}(D)f_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\eta_{q}^{\lambda}(D)m_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\frac{\psi_{q}^{\lambda}(D)}{z_{\lambda}}p_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\chi_{q}^{\lambda}(D)s_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\phi_{q}^{\lambda}(D)h_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}\negthinspace\gamma_{q}^{\lambda}(D)e_{\lambda}.\negthickspace\negthickspace

It is not difficult to show that
every symmetric function in
\mathbb{Q}(q)\otimes\Lambda_{n}(x)

is
Y_{q}(D)
for some
D\in\mathbb{Q}(q)\otimes H_{n}(q)
.
(See
[
58
, Prop. 3]
.)

Theorem
9.2
(
i
-
b
)
shows that
when

w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoids the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
the symmetric function
Y_{q}(\widetilde{C}_{w}(q))

is related to
colorings of
\mathrm{inc}(P(w))
.
More generally,
Stanley
[
60
]
defined
the
chromatic symmetric function
of any simple graph
G
to be

(11.6)

X_{G}:=\sum_{\kappa}x_{1}^{|\kappa^{-1}(1)|}x_{2}^{|\kappa^{-1}(2)|}\cdots,

where the sum is over all proper colorings

\kappa:V\rightarrow\{1,2,\dotsc,\}
of
G
(§
8.1
).
Expanding in the monomial basis of
\Lambda_{n}
, we have

X_{G}=\sum_{\lambda}c_{G,\lambda}m_{\lambda},

where
c_{G,\lambda}
is the number of
proper colorings of
G
of type
\lambda
.

Shareshian and Wachs
[
55
]
defined a quasisymmetric extension

X_{G,q}
of the
symmetric function
X_{G}
.
Given a proper coloring
\kappa

of
G
,
define
\textsc{inv}_{G}(\kappa)
to be the number of pairs
(i,j)\in E
with

i<j
and
\kappa(i)>\kappa(j)
.
For any composition
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{\ell})\vDash n
, define

c_{G,\alpha}(q)=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa\text{ proper}\\
\mathrm{type}(\kappa)=\alpha\end{subarray}}\negthickspace\negthickspace q^{\textsc{inv}_{G}(\kappa)},

and let

M_{\alpha}=\negthickspace\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}}\negthickspace x_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}^{\alpha_{\ell}}

be the
monomial quasisymmetric function
indexed by
\alpha
.
Then we have the definition

(11.7)

X_{G,q}=\sum_{\kappa}q^{\textsc{inv}_{G}(\kappa)}x_{1}^{|\kappa^{-1}(1)|}x_{2}^{|\kappa^{-1}(2)|}\cdots=\sum_{\alpha\vDash n}c_{G,\alpha}(q)M_{\alpha},

where the first sum is over
proper colorings of
G
.
It is easy to see that the
q=1
specialization of
X_{G,q}
satisfies

X_{G,1}=X_{G}
.
When
G=\mathrm{inc}(P)
for a unit interval order
P

labeled as in Algorithm
7.3
,
the quasisymmetric function
X_{\mathrm{inc}(P),q}

is in fact symmetric
[
56
, Thm. 4.5]
.
Furthermore, we have the following
[
17
, Thm. 7.4]
.

Theorem 11.1
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,
and
P=P(w)
defined as in Subsection
7.1
,
we have

Y_{q}(\widetilde{C}_{w}(q))=X_{\mathrm{inc}(P),q}
.

11.2.
Type-
\mathsf{BC}
symmetric functions

Corresponding to the eleven commonly used bases
of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
and
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{B}_{n})

(§
4.2
)
are eleven natural bases of the
\mathbb{Z}
-module
of
type-
\mathsf{BC}
symmetric functions
of degree
n
,

\Lambda_{n}(x,y):=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{n}\Lambda_{k}(x)\otimes\Lambda_{n-k}(y),

where
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\dotsc)
and
y=(y_{1},y_{2},\dotsc)
.
These bases of
\Lambda_{n}(x,y)
consist
of ten
nonplethystic bases

of the form

(og)_{\lambda,\mu}:=o_{\lambda}(x)g_{\mu}(y)
,

(11.8)

\begin{gathered}\{(ss)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(ee)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(hh)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\\
\{(eh)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(he)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\\
\{(mm)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(f\negthinspace f)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(mf)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\\
\{(fm)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(pp)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\\
\end{gathered}

and the
plethystic power sum basis

(11.9)

\{p^{+}_{\lambda}p^{-}_{\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},

defined in terms of
ordinary power sum symmetric functions

p_{k}(x):=x_{1}^{k}+x_{2}^{k}+\cdots
by

(11.10)

\begin{gathered}p_{\lambda}^{+}:=(p_{\lambda_{1}}(x)+p_{\lambda_{1}}(y))\cdots(p_{\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}}(x)+p_{\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}}(y)),\\
p_{\mu}^{-}:=(p_{\mu_{1}}(x)-p_{\mu_{1}}(y))\cdots(p_{\mu_{\ell(\mu)}}(x)-p_{\mu_{\ell(\mu)}}(y)).\end{gathered}

The functions (
11.10
)
often appear in the literature as

p_{\lambda}[X+Y],p_{\mu}[X-Y]
, respectively.
A correspondence between bases of
\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
and the bases
(
11.8
) – (
11.9
) of

\Lambda_{n}(x,y)
is given
explicitly by the (
q
-extension of the)
plethystic
\mathsf{BC}
-Frobenius map
[
48
, §1, App. B]
.
(See also
[
5
, Eq. (2.5)]
.)

(11.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{pch}_{q}\negthickspace:\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Lambda_{n}(x,y)

(11.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{pch}_{q}(\theta_{q})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\negthinspace\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}\negthickspace\theta(w)p^{+}_{\alpha(w)}p^{-}_{\beta(w)}

(11.13)

\displaystyle=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}\negthinspace\frac{\theta(\lambda,\mu)}{z_{\lambda}z_{\mu}2^{\ell(\lambda)+\ell(\mu)}}p^{+}_{\lambda}p^{-}_{\mu},

where
\alpha(w)
,
\beta(w)
are the partitions satisfying

\mathrm{sct}(w)=(\alpha(w),\beta(w))
, and where we define

\theta(\lambda,\mu):=\theta(w)
for any
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}

having
\mathrm{sct}(w)=(\lambda,\mu)
.
Specifically,
\mathrm{pch}_{q}
maps

(11.14)

\begin{gathered}\quad(\eta\eta)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto(hh)_{\lambda,\mu},\quad(\eta\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto(he)_{\lambda,\mu},\quad(\epsilon\eta)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto(eh)_{\lambda,\mu},\quad(\epsilon\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto(ee)_{\lambda,\mu},\\
(\phi\phi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto(mm)_{\lambda,\mu},\quad(\phi\gamma)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto(mf)_{\lambda,\mu},\quad(\gamma\phi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto(fm)_{\lambda,\mu},\quad(\gamma\gamma)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto(f\negthinspace f)_{\lambda,\mu},\negthickspace\negthickspace\\
(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto(ss)_{\lambda,\mu},\quad(\psi\psi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto(pp)_{\lambda,\mu},\quad\iota^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto p^{+}_{\lambda}p^{-}_{\mu}.\end{gathered}

We extend the involutive homomorphism
\omega
on
\Lambda_{n}(x)

(
11.5
)
to an involutive homomorphsim on
\Lambda_{n}(x,y)
in the simplest way:

\omega(o_{\lambda}(x)g_{\mu}(y)):=\omega(o_{\lambda}(x))\omega(g_{\mu}(y))
.
This exchanges the symmetric functions on line
1
of
(
11.14
) with the corresponding functions on line
2
,
transposes the index shapes of
(ss)_{\lambda,\mu}
and multiplies
each power sum basis element by
(-1)^{\ell(\lambda)+\ell(\mu)}
.
Transition matrices relating the ten nonplethystic bases
have entries which are simply products of entries of transition
matrices relating type-
\mathsf{A}
symmetric functions, e.g.,

(11.15)

(ss)_{\lambda,\mu}=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}\negthickspace\negthickspace K_{\lambda,\alpha}K_{\mu,\beta}(mm)_{\alpha,\beta}.

The plethystic power sum basis can be related to the others via the
nonplethystic Schur basis,

(11.16)

p_{\lambda}^{+}p_{\mu}^{-}=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}\negthickspace\negthickspace(\chi\chi)^{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda,\mu)(ss)_{\alpha,\beta}.

We construct generating functions for
H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
-trace evaluations as follows.
Given any element
D\in H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
, define the generating function

(11.17)

Y_{q}^{\mathsf{BC}}(D)=\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}(\epsilon\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(mm)_{\lambda,\mu}\in\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]\otimes\Lambda_{n}(x,y).

This symmetric function is in fact a generating
function for the evaluation of
all
the standard traces at
D
,
in the following sense.

Proposition 11.2
.

For
D\in H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
we have

\displaystyle Y_{q}^{\mathsf{BC}}(D)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\epsilon\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(mm)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\epsilon\eta)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(mf)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\eta\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(fm)_{\lambda,\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\eta\eta)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(f\negthinspace f)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\chi\chi)^{\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace},\mu^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}}_{q}(D)(ss)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\phi\phi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(ee)_{\lambda,\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\phi\gamma)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(eh)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\gamma\phi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(he)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\gamma\gamma)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(hh)_{\lambda,\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}\frac{(-1)^{\ell(\lambda)+\ell(\mu)}(\psi\psi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)}{z_{\lambda}z_{\mu}}(pp)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(-1)^{\ell(\lambda)+\ell(\mu)}\iota^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)p^{+}_{\lambda}p^{-}_{\mu}.

Equivalently,
\omega Y_{q}^{\mathsf{BC}}(D)

is equal to

(11.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\epsilon\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(f\negthinspace f)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\epsilon\eta)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(fm)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\eta\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(mf)_{\lambda,\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\eta\eta)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(mm)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(ss)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\phi\phi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(hh)_{\lambda,\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\phi\gamma)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(he)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\gamma\phi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(eh)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\gamma\gamma)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(ee)_{\lambda,\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}\frac{(\psi\psi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)}{z_{\lambda}z_{\mu}}(pp)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}\iota^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)p^{+}_{\lambda}p^{-}_{\mu}.

Proof.

Consider the fourth and fifth sums in (
11.18
),
in which the symmetric functions and traces satisfy

(11.19)

(ss)_{\lambda,\mu}=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}\negthickspace\negthickspace K_{\lambda,\alpha}K_{\mu,\beta}(mm)_{\alpha,\beta},\qquad(\eta\eta)^{\alpha,\beta}_{q}=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}\negthickspace\negthickspace K_{\lambda,\alpha}K_{\mu,\beta}(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}.

Using (
11.19
) to expand the fifth sum in the
monomial symmetric function basis, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}\negthickspace\negthickspace(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}\negthickspace\negthickspace K_{\lambda,\alpha}K_{\mu,\beta}(mm)_{\alpha,\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}K_{\lambda,\alpha}K_{\mu,\beta}(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)(mm)_{\alpha,\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}\negthickspace(\eta\eta)^{\alpha,\beta}_{q}(D)(mm)_{\alpha,\beta},

i.e., it is equal to the fourth sum.
Similarly, for each of the remaining sums of the form

\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\zeta\xi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(D)o_{\alpha}(x)g_{\beta}(y)

in (
11.18
),
there is a matrix

(M_{(\lambda,\mu),(\alpha,\beta)})_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n,(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}

and equations

(ss)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)}M_{(\lambda,\mu),(\alpha,\beta)}o_{\alpha}(x)g_{\beta}(y),\qquad(\zeta\xi)^{\alpha,\beta}_{q}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)}M_{(\lambda,\mu),(\alpha,\beta)}(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q},

relating it to the fifth sum.
In particular,
we have

M_{(\lambda,\mu),(\alpha,\beta)}=K_{\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace},\alpha}K_{\mu^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace},\beta}
,

K_{\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace},\alpha}K_{\mu,\beta}
,

K_{\lambda,\alpha}K_{\mu^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace},\beta}
,

K_{\negthinspace\alpha,\lambda}^{-1}K_{\negthinspace\beta,\mu}^{-1}
,

K_{\negthinspace\alpha,\lambda}^{-1}K_{\negthinspace\beta,\mu^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}}^{-1}
,

K_{\negthinspace\alpha,\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}}^{-1}K_{\negthinspace\beta,\mu}^{-1}
,

K_{\negthinspace\alpha,\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}}^{-1}K_{\negthinspace\beta,\mu^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace}}^{-1}
,

\chi^{\lambda}(\alpha)\chi^{\mu}(\beta)
,
respectively.
(See
[
9
, §3]
.)
Relating the last sum to the fifth sum, we have equations

(ss)_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)}\frac{(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}(\alpha,\beta)}{z_{\alpha}z_{\beta}2^{\ell(\alpha)+\ell(\beta)}}p^{+}_{\alpha}p^{-}_{\beta},\qquad\iota^{\alpha,\beta}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)}\frac{(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}(\alpha,\beta)}{z_{\alpha}z_{\beta}2^{\ell(\alpha)+\ell(\beta)}}(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}.

∎

To say that the functions
\{Y_{q}(D)\,|\,D\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]\}
arise often
in the study of type-
\mathsf{BC}
symmetric functions would be an
understatement;
essentially
every
element of
\mathbb{Z}[q]\otimes\Lambda_{n}(x,y)

has this form.

Proposition 11.3
.

Every symmetric function in
\mathbb{Z}[q]\otimes\Lambda_{n}(x,y)

has the form
Y_{q}(D)
for some element

D\in\mathbb{Q}(q)[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]
.

Proof.

Fix a symmetric function in
\Lambda_{n}(x,y)

and expand it in the plethystic power sum basis as

\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}a_{\lambda,\mu}(q)p_{\lambda}^{+}p_{\mu}^{-}
.
Then choose one representative
w_{\kappa,\nu}

of each conjugacy class of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.
We have

\iota^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}(T_{w_{\kappa,\nu}})=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}(\chi\chi)^{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda,\mu)(\chi\chi)_{q}^{\alpha,\beta}(T_{w_{\kappa,\nu}}).

Since the matrices

((\chi\chi^{\alpha,\beta}(\lambda,\mu))_{(\lambda,\mu),(\alpha,\beta)}

and

((\chi\chi_{q}^{\alpha,\beta}(T_{w_{\kappa,\nu}}))_{(\alpha,\beta),(\kappa,\nu)}

are both invertible, so is their product

((\iota_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}(T_{w_{\kappa,\nu}}))_{(\lambda,\mu),(\kappa,\nu)}
.
Call the inverse of this product
B=(b_{(\alpha,\beta),(\lambda,\mu)}(q))

and for each
(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n
define

U_{\alpha,\beta}=\sum_{(\kappa,\nu)\vdash n}b_{(\alpha,\beta),(\kappa,\nu)}(q)T_{w_{\kappa,\nu}}\in\mathbb{Q}(q)\otimes H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q).

Then
we have

\iota_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}(U_{\alpha,\beta})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $(\lambda,\mu)=(\alpha,\beta)$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Now define the
\mathbb{Q}(q)[\mathfrak{B}_{n}]

element

D=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}a_{\alpha,\beta}(q)U_{w_{\alpha,\beta}}.

By (
4.4
) and (
11.18
) we have

Y_{q}(D)=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}\iota_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}\Big(\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\vdash n}a_{\alpha,\beta}(q)U_{\alpha,\beta}\Big)p_{\lambda}^{+}p_{\mu}^{-}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}a_{\lambda,\mu}(q)p_{\lambda}^{+}p_{\mu}^{-},

as desired.
∎

For any type-
\mathsf{BC}

incomparability graph
\Gamma=(V,E)
,
we define the

type-
\mathsf{BC}
chromatic symmetric function of
\Gamma
to be

(11.20)

X^{\mathsf{BC}}_{G}:=\sum_{\kappa}(x_{1}^{|\kappa^{-1}(1)|}x_{2}^{|\kappa^{-1}(2)|}\cdots)(y_{1}^{|\kappa^{-1}(-1)|}y_{2}^{|\kappa^{-1}(-2)|}\cdots),

where the sum is over all proper
\mathsf{BC}
-colorings

\kappa:V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\smallsetminus\{0\}
of
\Gamma

(§
8.2
).

For the incomparability graph
\mathrm{inc}(Q)
of a
\mathsf{BC}
-poset
Q
,
we may express the symmetric function

X^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{inc}(Q)}
in terms of decompositions of
P
into chains.
Letting
c_{Q,\lambda,\mu}
be the number of column-strict marked
Q
-bitableaux
of shape
(\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace},\mu^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace})
, we have

X^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{inc}(Q)}=\sum_{\lambda}c_{Q,\lambda,\mu}(mm)_{\lambda,\mu}.

Theorem 11.4
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
and
Q=Q(w)
defined as in
Subsection
7.2
, we have

Y^{\mathsf{BC}}(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))=X^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{inc}(Q)}
.

Proof.

The coefficient of
(mm)_{\lambda,\mu}
in
X^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\mathrm{inc}(Q)}

equals the number
of proper
\mathsf{BC}
-colorings of
\mathrm{inc}(Q)
of type
(\lambda,\mu)
.
This is exactly the number of marked column-strict

\mathrm{inc}(Q)
-bitableaux of shape
(\lambda^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace},\mu^{\negthickspace\top\negthickspace})
.
By Theorem
9.6
,
this is
(\epsilon\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(1))
,
and by (
11.17
),
we have the desired equality.
∎

It would be interesting to state and prove
a
q
-analog of Theorem
11.4
.

Problem 11.5
.

Define a statistic
\mathrm{stat}
on proper
\mathsf{BC}
-colorings
\kappa
of
type-
\mathsf{BC}
interval graphs so that
for
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the patterns
3412
and
4231
,

Q=Q(w)
and

\Gamma=\mathrm{inc}(Q)
,
we have a
q
-chromatic symmetric function of the form

X^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\Gamma,q}=\sum_{\kappa}q^{\mathrm{stat}(\kappa)}(x_{1}^{|\kappa^{-1}(1)|}x_{2}^{|\kappa^{-1}(2)|}\cdots)(y_{1}^{|\kappa^{-1}(-1)|}y_{2}^{|\kappa^{-1}(-2)|}\cdots)

which satisfies the identity

Y_{q}^{\mathsf{BC}}(\widetilde{C}_{w}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))=X^{\mathsf{BC}}_{\Gamma,q}
.

11.3.
Another approach to type-
\mathsf{BC}
symmetric functions

From the plethystic power sum symmetric functions

\{p_{\lambda}^{+}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}\cup\{p_{\lambda}^{-}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}\subseteq\Lambda_{k}(x,y)
,
one can define other plethystic
symmetric functions
[
9
, §3]
,
[
64
]

\begin{gathered}\{s_{\lambda}^{+}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\},\quad\{h_{\lambda}^{+}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\},\quad\{e_{\lambda}^{+}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\},\quad\{m_{\lambda}^{+}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\},\quad\{f_{\lambda}^{+}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\},\\
\quad\{s_{\lambda}^{-}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\},\quad\{h_{\lambda}^{-}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\},\quad\{e_{\lambda}^{-}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\},\quad\{m_{\lambda}^{-}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\},\quad\{f_{\lambda}^{-}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}\end{gathered}

to be those symmetric functions in
\Lambda_{k}(x,y)
related to

\{p_{\lambda}^{+}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}

or

\{p_{\lambda}^{-}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}

just as

\{s_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}
,

\{h_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}
,

\{e_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}
,

\{m_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}
,

\{f_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}

in
\Lambda_{k}(x)
are related to

\{p_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash k\}
.
Such functions often appear in the literature as

s_{\lambda}[X+Y],\dotsc,f_{\lambda}[X+Y]

and
s_{\lambda}[X-Y],\dotsc,f_{\lambda}[X-Y]
.
Certain products of pairs of these
form nine more
plethystic
bases of the space
\Lambda_{n}(x,y)
:

(11.21)

\begin{gathered}\{s_{\lambda}^{+}s_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\}\\
\{h_{\lambda}^{+}h_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{h_{\lambda}^{+}e_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{e_{\lambda}^{+}h_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{e_{\lambda}^{+}e_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\\
\{m_{\lambda}^{+}m_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{m_{\lambda}^{+}f_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{f_{\lambda}^{+}m_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{f_{\lambda}^{+}f_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\}.\end{gathered}

Naturally, one may study
\Lambda_{n}(x,y)
in terms of these bases
and

\{p_{\lambda}^{+}p_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},\quad\{(pp)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\},

instead of using the eleven bases in (
11.8
) – (
11.9
).
It is straightforward to show
that matrices relating bases of the forms

\{o_{\lambda}^{+}g_{\mu}^{-}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\}

to

\{(pp)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\}

are the same as those relating bases of the forms

\{(og)_{\lambda,\mu}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\}

to

\{p_{\lambda}^{+}p_{\mu}^{-}/2^{\ell(\lambda)+\ell(\mu)}\,|\,(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n\}
.
Formulas for the matrix entries are given in
[
9
, App. A]
.

A correspondence between the plethystic bases and those of the trace space

\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))
is given explicitly by the (
q
-extension of the)

nonplethystic

\mathsf{BC}
-Frobenius map

[
9
, §3]
,
[
64
]

(11.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{nch}_{q}\negthickspace:\mathcal{T}(H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Lambda_{n}(x,y)

(11.23)

\displaystyle\mathrm{nch}_{q}(\theta_{q})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\negthinspace\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}\negthickspace 2^{\ell(\alpha(w))+\ell(\beta(w))}\theta(w)(pp)_{\alpha(w),\beta(w)}

(11.24)

\displaystyle=\negthickspace\negthickspace\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}\negthinspace\frac{\theta(\lambda,\mu)}{z_{\lambda}z_{\mu}}(pp)_{\lambda,\mu},

analogous to (
11.11
) – (
11.13
).
Specifically,
\mathrm{nch}_{q}
maps

\begin{gathered}\quad(\epsilon\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto e_{\lambda}^{+}e_{\mu}^{-},\quad(\epsilon\eta)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto e_{\lambda}^{+}h_{\mu}^{-},\quad(\eta\epsilon)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto h_{\lambda}^{+}e_{\mu}^{-},\quad(\eta\eta)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto h_{\lambda}^{+}h_{\mu}^{-},\\
(\phi\phi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto m_{\lambda}^{+}m_{\mu}^{-},\quad(\phi\gamma)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto m_{\lambda}^{+}f_{\mu}^{-},\quad(\gamma\phi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto f_{\lambda}^{+}m_{\mu}^{-},\quad(\gamma\gamma)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto f_{\lambda}^{+}f_{\mu}^{-},\\
(\chi\chi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto s_{\lambda}^{+}s_{\mu}^{-},\quad(\psi\psi)^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto p_{\lambda}^{+}p_{\mu}^{-},\quad\iota^{\lambda,\mu}_{q}\mapsto 2^{\ell(\lambda)+\ell(\mu)}(pp)_{\lambda,\mu}.\end{gathered}

Defining a trace generating function in
\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]\otimes\Lambda_{n}(x,y)

in terms of characters
(\epsilon\epsilon)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}
, plethystic monomial
symmetric functions
m_{\lambda}^{+}m_{\mu}^{-}
, and elements
D\in H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)
, we have
expansions analogous to those in Proposition
11.2
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\epsilon\epsilon)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}(D)m_{\lambda}^{+}m_{\mu}^{-}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\epsilon\eta)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}(D)m_{\lambda}^{+}f_{\mu}^{-}=\cdots=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(\gamma\gamma)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}(D)h_{\lambda}^{+}h_{\mu}^{-}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}\frac{(-1)^{\ell(\lambda)+\ell(\mu)}(\psi\psi)_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}(D)}{z_{\lambda}z_{\mu}}p^{+}_{\lambda}p^{-}_{\mu}=\sum_{(\lambda,\mu)\vdash n}(-1)^{\ell(\lambda)+\ell(\mu)}\iota_{q}^{\lambda,\mu}(D)(pp)_{\lambda,\mu}.

While one could define
Y^{\mathsf{BC}}_{q}(D)
to be the above symmetric function
instead of
that in (
11.17
), this would lead to a less natural
connection to the

\mathsf{BC}
-chromatic symmetric
function (
11.20
).

12.
Hessenberg varieties

Hessenberg varieties are subvarieties of flag varieties which were first
studied
[
21
]
,
[
22
]

in conjunction with questions
concerning eigenvalues of linear operators.
More recent work reveals connections to other varieties,
representation theory, and combinatorics.

The
Hessenberg varieties of Coxeter types
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{B}
,
C

are parametrized by
certain vector spaces called Hessenberg spaces. Specifically,
given a reducive algebraic group

\mathrm{G}
, Borel subgroup
\mathrm{B}
,
and Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
,
\mathfrak{b}
of these,
call a subspace

\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
a
Hessenberg space

if it satisfies
the Lie algebra containment conditions

(12.1)

\mathfrak{b}\subseteq\mathcal{H},\qquad\big[\mathfrak{b},\mathcal{H}\big]\subseteq\mathcal{H}.

Hessenberg spaces may be parametrized by appropriate sets of
roots in root systems.
(See
[
36
]

for definitions.)
Let
\Phi
be the root system of type
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{B}
or
\mathsf{C}
,
let
\Delta\subset\Phi
be a simple system, let
\Phi^{-}\subseteq\Phi

be the set of negative roots,
and for
\gamma\in\Phi
let
\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}

be the root space of
\mathfrak{g}
corresponding to
\gamma
.
Define the root poset on
\Phi
by

\alpha\leq_{\Phi}\beta
if
\beta-\alpha\in\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{N}}\Delta
,
and define the negative root poset to be the subposet of this
induced by
\Phi^{-}
.
Call subset
I\subseteq\Phi^{-}
a
dual order ideal
of
\Phi^{-}
if
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi^{-}
we have

\begin{matrix}\alpha\in I,\\
\alpha\leq_{\Phi^{-}}\beta\end{matrix}\bigg\}\Rightarrow\beta\in I.

In particular, we have the following
[
21
, Lem. 1]
.

Proposition 12.1
.

A bijection between Hessenberg spaces
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}

and dual order ideals
I
of the
negative root poset on
\Phi^{-}

is given by

(12.2)

I\mapsto\mathcal{H}(I):=\mathfrak{b}\oplus\bigoplus_{\gamma\in I}\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma}.

For Hessenberg space
\mathcal{H}
and matrix
S\in\mathfrak{g}
,
define a subvariety of the flag variety
\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{B}

by

(12.3)

\mathrm{Hess}(\mathcal{H})=\mathrm{Hess}(\mathcal{H},S):=\{g\mathrm{B}\in\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{B}\,|\,g^{-1}\negthinspace Sg\in\mathcal{H}\},

and
call this the

Hessenberg variety associated to
\mathcal{H}
.
If
S

is a regular semisimple element of
\mathfrak{g}
,
then
call
\mathrm{Hess}(\mathcal{H})
a
regular semisimple Hessenberg variety
.
In this case, its
cohomology
vanishes in odd degree
[
21
, §3]
,

(12.4)

\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathcal{\mathrm{Hess}}(\mathcal{H}))=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}\mathrm{H}^{2j}(\mathrm{Hess}(\mathcal{H})).

(See also
[
50
]
,
[
68
]
.)
Each
graded component of (
12.4
)
has the structure of a
W
-module,
where
W
is the Weyl group of
G

[
69
]
,
[
70
]
.
Let

(12.5)

\mathrm{ch}(\mathrm{H}^{2j}(\mathrm{Hess}(\mathcal{H})))

be the Frobenius characteristic of the character
of the module
\mathrm{H}^{2j}(\mathrm{Hess}(\mathcal{H}))
.
For regular semisimple Hessenberg varieties of type
\mathsf{A}
,
the grading (
12.4
) is
closely related to trace evaluations and graph coloring.
In types
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{C}
,
no such relation
is known.

12.1.
Regular semisimple Hessenberg varieties of type
\mathsf{A}

Define type-
\mathsf{A}
Hessenberg spaces
as in (
12.1
)
with

\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
,

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
In addition to the bijection (
12.2
) with dual order ideals,
we have the following bijection with codominant
elements of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.

Proposition 12.2
.

A bijective correspondence between

312
-avoiding permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
Hessenberg spaces in

\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})

is given by

(12.6)

w\mapsto\mathcal{H}(w):=\{A=(a_{i,j})\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\,|\,a_{i,j}=0\text{ for all }j>\max(w_{1},\dotsc,w_{i})\}.

Proof.

Given
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the pattern
312
,
define

m_{i}=\max(w_{1},\dotsc,w_{i})
for
i=1,\dotsc,n
.
By Theorem
7.1

the map
w\mapsto m_{1}\cdots m_{n}
is bijective,
and
it is easy to see that
m_{1}\cdots m_{n}

satisfies the defining conditions of
a
Hessenberg function
:

(1)

i\leq m_{i}\leq n
for
i=1,\dotsc,n
,

(2)

m_{1}\leq\cdots\leq m_{n}
.

Thus the
\frac{1}{n+1}\tbinom{2n}{n}

spaces
\mathcal{H}(w)
in (
12.6
)
are precisely the Hessenberg spaces
usually denoted by
\mathcal{H}(m_{1}\cdots m_{n})
in the literature.
(See, e.g.,
[
1
, Eq.(̇2.2)]
.)
∎

Let
\mathrm{Hess^{\mathsf{A}}}(\mathcal{H})
denote the
type-
\mathsf{A}

regular semisimple Hessenberg variety,
defined as in (
12.3
) with
\mathcal{H}
as above
and
S=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n})
,
where
\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n}
are distinct.
For
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the pattern
312
, define the generating function

(12.7)

\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{H}(w)):=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell(w)}\mathrm{ch}(\mathrm{H}^{2j}(\mathrm{Hess^{\mathsf{A}}}(\mathcal{H}(w))))q^{j}

for the type-
\mathsf{A}
Frobenius characteristics
(
12.5
).
Shareshian and Wachs conjectured
that
\omega\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{H}(w))

is equal to a chromatic symmetric function,
viewed as a polynomial in
q
with coefficients
in
\Lambda_{n}

[
56
, Conj. 10.1]
.
This was proved by Brosnan–Chow
[
15
]

and Guay-Paquet
[
31
]
.
Combining the equality with Theorem
11.1

we specifically have the following.

Theorem 12.3
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the pattern
312

and unit interval order
P=P(w)
,
we have

(12.8)

\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{H}(w))=\omega X_{\mathrm{inc}(P),q}=\omega Y_{q}(\widetilde{C}_{w}(q)).

We can see in three ways that the function in (
12.8
)
belongs to
\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{N}[q]}\{s_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
:

(1)

The character of

\mathrm{H}^{2j}(\mathrm{Hess^{\mathsf{A}}}(\mathcal{H}(w)))

belongs to
\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{N}}\{\chi^{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
.
Thus its Frobenius characteristic (
11.4
)
belongs to

\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{N}}\{s_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
.

(2)

By
[
56
, Thm. 6.3]
the coefficient of

s_{\lambda}
in
X_{\mathrm{inc}(P),q}
belongs to
\mathbb{N}[q]
.

(3)

By
[
32
, Lem. 1.1]
we have

\chi_{q}^{\lambda}(\widetilde{C}_{w}(q))\in\mathbb{N}[q]
.

Furthermore, the function (
12.8
) is conjectured
to belong to
\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{N}[q]}\{h_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
.
This open problem also can be viewed in three ways.

Problem 12.4
.

Prove one of the following.

(1)

[
56
, Conj. 10.4]

For each
permutation
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
avoiding the pattern
312

and index
j=0,\dotsc,\ell(w)
,
the
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-module
\mathrm{H}^{2j}(\mathrm{Hess}^{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{H}(w)))

is a permutation module in which each point stabilizer is a Young subgroup.

(2)

[
56
, Conj. 5.1]

For each unit interval order
P
labeled as in
Algorithm
7.3
,
the function
X_{\mathrm{inc}(P),q}
belongs to

\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{N}[q]}\{e_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\vdash n\}
.
(See also
[
63
, Conj. 5.5]
.)

(3)

[
32
, Conj. 2.1]

For each permutation
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and each partition
\lambda\vdash n
,
we have
\phi_{q}^{\lambda}(\widetilde{C}_{w}(q))\in\mathbb{N}[q]
.
(See also
[
65
, Conj. 2.1]
.)

The statements above satisfy the implications

(1)\Leftrightarrow(2)\Leftarrow(3)
.
For progress on these problems, see, e.g.,
[
4
]
,

[
33
]
,
[
53
]
, and references
cited in
[
58
, §3.5]
.

12.2.
Regular semisimple Hessenberg varieties of types
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{C}

Define type-
\mathsf{B}
Hessenberg spaces
as in (
12.1
) with

\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{SO}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{C})
,

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{C})
;
define type-
\mathsf{B}
Hessenberg spaces
similarly
with

\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{SP}_{2n}(\mathbb{C})
,

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{C})
.
Let
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{B}}_{n}
,

\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{C}}_{n}

denote the sets of these spaces, respectively.
The cardinalities
of these sets are equal to
the number of order ideals appearing in (
12.2
).
By
[
16
, Thm. 3.1]
,
this is
\tbinom{2n}{n}
.
Thus neither collection of spaces
corresponds bijectively to the
\smash{\tfrac{1}{n+2}\tbinom{2n+2}{n+1}}

“codominant” elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
avoiding the signed patterns
1\overline{2}
,
\overline{2}1
,
\overline{2}\overline{1}
,
312
,
3\overline{1}2
.
This suggests the following problem.
(See also the type-
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{C}

Hessenberg functions defined in

[
2
, §10]
.)

Problem 12.5
.

State a type-
\mathsf{B}
or
\mathsf{C}
analog of
Proposition
12.2

in terms of a subset of
\tbinom{2n}{n}
elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

Let
\mathrm{Hess}^{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{H})
denote the
type-
\mathsf{B}

regular semisimple Hessenberg variety
defined as in (
12.3
)
with
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{B}}_{n}

and
S\in\mathfrak{so}_{2n+1}(\mathbb{C})
having distinct eigenvalues

(0,\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n},-\lambda_{1},\dotsc,-\lambda_{n})
.
Similarly, let
\mathrm{Hess}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathcal{H})
denote the
type-
\mathsf{C}

regular semisimple Hessenberg variety
defined as in (
12.3
)
with
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{C}}_{n}

and
S\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{C})
having distinct eigenvalues

(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n},-\lambda_{1},\dotsc,-\lambda_{n})
.

In analogy to (
12.7
) we
define the generating functions

(12.9)

\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{H}):=\sum_{j}\negthinspace\mathrm{ch}(\mathrm{H}^{2j}(\mathrm{Hess}^{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{H})))q^{j},\quad\ \mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\textsf{C}}(\mathcal{H}):=\sum_{j}\negthinspace\mathrm{ch}(\mathrm{H}^{2j}(\mathrm{Hess}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathcal{H})))q^{j}.

It would be interesting
to connect the symmetric functions

\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{H})
,
\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathcal{H})

to type-
\mathsf{BC}
chromatic symmetric functions and
type-
\mathsf{BC}
trace generating functions,
i.e., to formulate type-
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{C}
analogs
of Theorem
12.3
.
By Theorem
11.4

the
q=1
specializations of the
chromatic symmetric functions and trace generating functions
are equal;
general equality is stated as
Problem
11.5
.
One could also consider
interpreting

\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{H})
,

\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathcal{H})
as
trace generating functions.
By Proposition
11.3
,
such interpretations must exist.

Problem 12.6
.

Find families

\{D^{\mathsf{B}}_{\mathcal{H}}\,|\,\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{B}}_{n}\}
,

\{D^{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathcal{H}}\,|\,\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{C}}_{n}\}
of
elements of

H_{n}^{\mathsf{BC}}(q)

whose trace generating functions satisfy

(1)

\omega Y_{q}^{\mathsf{BC}}(D^{\mathsf{B}}_{\mathcal{H}})=\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{H})
for all

\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{B}}_{n}
,

(2)

\omega Y_{q}^{\mathsf{BC}}(D^{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathcal{H}})=\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathcal{H})
for all

\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{C}}_{n}
.

Similarly, one could try to interpret

\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{H})
,
\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\textsf{C}}(\mathcal{H})

in terms of graph coloring
[
54
]
.

Problem 12.7
.

Define families

\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{B}}=\{G^{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{H})\,|\,\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{B}}_{n}\}
,

\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{C}}=\{G^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathcal{H})\,|\,\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{C}}_{n}\}

of graphs and families

\{X_{G,q}^{\mathsf{B}}\,|\,G\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{B}}\}
,

\{X_{G,q}^{\mathsf{C}}\,|\,G\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{C}}\}

of chromatic symmetric functions
so that we have

(1)

\omega X^{\mathsf{B}}_{G^{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{H}),q}=\mathrm{Fr}_{q}^{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{H})
for all

\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{B}}_{n}
,
